BBA304F-5 · BBA Sem V · CHRIST University

Sustainable Finance — Complete End-Semester Study Guide

How to use: read each unit top-to-bottom (Intuition → Definition → Detail → Indian example → self-check), then drill the Unit exam kit; on exam morning read only the cheat sheet and the traps.

Built from your syllabus and course plan, the Unit 1, 2 and 3 slide decks, the Unit 5 presentation-topic pointers, the August 2026 mid-semester paper with its official scheme (solved in Past papers), the CIA 1 and CIA 3 briefs and the course reading on green finance. Every figure in the worked examples has been computed and cross-checked; figures labelled "illustrative" are teaching numbers, not official data.

Start here: how to use this guide

This guide assumes you know nothing about sustainable finance. Every topic in the syllabus is taught from zero, in the same order as the syllabus. Each topic follows the same rhythm, so you always know where you are:

  1. Intuition (yellow box) — the idea in everyday language.
  2. Definition (green box) — an exam-quotable definition. Learn these word-for-word; examiners give marks for precise definitions.
  3. Detail — features, types, process, advantages, limitations and comparison tables.
  4. Why it matters (blue box) — one or two lines you can use as an introduction or conclusion.
  5. Indian example (orange box) — real Indian regulators, schemes and companies. Examiners in India reward Indian examples.
  6. Formula + worked example — wherever the topic has any numbers (carbon footprint, green bond allocation, SLB coupon step-up, PAT certificates, financed emissions, bond price, duration...).
  7. Mini self-check — answer from memory, then open the answer.

After each unit there is a Unit exam kit with 5-mark, 10-mark and 15-mark case answers written the way a full-marks answer should look.

7-day revision plan

DayMorning (3 h)Evening (2 h)
1Unit 1: history, definition, ESG, TBL, stakeholder theory, SDGsUnit 1: agreements (UNFCCC, Kyoto, Paris), role of financial system; self-checks
2Unit 1: ESG risk, reporting (BRSR, GRI, SASB, TCFD, ISSB, CDP, IR), carbon accounting numericalsUnit 1: regulators, ratings, PRI, pillars; Unit 1 exam kit
3Unit 2: climate change, financial impact, Paris, COPsUnit 2: Indian regulations (NAPCC, EPA, NGT, PAT, CCTS, NGHM, FAME, BRSR, RBI, SEBI); scenario/stress/rating; exam kit
4Unit 3: strategies, green/social/SLB bonds, other labels, numericalsUnit 3: impact investing, microfinance, carbon trading, green loans, green microfinance, AI/ML; exam kit
5Unit 4: risk identification, quantification (PCAF, WACI), frameworks, metricsUnit 4: disclosure, greenwashing, AI, blockchain, trends; exam kit
6Unit 5: the 15 presentation topics (esp. SLB, green bond, digital/tokenized bonds, bond maths)Unit 5: five company cases + comparison; case kit
7Past papers (mid-sem, timed) + question bank: MCQs, short answers, 3 long answers written out by hand under timeDifferences table, traps, glossary, cheat sheet

1-day (night-before / exam-morning) plan

  1. (45 min) Read all Definition boxes only — use the filter box in the contents to jump.
  2. (45 min) Differences-between table — this is where most 5-mark questions come from.
  3. (60 min) One model answer per unit from each exam kit + the Unit 5 case kit (case study is compulsory, 15 marks).
  4. (30 min) Formula sheet: SLB step-up, green bond allocation, carbon footprint, PAT, PCAF, bond price, duration.
  5. (30 min) Past papers: reread the examiner-rewards box and the mid-sem case answer.
  6. (30 min) Traps + cheat sheet + exam-day checklist.

Concept map: how the five units connect

Exam pattern and how to write a full-marks answer

End-Semester Examination (ESE), 50 marks: Section A 5 × 5 = 25 marks; Section B 5 × 10 = 50 marks; Section C compulsory case study, 15 marks. The three sections add up to 90 marks as printed; the assessment table lists the ESE at 50 maximum marks contributing 30 marks to your final grade, so the paper is scaled. CIA: Assignment 1 (20), mid-semester exam (50, counted as 25), Assignment 2 (20), attendance (5).

The course plan does not print the ESE duration or whether questions carry internal choice. The August 2026 mid-semester paper (50 marks, 2 hours) used the same three-section design on a smaller scale: Section A 3 × 5 = 15, Section B 2 × 10 = 20, Section C compulsory case 15 (three 5-mark sub-parts), with every Section A and B question offered as an (a) OR (b) pair. Its official scheme is solved in Past papers.

Question typeLengthStructure that scores full marks
5 marks (Section A)1–1.5 pages, ~12 minutes at the mid-sem rateDefinition (1–2 marks in the mid-sem scheme) → 3–5 headed points (one mark per point) with one line of explanation each → one Indian example → one-line conclusion. A small diagram or table earns an easy mark.
10 marks (Section B)2.5–3 pages, ~24 minutes at the mid-sem rateIntro with definition and context → diagram → 7–9 headed points (or two sub-parts, e.g. features + limitations) → a comparison table if the question has "vs" → Indian example with specifics (regulator, scheme, company) → conclusion linking to SDGs/Paris/long-term value.
15 marks case (Section C)4 pages, ~36 minutes at the mid-sem rate1. Case summary (2–3 lines) 2. Issues identified 3. Concepts/frameworks applied (ESG pillars, TCFD, ICMA principles, stakeholder theory) 4. Analysis with any numbers from the case worked out 5. Options with pros/cons 6. Recommendation 7. Conclusion. Answer every sub-question separately with its own heading.

Timing: the mid-sem gave 120 minutes for 50 marks, i.e. 2.4 minutes per mark (12 / 24 / 36 minutes for a 5 / 10 / 15-mark answer). For the ESE, read the duration on the paper and keep the same rule: time in proportion to marks. What the evaluators reward, point by point, is in What the examiner rewards.

Name the framework every time: "As per ICMA's Green Bond Principles (four core components)...", "TCFD's four pillars...", "Under SEBI's BRSR...". Naming the correct framework is the single fastest way to look like a full-marks candidate.

Unit 1 — Introduction to Sustainable Finance

Syllabus: history; definition, scope and overview of ESG factors; Triple Bottom Line; UN SDGs financing; international agreements on climate change and sustainable development; role of the financial system; evolution (global and Indian); fundamentals of ESG risk management; financial and sustainability (impact) reporting and communication; regulatory frameworks, governing bodies and responsible investments; the pillars of sustainable finance. (8 teaching hours; CO1, CO2.)

1.1 History and evolution of sustainable finance (global)

For most of history, finance asked one question: "Will I get my money back with a good return?" Sustainable finance adds two more: "What does my money do to the planet and to people?" and "What will the planet and people do to my money?" (e.g. a flood destroying a factory you lent to). The history is the story of these two questions moving from the fringe (religious investors avoiding "sin" stocks) to the centre (central banks stress-testing banks for climate risk).

Your slides present the history as a sequence of landmark events. Learn this table in order — a timeline question ("Trace the evolution of sustainable finance") is very likely.

Slide 10–11 timeline (Unit 1 deck), expanded with what each event contributed.

YearEvent / authorWhat it contributed to sustainable finance
1713Hans Carl von Carlowitz (Sylvicultura oeconomica)A German forestry administrator who argued that only as much timber should be cut as the forest can regrow. First written statement of the sustainability principle ("sustained yield"): live off the income, not the capital, of nature.
1962Silent Spring (Rachel Carson)Showed harm from pesticides such as DDT. Birth of the modern environmental movement; established that business activity has externalities (costs borne by others).
1972UN Conference on the Human Environment (Stockholm)First major UN environmental conference; led to the creation of UNEP. Environment became an international policy issue.
1987Brundtland Report, Our Common FutureGave the classic definition of sustainable development: development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs.
1992Earth Summit, Rio de JaneiroProduced the UNFCCC, the Convention on Biological Diversity and Agenda 21. UNEP Finance Initiative (UNEP FI) also began in 1992, bringing banks into the conversation.
1997Kyoto ProtocolFirst legally binding emission targets (for developed countries) and the first carbon-market mechanisms (CDM, JI, emissions trading) — carbon got a price.
2004Who Cares Wins report (UN Global Compact with financial institutions)Coined and popularised the term ESG; argued that ESG integration leads to better markets and better outcomes.
2006Principles for Responsible Investment (PRI)Six voluntary principles for investors to incorporate ESG into investment and ownership. Mainstreamed responsible investing among large asset owners and managers.
2015UN Sustainable Development Goals (Agenda 2030)17 goals, 169 targets for 2030 — a common "impact language" for investors and companies.
2015Paris Agreement (COP21)Temperature goal (well below 2°C, pursue 1.5°C) and, in Article 2.1(c), the goal of making finance flows consistent with low-emission, climate-resilient development — the legal hook for sustainable finance.
2020 onwardsGrowth of ESG and green financeExplosion of green, social, sustainability and sustainability-linked bonds; mandatory disclosure (SEBI BRSR, EU rules, ISSB standards); climate stress tests by central banks.

Other milestones worth quoting (adds depth)

  • Religious and ethical investing (18th–20th century): Methodist and Quaker investors avoided "sin" industries (alcohol, tobacco, gambling, slave trade). This is the origin of negative screening.
  • 1971 – Pax World Fund (USA): one of the first modern socially responsible mutual funds, avoiding weapons makers during the Vietnam war.
  • 1970s–1980s – South Africa divestment campaign: universities and pension funds divested from companies doing business under apartheid — proof that investors could use capital as a lever for social change.
  • 1990 – Domini 400 Social Index (now MSCI KLD 400): first SRI stock index.
  • 1994 – John Elkington coins the "Triple Bottom Line".
  • 2003 – Equator Principles: banks' framework for managing environmental and social risk in project finance.
  • 2007–08 – First green bonds: European Investment Bank's Climate Awareness Bond (2007) and the World Bank's first green bond (2008).
  • 2014 – ICMA Green Bond Principles; 2015 – FSB creates the TCFD (recommendations published 2017); 2017 – NGFS (central banks' network) formed; 2020 – ICMA Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles; 2021 – ISSB announced at COP26; 2023 – ISSB issues IFRS S1 and S2.

The three "eras" of sustainable finance (a neat way to structure a 10-mark answer)

EraLabelQuestion askedTypical tool
Up to ~2000SRI (Socially Responsible Investing)"Is this investment morally acceptable?" (values-based)Exclusion / negative screening
~2004–2015ESG investing"Do E, S and G issues affect risk and return?" (value-based, financial materiality)ESG integration, ratings, engagement
2015 onwardsImpact / sustainable finance"Does my money create measurable positive change?" (outcome-based)Impact investing, green/SLB bonds, transition finance, SDG alignment

Knowing the evolution lets you explain why today's rules exist: every tool in Units 2–4 (carbon markets, BRSR, green bonds) is the answer to a problem identified at one of these milestones.

Q1. Which report defined sustainable development, and how?

The Brundtland Report, Our Common Future (1987): development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of future generations to meet their own needs.

Q2. Where did the term "ESG" come from?

The 2004 Who Cares Wins report, prepared under the UN Global Compact with financial institutions.

Q3. Put in order: PRI, Kyoto, Brundtland, Paris.

Brundtland (1987) → Kyoto (1997) → PRI (2006) → Paris (2015).

1.2 Evolution of sustainable finance in India

India's path is different from Europe's. Europe began with investors demanding ESG; India began with the state directing credit to social goals (priority sector lending, rural banking), then added mandatory CSR and mandatory ESG disclosure. So in India, regulation has been the main engine.

PeriodMilestoneSignificance
1969 & 1980Bank nationalisation; priority sector lending (PSL) targetsCredit directed to agriculture, small enterprises and weaker sections — an early form of "social finance". Renewable energy is now within PSL.
1982 / 1992NABARD set up (1982); SHG–Bank Linkage Programme (1992)Group-based lending to poor women; foundation of Indian microfinance.
1986Environment (Protection) ActUmbrella environmental law enacted after the Bhopal gas tragedy (1984).
2001Energy Conservation Act; Bureau of Energy Efficiency (BEE)Basis for PAT and later the carbon market.
2008National Action Plan on Climate Change (8 missions)First national climate strategy.
2010National Green Tribunal ActSpecialised environmental court.
2011–12MCA's National Voluntary Guidelines (2011); SEBI's Business Responsibility Report (BRR) for top 100 listed companies (2012)Beginning of ESG disclosure; BRR later extended to top 500 and top 1,000.
2012PAT scheme Cycle ITradable energy-saving certificates (ESCerts).
2013 (effective 2014)Companies Act, 2013 — Section 135 mandatory CSRIndia became the first country to mandate CSR spending (2% of average net profit for qualifying companies).
2015Yes Bank issues India's first green bond; India's first INDC; International Solar Alliance launched with FranceGreen debt market begins.
2017SEBI disclosure requirements for green debt securitiesIndia among the early regulators to define green bonds.
2019National Guidelines on Responsible Business Conduct (NGRBC) — 9 principlesThe nine principles on which the BRSR is built.
2021SEBI BRSR (mandatory for top 1,000 listed companies from FY 2022-23); RBI joins NGFS; India's Panchamrit and Net Zero 2070 announced at COP26ESG disclosure becomes mandatory and standardised.
2022Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act enabling a carbon market; updated NDC; RBI discussion paper on climate risk; SEBI framework for Social Stock ExchangeLegal base for carbon trading; banks' climate risk agenda.
2023First Sovereign Green Bonds (₹16,000 crore in two tranches); RBI framework for green deposits; SEBI BRSR Core with assurance, revised green debt framework, ESG fund norms and regulation of ESG rating providers; Carbon Credit Trading Scheme notified; National Green Hydrogen MissionIndia's sustainable finance architecture becomes comprehensive.
2024 onwardsRBI draft climate-risk disclosure framework and draft guidance on climate scenario analysis & stress testing; GHG-intensity targets for CCTS sectorsMoving from disclosure to risk management.

Dates in this table are safe to quote. Where you are unsure of an exact figure in the exam, write the concept without the figure — examiners penalise wrong numbers more than missing numbers.

Q1. Which document's nine principles form the basis of BRSR?

The National Guidelines on Responsible Business Conduct (NGRBC), 2019, issued by the Ministry of Corporate Affairs.

Q2. Name India's first green bond issuer and year.

Yes Bank, 2015.

1.3 Definition and scope of sustainable finance

Think of finance as the "plumbing" of the economy — it decides which projects get water (money) and which dry up. Sustainable finance re-routes the plumbing so that money flows towards projects that are good for the long run (solar parks, affordable housing, well-governed firms) and away from those that destroy value for society (polluting, exploitative or badly governed ones), while still earning a financial return.

Sustainable finance refers to financial activities, investments and banking decisions that consider not only profit but also environmental, social and governance (ESG) factors. It aims to support long-term economic growth while protecting the environment and society. (Course definition — Unit 1 slide 12.)

The European Commission defines sustainable finance as "the process of taking environmental, social and governance considerations into account when making investment decisions in the financial sector, leading to more long-term investments in sustainable economic activities and projects." The UNEP definition stresses aligning the financial system with sustainable development. Your course definition is equivalent; quote the course definition first and add "long-term" and "financial return plus positive impact" to enrich it.

Related terms — know the nesting

TermMeaningRelationship
Climate financeFinance for climate mitigation (reducing emissions) and adaptation (coping with climate impacts). Under UNFCCC it also means flows from developed to developing countries.Narrowest
Green financeFinance for all environmental objectives: climate plus pollution, water, biodiversity, circular economy.Includes climate finance
Sustainable financeGreen + social + governance considerations (full ESG).Broadest; includes green finance
Transition financeFinance for high-emitting firms (steel, cement) to decarbonise over time.Overlaps; fills the gap for "brown-to-green" activities

Scope of sustainable finance

  1. Who: banks (lending), NBFCs and MFIs, insurers (underwriting and investing), asset managers and mutual funds, pension funds, development finance institutions (World Bank, ADB, IFC, SIDBI, NABARD), regulators (RBI, SEBI, IRDAI), governments (sovereign green bonds), companies (issuers, reporters).
  2. What: equity (ESG funds, impact funds), debt (green/social/sustainability/SLB bonds, green loans, SLLs), insurance (parametric climate insurance), carbon markets, microfinance, blended finance.
  3. How: screening, integration, engagement, thematic and impact investing (3.1); risk management (4.1); disclosure (1.11).
  4. Why (two directions of materiality): outside-in — how ESG issues affect the firm's value (financial materiality); inside-out — how the firm affects people and planet (impact materiality). Together = double materiality.

Features

  • Long-term orientation (beyond quarterly profit).
  • Integrates non-financial information into financial decisions.
  • Considers externalities (pollution, social harm) that markets do not price.
  • Seeks risk-adjusted financial return plus positive or at least non-harmful impact.
  • Relies on disclosure, standards and third-party verification for credibility.

Achieving the SDGs and India's Net Zero 2070 requires enormous investment that governments cannot fund alone; sustainable finance mobilises private capital and prices risks that ordinary finance ignores.

Q1. Distinguish climate finance from green finance in one line.

Climate finance targets only mitigation and adaptation of climate change; green finance covers all environmental objectives (including pollution, water, biodiversity), so climate finance is a subset of green finance.

Q2. What is double materiality?

Considering both how sustainability issues affect the company's financial value (financial / outside-in) and how the company affects society and the environment (impact / inside-out).

1.4 Overview of ESG factors

ESG is a health check-up for a company that looks beyond the balance sheet. E asks how it treats the planet, S how it treats people, G how it is run and controlled. A company can be very profitable yet "unhealthy" on ESG — and that ill-health usually shows up later as fines, strikes, boycotts or scandals.

ESG stands for Environmental, Social and Governance factors — standards used to measure how responsibly an organisation operates and how it impacts society and the environment. ESG factors help investors, companies, governments and stakeholders evaluate sustainability and ethical practices in business. (Course definition — slide 14.)

The three pillars in detail (mirrors slide 34)

PillarCourse descriptionTypical metricsFinancial link
EnvironmentalA company's relationship with the planet — carbon footprint, water scarcity, climate-related operational disruptions.Scope 1/2/3 emissions (tCO2e), energy intensity, % renewable energy, water withdrawal (kL), waste recycled (%), spillsCarbon costs, clean-up liabilities, stranded assets, resource costs, access to green finance
SocialHow a business treats people — labour rights, workplace safety, community impact, data privacy.LTIFR (lost-time injury frequency rate), fatalities, employee turnover, % women in workforce, pay gap, complaints, CSR spendProductivity, strikes, licence to operate, litigation, brand value
GovernanceRules and ethics driving top leadership — anti-corruption, executive pay, independent board oversight.% independent directors, CEO-chair separation, CEO pay ratio, related-party transactions, auditor opinions, whistle-blower casesFraud risk, cost of capital, investor trust

Slide 16 examples of ESG in sustainable finance (Environmental): investing in solar energy projects; companies reducing carbon emissions; green bonds for renewable energy.

Add for Social: affordable housing loans, microfinance for women entrepreneurs, social bonds for hospitals. For Governance: independent boards, anti-bribery policies, sustainability-linked executive pay.

Materiality — which ESG issues matter most in each sector

Not every ESG issue matters equally for every company. Materiality means focusing on issues likely to affect the company's value (or its impact). SASB built industry-specific materiality maps; here is a simplified version for Indian sectors.

SectorMost material EMost material SMost material G
Banks / NBFCs (HDFC Bank)Financed emissions, climate risk in loan bookFinancial inclusion, data privacy, customer fairnessRisk management, board oversight, AML/KYC
IT services (Infosys)Energy use of campuses and data centresTalent attraction, diversity, data securityBusiness ethics, client confidentiality
FMCG / tobacco (ITC)Packaging waste, water, sustainable sourcingProduct health impacts, farmer livelihoodsRelated-party dealings, product responsibility
Steel (JSW Steel)GHG intensity (tCO2/tonne crude steel), air emissions, waterWorker safety, community resettlementCapital allocation to decarbonisation
Metals & mining (Vedanta)Tailings, biodiversity, pollution, GHGIndigenous/tribal rights, community consentGroup debt, related parties, legal compliance
CementProcess CO2, dustLocal communitiesPricing conduct (competition law)
Power utilitiesCoal share, GHG, fly ashAffordable accessRegulatory relations

SEBI's BRSR asks every top-1,000 listed Indian company to disclose, among other things, energy consumption and intensity, Scope 1 and 2 emissions, water withdrawal and consumption, waste generated and recycled (E); employee wellbeing, safety incidents, gender diversity, complaints (S); and ethics, anti-corruption and board committees (G). That is the ESG pillar model turned into law.

Q1. Classify: (a) CEO pay ratio (b) water withdrawal (c) data breach (d) related-party transactions.

(a) G (b) E (c) S (customer data privacy; some raters treat it as G) (d) G.

Q2. Why is "financed emissions" the key E issue for a bank but not for a steel maker?

A bank's own operations emit little; its real environmental footprint is the emissions of the companies it lends to (Scope 3, category 15). A steel maker's footprint is dominated by its own direct (Scope 1) emissions from blast furnaces.

1.5 Triple Bottom Line (TBL)

A normal income statement has one "bottom line" — net profit. TBL says a business should keep three scorecards: how much money it made (Profit), how it affected people (People), and how it affected nature (Planet). A company that makes profit by poisoning a river has a good first line and a terrible third line.

The Triple Bottom Line (John Elkington, 1994) is a sustainability framework which holds that businesses should measure and report their performance on three dimensions — economic (Profit), social (People) and environmental (Planet) — rather than on financial profit alone.

DimensionQuestionMeasuresExample
Profit (economic)Does the business create economic value (not just for shareholders but for the economy)?Revenue, profit, taxes paid, jobs created, supplier paymentsITC's agri-business income that also flows to farmers via e-Choupal
People (social)Is it fair to employees, communities, customers?Fair wages, safety, training hours, CSR impact, human rightsHDFC Bank's Parivartan rural development programme
Planet (environmental)Does it reduce its ecological footprint?Emissions, water, waste, biodiversityInfosys's carbon neutrality since 2020

Advantages

  • Broadens managerial focus from short-term profit to long-term value.
  • Improves reputation and stakeholder trust.
  • Identifies risks and cost savings (energy efficiency pays).
  • Provides the conceptual base for ESG reporting and the SDGs.

Limitations

  • No common unit — profit is in rupees, but people and planet are in tonnes, lives, hectares; they cannot simply be added.
  • Measurement subjectivity and risk of cherry-picking good numbers (greenwashing).
  • Trade-offs between lines are not resolved by the framework.
  • Elkington himself (2018) "recalled" the concept, saying it had become an accounting tool rather than a driver of system change.

TBL vs ESG: TBL is a performance scorecard from the company's view (what it achieved on three lines); ESG is a risk and evaluation lens mainly used by investors. G (governance) has no separate line in TBL — it is the "how" behind all three lines.

Q1. Who coined TBL and what are its "3Ps"?

John Elkington (1994): People, Planet, Profit.

Q2. State the main limitation of TBL.

The three lines lack a common unit of measurement, so they cannot be aggregated or traded off objectively.

1.6 Shareholder theory vs stakeholder theory (the theory behind TBL and ESG)

Shareholder (primacy) theory (Milton Friedman, 1970): the social responsibility of business is to increase its profits, within the rules of the game; managers are agents of shareholders.

Stakeholder theory (R. Edward Freeman, 1984): a firm should create value for all parties who affect or are affected by it — employees, customers, suppliers, communities, lenders, government and the environment — not only shareholders.

BasisShareholder theoryStakeholder theory
ObjectiveMaximise shareholder wealthBalance and create value for all stakeholders
AccountabilityTo ownersTo everyone affected
Time horizonOften short-term (quarterly results)Long-term
ExternalitiesGovernment's job to regulateFirm's responsibility to manage
Link to SFJustifies ESG only when financially material ("enlightened" version)Foundation of TBL, CSR, impact investing

Enlightened shareholder value (middle path): maximising long-run shareholder value requires treating stakeholders well. This is the logic of ESG integration. In 2019 the US Business Roundtable statement on the purpose of a corporation publicly endorsed serving all stakeholders.

Section 166(2) of the Companies Act, 2013 requires a director to act in the best interests of the company, its employees, the shareholders, the community and for the protection of the environment — a statutory stakeholder model. Section 135 (mandatory CSR) goes further.

1.7 UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and SDG financing

The SDGs are the world's "to-do list" for 2030: 17 goals agreed by all UN members. For finance they act as a shared dictionary — an investor can say "this bond finances SDG 7 (clean energy)" and everyone understands.

The Sustainable Development Goals are 17 global goals adopted by all UN member states in 2015 as part of the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development, to end poverty, protect the planet and ensure prosperity for all by 2030. They have 169 targets. (Slide 21: "The United Nations created 17 SDGs in 2015 to improve people's lives and protect the planet by 2030.")

#GoalFinance link (what can be financed)
1No PovertyMicrofinance, financial inclusion (Jan Dhan), social bonds
2Zero HungerAgri-credit, warehouse receipts, sustainable agriculture loans
3Good Health and Well-beingSocial bonds for hospitals, health impact funds
4Quality EducationEducation loans, social bonds for schools
5Gender EqualityWomen-entrepreneur loans, gender ("orange") bonds
6Clean Water and SanitationWater/wastewater green bonds, blue bonds
7Affordable and Clean EnergyGreen bonds for solar and wind, green loans, green microfinance (solar home systems)
8Decent Work and Economic GrowthMSME lending, social bonds for employment
9Industry, Innovation and Infrastructure (the SDG named in your syllabus)Green infrastructure, transition finance for steel/cement, green hydrogen
10Reduced InequalitiesInclusive finance, affordable credit
11Sustainable Cities and CommunitiesGreen buildings, metro rail, municipal green bonds
12Responsible Consumption and ProductionCircular-economy finance, waste management; target 12.6 encourages sustainability reporting
13Climate ActionAll climate finance: mitigation and adaptation
14Life Below WaterBlue bonds, sustainable fisheries
15Life on LandForestry, biodiversity finance, nature-based solutions
16Peace, Justice and Strong InstitutionsGovernance, anti-corruption (the "G" in ESG)
17Partnerships for the GoalsBlended finance, development finance, public-private partnerships

Memory trick for 1–6 ("basic needs"): Poverty, Hunger, Health, Education, Gender, Water → "Poor Hungry Humans Expect Good Water". 7–12 are "economy" goals (energy, work, industry, inequality, cities, consumption), 13–15 are "planet" (climate, water life, land life), 16–17 are "enablers" (institutions, partnerships).

SDG financing — what it means

SDG financing means mobilising public and private money to achieve the SDGs. UNCTAD and the UN estimate that developing countries face a financing gap of several trillion US dollars per year to achieve the SDGs; public budgets and aid alone cannot close it. Channels:

  1. Public finance — government budgets, sovereign green/social bonds, development banks (World Bank, ADB, NABARD, SIDBI).
  2. Private finance — ESG funds, green bonds, impact investing, CSR spending.
  3. Blended finance — public/philanthropic money takes first loss to attract private money (3.12).
  4. Innovative finance — social impact bonds/development impact bonds, social stock exchange, carbon markets.
  5. International cooperation — Addis Ababa Action Agenda (2015) on financing for development; climate finance commitments under the Paris Agreement.

Slide 21 examples of SDG-linked projects: solar energy projects in villages (SDG 7); free education programmes for children (SDG 4); clean drinking water facilities (SDG 6); women entrepreneurship loans (SDG 5, 8); tree plantation drives (SDG 13, 15).

NITI Aayog publishes the SDG India Index (since 2018) ranking states on SDG progress. India's Sovereign Green Bond framework maps eligible expenditure (solar, metro rail, afforestation) to SDGs 7, 11, 13 and 15. Mandatory CSR under Section 135 channels corporate money into Schedule VII activities that closely track SDGs 1–6.

Q1. How many SDGs and targets are there, and what is the deadline?

17 goals, 169 targets, 2030.

Q2. Which SDG does a green bond for a metro rail project mainly support?

SDG 11 (Sustainable Cities and Communities), with co-benefits for SDG 13 (Climate Action) and SDG 9 (Infrastructure).

Q3. Name three channels of SDG financing.

Public finance (budgets, sovereign bonds, DFIs), private finance (ESG funds, green bonds, impact investing) and blended finance.

1.8 International agreements on climate change and sustainable development

Climate change is a "tragedy of the commons": each country gains by polluting but everyone suffers the warming. Only agreements can make countries act together. The three big climate agreements are a relay race: UNFCCC (1992) set up the race track; Kyoto (1997) made only rich countries run; Paris (2015) made everyone run at a pace they choose themselves.

Slide 23 lists three conventions: UNFCCC (United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change), CBD (Convention on Biological Diversity) and CITES (Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species). Slides 24–30 cover UNFCCC, Kyoto and Paris.

(a) UNFCCC — 1992

The United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change is an international treaty adopted at the 1992 Earth Summit in Rio de Janeiro whose objective is to stabilise greenhouse gas concentrations in the atmosphere at a level that would prevent dangerous human interference with the climate system.

  • Climate change as a global issue: all countries recognised it affects the whole world.
  • Reduction of emissions: countries encouraged to reduce GHG emissions (no binding targets in the Convention itself).
  • Common But Differentiated Responsibilities and Respective Capabilities (CBDR-RC): all countries share responsibility, but developed countries, having emitted most historically, bear greater responsibility and must provide finance and technology.
  • Country groups: Annex I (industrialised countries and economies in transition), Annex II (richer subset that must provide finance), Non-Annex I (developing countries, including India).
  • Conference of the Parties (COP): the supreme decision-making body, meeting every year since COP1 (Berlin, 1995). COP3 produced Kyoto; COP21 produced Paris.
  • Importance: foundation for all later climate agreements (slide 25).

(b) Kyoto Protocol — 1997

The Kyoto Protocol (adopted at COP3 in 1997, in force 2005) is a protocol under the UNFCCC that set legally binding greenhouse-gas reduction targets for developed (Annex I) countries — on average about 5% below 1990 levels over the first commitment period 2008–2012 — and created three market-based "flexible mechanisms".

Flexible mechanismHow it works (slides 28–29)Unit tradedRelevance to India
Clean Development Mechanism (CDM)A developed country (or its companies) invests in an emission-reduction project in a developing country and receives credits for the reductions.Certified Emission Reductions (CERs), 1 CER = 1 tCO2eIndia was one of the largest CDM host countries (wind, solar, biomass, energy efficiency projects).
Joint Implementation (JI)One developed country invests in a project in another developed country (or economy in transition); countries jointly work on reductions.Emission Reduction Units (ERUs)Not applicable (India not Annex I).
International Emissions Trading (ET)Countries with spare allowed emissions sell them to countries exceeding their targets — countries trade carbon credits.Assigned Amount Units (AAUs)Inspired domestic schemes: PAT (2012), CCTS (2023).

Limitations of Kyoto: no targets for fast-growing developing emitters; the USA never ratified; Canada withdrew; the second commitment period (Doha Amendment, 2013–2020) had limited participation; CDM credit prices collapsed after 2012, and some projects were criticised for weak "additionality".

(c) Paris Agreement — 2015

The Paris Agreement, adopted at COP21 in Paris in December 2015 under the UNFCCC and in force from November 2016, is a legally binding international treaty whose goal is to hold the increase in global average temperature to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels and to pursue efforts to limit it to 1.5°C, with every country setting its own targets through Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs).

Full objectives and articles are taught in Unit 2 (2.4). Key features from slide 30: NDCs; climate finance support for developing nations; global cooperation; promotion of renewable energy.

BasisKyoto Protocol (1997)Paris Agreement (2015)
Who has targetsOnly developed (Annex I) countriesAll countries
Nature of targetsTop-down, legally binding numbersBottom-up, self-determined NDCs; the obligation to prepare and communicate NDCs is binding, the achievement is not
GoalCut emissions ~5% below 1990 (2008–12)Temperature goal: well below 2°C, pursue 1.5°C; net zero in second half of the century
MarketsCDM, JI, emissions tradingArticle 6 (6.2 bilateral ITMOs, 6.4 central mechanism, 6.8 non-market)
ReviewCommitment periodsFive-yearly NDC updates ("ratchet") and Global Stocktake every five years (first 2023)
FinanceLimitedArticle 2.1(c): make finance flows consistent with low-GHG, climate-resilient development; developed countries to provide climate finance

(d) Other sustainable-development agreements (slide 23 and beyond)

  • Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), 1992 (Rio): three objectives — conservation of biodiversity, sustainable use of its components, and fair and equitable sharing of benefits from genetic resources. The Kunming-Montreal Global Biodiversity Framework (2022) set targets for 2030, including protecting 30% of land and sea ("30 by 30"). India implements it through the Biological Diversity Act, 2002. Finance link: nature-related disclosures (TNFD), biodiversity credits.
  • CITES, 1973 (Convention on International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora): regulates international trade in listed species through permits under three Appendices (I — trade generally prohibited; II — controlled; III — listed by one country). Finance link: supply-chain due diligence for companies trading timber, leather, etc.
  • Montreal Protocol, 1987 (ozone-depleting substances) and its Kigali Amendment (2016) to phase down HFCs — often called the most successful environmental treaty.
  • Ramsar Convention, 1971 (wetlands).
  • 2030 Agenda / SDGs and the Addis Ababa Action Agenda (2015) on financing for development.
  • Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction 2015–2030 (physical-risk resilience).
Q1. Name the three Kyoto flexible mechanisms and their credit units.

CDM → CERs; Joint Implementation → ERUs; International Emissions Trading → AAUs.

Q2. What does CBDR mean?

Common But Differentiated Responsibilities: all countries share the duty to address climate change, but developed countries bear greater responsibility because of historical emissions and greater capacity.

Q3. What is the single biggest structural difference between Kyoto and Paris?

Kyoto imposed top-down binding targets only on developed countries; Paris requires every country to set its own targets (NDCs), reviewed and strengthened every five years.

1.9 Role of the financial system in sustainable development

Banks, markets and insurers are the "traffic police" of capital. Whatever they fund grows; whatever they refuse shrinks. If lenders charge more to a coal plant and less to a solar farm, the economy tilts green without a single new law.

The role of the financial system in sustainable finance is to mobilise savings and allocate them to sustainable economic activities, to price and manage environmental and social risks, to provide risk-sharing (insurance) against climate shocks, and to use its ownership and lending power to influence corporate behaviour — while maintaining financial stability.

  1. Capital mobilisation and allocation: channel savings (deposits, pensions, mutual funds) into renewable energy, green buildings, clean transport, affordable housing. Example: IREDA financing renewable projects; SBI's green bond proceeds.
  2. Risk pricing and management: build climate and ESG risk into credit appraisal and interest rates so polluting activities pay a risk premium. Example: RBI's draft guidance on climate scenario analysis for banks.
  3. Product innovation: green bonds, SLBs, green deposits, ESG mutual funds, carbon credits, parametric insurance.
  4. Risk transfer and insurance: insurers spread losses from floods and cyclones; parametric crop insurance (PMFBY uses yield data; weather-index products exist).
  5. Price discovery and information: markets and rating agencies convert ESG information into prices (greenium, ESG ratings).
  6. Stewardship and engagement: investors vote at AGMs and engage boards on climate targets. SEBI's Stewardship Code (2019/2020) applies to mutual funds and AIFs.
  7. Financial inclusion: microfinance, SHGs, PMJDY accounts — social dimension of sustainability.
  8. Maintaining stability: central banks (RBI in NGFS) ensure climate shocks do not trigger a financial crisis.
ActorSustainable-finance roleIndian example
Commercial banksGreen loans, ESG credit policy, exclusion lists, green depositsSBI, HDFC Bank green and sustainable finance
Development finance institutionsConcessional and long-tenor finance; de-risk projectsNABARD (climate adaptation funds), SIDBI (MSME energy efficiency), IREDA (renewables)
Capital marketsGreen/social/SLB bonds; ESG disclosureSEBI green debt framework; NSE/BSE listed green bonds
Asset managersESG funds, stewardshipESG mutual fund category under SEBI norms
InsurersUnderwriting climate risk, investing reserves sustainablyCrop and catastrophe insurance
Regulators / central bankRules, disclosure, supervision, stabilityRBI, SEBI, IRDAI

Because the financial system sits between savers and every productive project, changing how it allocates and prices risk is the fastest lever to move the whole economy towards sustainability.

1.10 Fundamentals of ESG risk management

ESG risks are "slow-burning fuses": a leaking tailings dam, an unsafe factory, a bribery culture. They do not show on this year's balance sheet but can explode into huge losses. ESG risk management is the habit of finding these fuses early.

ESG risk is the possibility that environmental, social or governance events or conditions cause an actual or potential negative impact on the value, earnings or reputation of a company or investment. ESG risk management is the continuous process of identifying material ESG issues, assessing their financial impact, mitigating them, and monitoring performance. (Slide 34 point 5: Identify → Assess → Mitigate → Monitor.)

Slide 34–35 key points (learn all ten)

  1. Beyond financials: modern management looks past balance sheets to non-financial risks across E, S and G.
  2. E pillar: carbon footprints, water scarcity, climate-related disruptions.
  3. S pillar: labour rights, workplace safety, community impact, data privacy.
  4. G pillar: anti-corruption, executive pay, independent board oversight.
  5. The risk cycle: Identify material issues → Assess financial impact → Mitigate → Monitor (continuous loop).
  6. Reporting shift: from retrospective financial reporting ("how much we made") to forward-looking sustainability reporting ("how our operations impact the future").
  7. Global standards (ISSB and GRI): ISSB focuses on how sustainability/climate risks affect company value; GRI focuses on how the company affects the wider world.
  8. India's mandate (BRSR): top 1,000 listed companies must file a Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report.
  9. BRSR Core and value chain: third-party assurance of core KPIs and ESG reporting on the value chain for large companies.
  10. Integrated reporting vs greenwashing: the goal is an integrated report combining financial and ESG data honestly, avoiding greenwashing (exaggerating or faking environmental claims).

The ESG risk-management process (expanded)

StepWhat is doneTools
1. IdentifyList ESG issues relevant to the business; prioritise by materialityMateriality assessment/matrix, SASB map, stakeholder consultation, risk register
2. AssessEstimate likelihood and financial impact (revenue, cost, asset value, cost of capital)Heat maps, scenario analysis, carbon pricing, ESG scores, stress tests
3. MitigateAvoid, reduce, transfer or accept the riskDecarbonisation capex, supplier codes, insurance, board policies, exclusion lists
4. Monitor & reportTrack KPIs, escalate to board, discloseESG dashboards, BRSR, TCFD/ISSB reports, internal audit

Integration into enterprise risk management (ERM): the COSO/WBCSD guidance (2018) on applying ERM to ESG-related risks recommends treating ESG risks within the same ERM framework as other risks rather than as a separate CSR activity. Board oversight is usually through a Risk Management Committee (mandatory for the top 1,000 listed companies under SEBI LODR) and a CSR/ESG committee.

The 2020 LG Polymers styrene gas leak in Visakhapatnam and the 2019 collapse of IL&FS (a governance failure) are Indian reminders that E and G risks become financial losses: plant shutdowns, penalties, write-offs and loss of investor confidence.

1.11 Financial and sustainability (impact) reporting and communication

Financial reporting tells you how the company did with money; sustainability reporting tells you how it did to the world (and how the world may hit it back). Investors now want both, in one comparable, audited story.

Sustainability reporting is the disclosure by an organisation of its economic, environmental, social and governance performance and impacts, and of the sustainability-related risks and opportunities that could affect its value, using recognised frameworks (e.g. BRSR, GRI, ISSB). Impact reporting specifically reports the measurable outcomes (e.g. tonnes of CO2 avoided, people served) of an investment or activity.

BasisFinancial reportingSustainability reporting
OrientationRetrospective (past results)Forward-looking (risks, targets, transition plans)
UnitMoneyMoney plus physical units (tCO2e, kL, injuries)
StandardsInd AS / IFRSBRSR, GRI, SASB, TCFD, ISSB (IFRS S1/S2), CDP, <IR>
AssuranceMandatory statutory auditGrowing: BRSR Core assurance/assessment in India
MaterialityFinancialFinancial and/or impact (double)

Slide 37 comparison of reporting frameworks:

FeatureBRSRGRISASBTCFD
ScopeIndiaGlobalGlobalGlobal
Main usersListed Indian companiesAll organisationsInvestors & companiesInvestors & companies
FocusESG & responsible businessSustainability impactsFinancially material ESG issuesClimate-related financial risks

Each framework, plus ISSB, CDP and Integrated Reporting, is taught in detail in Unit 4 (4.3). The key numerical skill of reporting — carbon accounting — is next.

Communication channels

  • Annual report (BRSR is part of it for listed companies), standalone sustainability/ESG report, integrated report.
  • Green bond allocation and impact reports; TCFD report; CDP questionnaire response.
  • Investor presentations, ESG factsheets, website ESG data portals.
  • Stakeholder engagement: AGMs, community consultations, supplier meets.

Do not confuse CSR reporting (Section 135 — how much was spent on Schedule VII activities) with BRSR (SEBI — ESG performance of the whole business). A company can spend its 2% CSR yet perform badly on BRSR metrics.

1.12 Carbon accounting: GHG Protocol, Scopes 1, 2 and 3 (with numericals)

You cannot manage what you cannot measure. Carbon accounting is simply "bookkeeping for greenhouse gases": multiply each activity (litres of diesel, kWh of electricity) by how much CO2 it releases, then add up. The Scopes tell you whose smoke it is: your own chimney (Scope 1), the power plant that made your electricity (Scope 2), or everyone else in your value chain (Scope 3).

Under the GHG Protocol Corporate Standard (WRI and WBCSD): Scope 1 = direct emissions from sources owned or controlled by the company (fuel combustion, company vehicles, process emissions, refrigerant leaks). Scope 2 = indirect emissions from the generation of purchased or acquired electricity, steam, heat or cooling. Scope 3 = all other indirect emissions in the value chain, upstream and downstream (15 categories, e.g. purchased goods, business travel, use of sold products, investments).

GHG emissions (tCO2e) = Activity data × Emission factor × GWP

Activity data = quantity of the activity (litres of diesel, MWh of electricity, tonnes of steel bought, km travelled). Emission factor (EF) = kg or tonnes of gas released per unit of activity (e.g. kg CO2 per litre). GWP (global warming potential) converts non-CO2 gases into CO2-equivalent (CO2 = 1; methane CH4 = 28; nitrous oxide N2O = 265 on the IPCC AR5 100-year basis). Use this for every carbon footprint question.

Emission intensity = Total emissions ÷ Output (or revenue)

Used to compare companies of different sizes, e.g. tCO2 per tonne of steel, tCO2e per ₹ crore revenue (BRSR asks for intensity per rupee of turnover).

Location-based Scope 2 = MWh × grid EF;   Market-based Scope 2 = (MWh not covered by renewable contracts/certificates) × residual EF

Emission factors used below are illustrative teaching values close to commonly published figures (diesel ≈ 2.68 kg CO2/litre; LPG ≈ 2.98 kg CO2/kg; Indian grid ≈ 0.71 tCO2/MWh, near the Central Electricity Authority's recent average; GWP of refrigerant R-410A = 2,088). In an exam, always use the factors given in the question.

Worked example 1.1 (basic) — Scope 1 and 2 footprint. GreenTex Ltd, a Tiruppur textile unit with revenue ₹250 crore, reports for the year: diesel for generators 50,000 litres; LPG 20,000 kg; refrigerant R-410A leakage 10 kg; purchased grid electricity 1,200 MWh. Compute Scope 1, Scope 2, total, and intensity.

  1. Diesel: 50,000 × 2.68 kg = 134,000 kg = 134.00 tCO2 (Scope 1 — own generators).
  2. LPG: 20,000 × 2.98 kg = 59,600 kg = 59.60 tCO2 (Scope 1).
  3. Refrigerant: 10 kg × GWP 2,088 = 20,880 kg = 20.88 tCO2e (Scope 1 — fugitive).
  4. Scope 1 = 134.00 + 59.60 + 20.88 = 214.48 tCO2e.
  5. Scope 2 (location-based): 1,200 MWh × 0.71 = 852.00 tCO2.
  6. Total Scope 1 + 2 = 214.48 + 852.00 = 1,066.48 tCO2e.
  7. Intensity = 1,066.48 ÷ 250 = 4.27 tCO2e per ₹ crore of revenue (4.2659, rounded).

Answer: Scope 1 = 214.48 t; Scope 2 = 852 t; total 1,066.48 tCO2e; intensity 4.27 t/₹ crore. Electricity is 80% of the footprint, so rooftop solar (the slide's Tiruppur example) is the biggest lever.

Worked example 1.2 (intermediate) — converting gases to CO2e. A fertiliser plant emits 1,000 t CO2, 5 t CH4 and 0.5 t N2O. Using GWP CH4 = 28 and N2O = 265, find total emissions in tCO2e.

  1. CO2: 1,000 × 1 = 1,000.0
  2. CH4: 5 × 28 = 140.0
  3. N2O: 0.5 × 265 = 132.5
  4. Total = 1,000 + 140 + 132.5 = 1,272.5 tCO2e

Answer: 1,272.5 tCO2e. Small tonnages of CH4 and N2O add 27% because of their high GWP.

Worked example 1.3 (reverse / market-based). GreenTex (1,200 MWh) signs a green power purchase agreement and buys renewable energy certificates covering 500 MWh. (a) Market-based Scope 2 using 0.71 as residual factor? (b) Reduction versus location-based?

  1. Location-based = 1,200 × 0.71 = 852 t.
  2. Uncovered electricity = 1,200 − 500 = 700 MWh.
  3. Market-based = 700 × 0.71 = 497 t.
  4. Reduction = 852 − 497 = 355 t (41.7%).

Answer: 497 tCO2; 355 t lower. Good practice (GHG Protocol) is to report both figures ("dual reporting").

Worked example 1.4 — Scope classification exercise. Classify each item for a cement company: (a) CO2 released when limestone is heated in its kiln; (b) electricity bought from the state DISCOM; (c) coal burnt in its captive power plant; (d) trucks owned by a logistics contractor moving its cement; (e) employees flying to a conference; (f) CO2 from customers' use of the cement; (g) emissions of the coal mine that supplies it.

  1. (a) Scope 1 (process emissions, own kiln).
  2. (b) Scope 2 (purchased electricity).
  3. (c) Scope 1 (captive plant is owned; its electricity is not "purchased").
  4. (d) Scope 3, category 4 or 9 (transport by third party — upstream if the company pays, downstream if the customer pays).
  5. (e) Scope 3, category 6 (business travel).
  6. (f) Scope 3, category 11 (use of sold products) — for cement, negligible direct use-phase emissions.
  7. (g) Scope 3, category 1 or 3 (purchased goods / fuel- and energy-related activities).

Worked example 1.5 (advanced) — adding Scope 3. GreenTex also buys 5,000 t of steel machinery parts (EF 2.3 tCO2/t) and has 400 employees who each commute 30 km per working day for 220 days in vehicles averaging 0.12 kg CO2/km. Compute these two Scope 3 items.

  1. Purchased goods: 5,000 × 2.3 = 11,500 tCO2 (category 1).
  2. Commuting: 400 × 220 × 30 km = 2,640,000 km; × 0.12 kg = 316,800 kg = 316.8 tCO2 (category 7).
  3. Scope 3 (these two) = 11,816.8 t, more than 11 times Scope 1 + 2 (1,066.48 t).

Lesson: Scope 3 is usually the largest share, which is why BRSR Core and ISSB push value-chain reporting.

Q1. A bank's financed emissions fall in which scope and category?

Scope 3, category 15 (investments).

Q2. Electricity from a company-owned rooftop solar plant: which scope?

Zero emissions from generation; it is own generation, so it is not Scope 2 (nothing purchased) and adds no Scope 1 CO2. It reduces Scope 2 by displacing grid power.

Q3. 2,000 litres of diesel at 2.68 kg/L = ? tCO2

2,000 × 2.68 = 5,360 kg = 5.36 tCO2 (Scope 1 if in own vehicles/generators).

1.13 Regulatory frameworks and governing bodies

Think of three layers: global standard-setters write the "rulebooks" (what to disclose, what counts as green), national regulators make them law, and market bodies (exchanges, rating agencies, verifiers) enforce them day to day.

BodyLevelRole in sustainable finance
