NITI AayogIndia (think-tank)SDG India Index

SEBI BRSR — the must-know Indian framework

  • Introduced by SEBI in May 2021, replacing the Business Responsibility Report (BRR). Voluntary for FY 2021-22, mandatory for the top 1,000 listed companies by market capitalisation from FY 2022-23.
  • Structure: Section A general disclosures; Section B management and process disclosures; Section C principle-wise performance disclosures under the nine NGRBC principles, each with essential (mandatory) and leadership (voluntary) indicators.
  • Nine principles: P1 ethics, transparency and accountability; P2 safe and sustainable goods and services; P3 employee well-being; P4 responsiveness to stakeholders; P5 human rights; P6 environment protection and restoration; P7 responsible and transparent public-policy advocacy; P8 inclusive growth and equitable development; P9 responsible engagement with consumers.
  • BRSR Core (July 2023): a smaller set of verifiable KPIs under nine ESG attributes (GHG footprint, water footprint, energy footprint, circularity/waste, employee well-being and safety, gender diversity, inclusive development, fairness with customers and suppliers, openness of business), subject to third-party assurance phased in from the top 150 listed companies (FY 2023-24) upward. Value-chain ESG disclosures were introduced for the largest companies on a comply-or-explain basis. In 2025 SEBI eased the regime, allowing an independent "assessment" as an alternative to "assurance" and deferring value-chain requirements.

SEBI green debt and ESG fund norms

  • Green debt securities: first disclosure requirements in 2017; revised in February 2023 to align with international principles — issuers must disclose use of proceeds, project evaluation, management of proceeds and reporting, obtain independent review, and follow do's and don'ts against greenwashing. The 2023 framework also introduced sub-categories such as blue bonds (water/ocean), yellow bonds (solar) and transition bonds.
  • ESG mutual fund schemes (2023): at least 65% of assets in companies reporting BRSR with comprehensive assurance (phased), fund managers must disclose ESG strategy (exclusion, integration, best-in-class and positive screening, impact, sustainable objectives, transition) and voting rationale, and obtain independent assurance of ESG compliance.
  • ESG Rating Providers (ERPs) (2023): SEBI brought ESG rating providers under regulation (within the Credit Rating Agencies Regulations), requiring registration and India-specific parameters.
Q1. Name the three sections of BRSR.

A: general disclosures; B: management and process disclosures; C: principle-wise performance disclosures (9 NGRBC principles).

Q2. Which body now maintains the SASB standards?

The IFRS Foundation (through the ISSB), after the Value Reporting Foundation was consolidated into it in 2022.

1.14 ESG ratings, rating agencies and rating divergence

An ESG rating is an opinion, issued by a specialised provider, on a company's exposure to and management of environmental, social and governance risks (or on its sustainability performance), usually expressed as a letter grade or score relative to peers.

ProviderWhat it measuresScale
MSCI ESG RatingsResilience to financially material ESG risks, relative to industry peersAAA, AA (leader); A, BBB, BB (average); B, CCC (laggard)
Sustainalytics (Morningstar) ESG Risk RatingUnmanaged ESG risk on an absolute scale (comparable across industries); lower is betterNegligible (0–10), Low (10–20), Medium (20–30), High (30–40), Severe (40+)
ISS ESG Corporate Rating (slide 41)ESG performance; ISS also provides governance research, proxy advisory and responsible investment solutionsA+ to D−; "Prime" status for leaders
CDP (slide 41)Environmental disclosure and performance on climate, water and forestsA, A−, B, B−, C, C−, D, D− (Leadership, Management, Awareness, Disclosure bands); F = failed to disclose
S&P Global CSACorporate Sustainability Assessment; basis of the Dow Jones Sustainability IndicesScore 0–100
Indian providersCRISIL ESG Ratings & Analytics, ICRA ESG Ratings, CareEdge ESG and others, registered with SEBI as ESG Rating ProvidersProvider-specific scores

Rating divergence (why ratings disagree)

Unlike credit ratings (correlation around 0.9+ between agencies), ESG ratings of the same company often differ widely. The "Aggregate Confusion" study by Berg, Kölbel and Rigobon (2022) found correlations between major raters of only about 0.38 to 0.71, and identified three sources:

  1. Scope divergence — raters include different attributes (one counts lobbying, another does not).
  2. Measurement divergence — they measure the same attribute with different indicators (e.g. labour practices via turnover vs via lawsuits). This was the largest source.
  3. Weight divergence — they weigh attributes differently.

Other causes: risk vs impact focus, industry-relative vs absolute scales, reliance on company-reported data, size and disclosure bias (big companies that publish more score better).

Composite ESG score = wE·E + wS·S + wG·G,   with wE + wS + wG = 1

E, S, G = pillar scores (0–100); w = weights reflecting materiality for the industry.

Worked example 1.6 — how weights create divergence. Two companies have pillar scores A: E 60, S 72, G 80; B: E 78, S 65, G 55. Rater X uses weights (E 0.4, S 0.3, G 0.3); rater Y uses (E 0.2, S 0.3, G 0.5). Compute composite scores and rank.

  1. Rater X, A: 0.4×60 + 0.3×72 + 0.3×80 = 24 + 21.6 + 24 = 69.6
  2. Rater X, B: 0.4×78 + 0.3×65 + 0.3×55 = 31.2 + 19.5 + 16.5 = 67.2
  3. Rater Y, A: 0.2×60 + 0.3×72 + 0.5×80 = 12 + 21.6 + 40 = 73.6
  4. Rater Y, B: 0.2×78 + 0.3×65 + 0.5×55 = 15.6 + 19.5 + 27.5 = 62.6

A ranks above B under both, but the gap widens from 2.4 to 11.0 points purely because of weights. With slightly different data, the ranking itself would flip — this is "weight divergence".

Worked example 1.7 (reverse). A rater uses E 0.5, S 0.2, G 0.3. A company has S = 70 and G = 60 and its composite score is 65. What is its E score?

  1. 65 = 0.5E + 0.2×70 + 0.3×60 = 0.5E + 14 + 18
  2. 0.5E = 65 − 32 = 33
  3. E = 66

ITC is often given reasonable-to-good ESG scores for environmental performance, yet many ESG funds exclude it because of tobacco — a live example of scope divergence (whether product harm is counted) and of exclusion-based strategies.

Q1. MSCI rates "relative to industry"; Sustainalytics rates on an "absolute" scale. Why does this matter?

An oil company may be an MSCI "AA" leader among oil peers yet have a "High" Sustainalytics risk because its absolute exposure is large. Investors must know which basis they are using.

Q2. Which source of divergence was found to be largest?

Measurement divergence.

1.15 Responsible investment and the Principles for Responsible Investment (PRI)

Responsible investment is a strategy and practice of incorporating environmental, social and governance factors into investment decisions and active ownership, to better manage risk and generate sustainable, long-term returns. (PRI's definition, paraphrased. Your Unit 3 slide: responsible investing "invests in companies that follow ethical, socially responsible and sustainable business practices and avoids industries with harmful impacts".)

The six PRI principles (launched 2006)

  1. We will incorporate ESG issues into investment analysis and decision-making processes.
  2. We will be active owners and incorporate ESG issues into our ownership policies and practices.
  3. We will seek appropriate disclosure on ESG issues by the entities in which we invest.
  4. We will promote acceptance and implementation of the Principles within the investment industry.
  5. We will work together to enhance our effectiveness in implementing the Principles.
  6. We will each report on our activities and progress towards implementing the Principles.

Mnemonic: I-O-D-P-C-R = Incorporate, Own actively, Disclosure, Promote, Collaborate, Report.

Responsible-investment strategies (exclusion, best-in-class, norms-based, integration, thematic, impact, engagement) are covered in 3.1.

Several Indian asset managers are PRI signatories, and SEBI's Stewardship Code requires mutual funds and AIFs to have a stewardship policy, monitor investee companies, and disclose voting. Indian proxy advisers (e.g. IiAS, InGovern, SES) publish voting recommendations on governance issues.

1.16 The pillars of sustainable finance

"Pillars" is used in two senses. Your slides use the E, S, G pillars (slide 34). Policy-makers (e.g. the EU Action Plan, G20 Sustainable Finance Roadmap) describe the structural pillars of a sustainable financial system. A full-marks answer mentions both.

Sense 1 — the three ESG pillars

Environmental, Social, Governance (see 1.4).

Sense 2 — structural pillars of a sustainable finance system

PillarPurposeIndian instance
1. Taxonomy and standardsDefine what counts as "green/sustainable"Climate finance taxonomy (draft 2025); SEBI green debt definitions
2. Disclosure and transparencyReliable, comparable ESG dataBRSR, BRSR Core, RBI climate-risk disclosure framework
3. Products and instrumentsChannel capitalSovereign green bonds, green deposits, ESG funds, SLBs
4. Risk management and supervisionPrice and control ESG/climate riskRBI scenario analysis & stress-test guidance
5. Governance, incentives and capacityBoard accountability, stewardship, skillsSEBI Stewardship Code, CSR committees

Sense 3 — the industry pillars (UNEP FI frameworks)

Sustainable banking (Principles for Responsible Banking, 2019), sustainable insurance (Principles for Sustainable Insurance, 2012) and sustainable investment (PRI, 2006).

Sense 4 — the five pillars accepted in your mid-sem scheme (August 2026)

The official scheme for mid-sem Q5(b) listed: (1) transparency and disclosure, (2) risk management, (3) capital mobilisation, (4) stakeholder value creation, (5) policy alignment — and accepted "any consistent framework taught in class". This list maps onto Sense 2: transparency = pillar 2; risk management = pillar 4; capital mobilisation = pillar 3; stakeholder value creation = pillar 5 (governance and stewardship); policy alignment = pillar 1 (taxonomy and national goals). For a lending question, pair each pillar with a concrete bank mechanism (ESG checks in underwriting, climate stress tests of the loan book, green-linked pricing) — see the solved answer in Past papers.

No single pillar works alone: products without taxonomy invite greenwashing; disclosure without risk management changes nothing; risk management without data is guesswork.

1.17 India vs developed economies: comparing sustainable finance regimes

Every major economy wants the same thing — money flowing to sustainable activity and honest reporting about it — but they build it differently. India mostly says "tell us" (disclosure). The EU also says "here is the official list of what counts as green" (classification). The US leans on "we will pay you to go green" (tax incentives) more than on disclosure rules. Knowing these three styles lets you answer any "compare India with..." question.

A disclosure-based regime requires companies to report ESG information but leaves the judgement of what is "sustainable" to investors (India's BRSR). A classification-based regime defines by regulation which activities or products qualify as sustainable and requires products to be labelled against that definition (the EU Taxonomy and SFDR). An incentive-based approach changes behaviour mainly through subsidies and tax credits (the US Inflation Reduction Act, 2022).

The four regimes side by side

BasisIndiaEuropean UnionUnited StatesUnited Kingdom
Lead bodiesSEBI (listed companies, funds, green debt); RBI (banks); Ministry of Finance (taxonomy, sovereign green bonds)European Commission; ESMA and national supervisorsSEC (securities); states such as CaliforniaFCA; UK government
Core company disclosureBRSR, mandatory for the top 1,000 listed companies (FY 2022-23); BRSR Core KPIs with phased assuranceCSRD with ESRS: large companies, listed or unlisted, double materialityNo federal climate-disclosure rule in force: the SEC's 2024 rule was stayed amid litigation and the SEC stopped defending it in 2025; California has its own lawsTCFD-aligned climate disclosure mandatory for listed and large companies; moving towards ISSB-based UK standards
Classification / taxonomyClimate finance taxonomy in draft (2025); SEBI green debt definitionsEU Taxonomy (2020): six environmental objectives, "do no significant harm", minimum safeguardsNone at federal levelNo green taxonomy in force
Fund / product rulesSEBI ESG fund categories; ESG schemes must invest mostly in companies with assured BRSR Core disclosuresSFDR (2021): Article 6, 8 ("light green"), 9 ("dark green") fundsLimited; SEC "names rule" for fund namesSustainability Disclosure Requirements and investment labels; anti-greenwashing rule (2024)
AssuranceReasonable assurance of BRSR Core KPIs, phased from the top 150; independent "assessment" allowed as an alternative from 2025Limited assurance of the sustainability statementNot required federallyNot generally mandatory
Main climate leverDisclosure + targets (NDC, Net Zero 2070) + carbon market (CCTS) + sovereign green bondsRegulation + carbon price (EU ETS) + CBAMTax credits and subsidies (IRA, 2022)Disclosure + transition plans + UK ETS
Recent directionEasing in 2025 (assessment option; value-chain deferral) after rapid build-up2025 "Omnibus" simplification: later CSRD waves delayed, narrower scope proposedFederal retreat from disclosure; state-level actionContinued build-out of transition-plan expectations

Five named differences to use in a comparison answer

  1. Scope of applicability: India's BRSR covers listed companies by market-cap rank; CSRD reaches large unlisted companies and non-EU groups with large EU turnover.
  2. Disclosure vs classification: India asks companies to disclose; the EU also defines what "green" means and forces funds to classify themselves.
  3. Assurance design: India requires reasonable assurance on a small, fixed set of Core KPIs; the EU requires limited assurance over the whole report.
  4. Materiality: the EU mandates double materiality; ISSB-based regimes (the UK path) use financial materiality; India's BRSR is principle-based (the nine NGRBC principles) and covers both impacts and risks.
  5. Enforcement maturity: EU rules sit on years of supervisory practice; India's regime is young and still building assurance, ratings (SEBI ESG Rating Providers, 2023) and taxonomy capacity.

What the comparison suggests about India's path

  • India sequences: data first (BRSR), credibility next (Core + assurance), classification last (taxonomy) — a deliberate, proportionate strategy for a developing economy that must fund growth and transition together.
  • Regulatory capacity (assurance providers, data infrastructure) is the binding constraint, which explains phasing by company size.
  • The EU's own 2025 simplification shows that very broad mandates carry high compliance costs — support for India's gradual approach.
  • External pressure still matters: exporters to the EU face CBAM and CSRD value-chain data requests regardless of Indian rules.

Country initiatives at a glance (the CIA 3 list)

CountryInitiativeWhat it is, in one line
IndiaSovereign Green Bonds & SEBI ESG disclosureGovernment green bonds (framework 2022, first issues 2023, auctioned by RBI) financing public green projects; BRSR/BRSR Core for listed companies.
European UnionEU Green Deal & EU TaxonomyGreen Deal (2019) aims at climate neutrality by 2050; the Taxonomy defines sustainable activities for investors and companies.
United StatesInflation Reduction Act & climate investment incentives2022 law providing large tax credits for clean power, EVs and clean-tech manufacturing.
ChinaGreen Credit Policy & Carbon Neutrality 2060Green credit guidelines for banks (from 2012); pledge to peak emissions before 2030 and reach carbon neutrality before 2060; national emissions trading scheme (2021).
JapanGX (Green Transformation) financing strategyPublic–private decarbonisation programme funded partly by sovereign "climate transition bonds" (first issued 2024).
United KingdomTransition Finance FrameworkGuidance and market work on credible transition plans and financing for hard-to-abate sectors.
SingaporeMAS Green Finance Action PlanMonetary Authority of Singapore plan (2019) for resilience, green finance markets, technology and skills; Singapore-Asia Taxonomy (2023).
BrazilAmazon conservation & climate financeAmazon Fund (2008) financing forest protection with donor money; sovereign sustainable bonds.
UAESustainable finance framework & Net Zero 2050National net-zero-by-2050 initiative; host of COP28 (2023).
South AfricaJust Energy Transition Investment PlanPlan under the Just Energy Transition Partnership (announced at COP26, 2021) to finance a shift away from coal while protecting workers and communities.
IndonesiaBlue bonds & Energy Transition MechanismOcean-linked (blue) bond issuance; ADB's Energy Transition Mechanism for early retirement of coal plants; a JETP agreed in 2022.
KenyaClimate adaptation finance & green bond programmeAdaptation finance for drought and climate resilience; development of a local green bond market.

Indian exporters of steel, aluminium and cement feel the EU regime directly: under CBAM (definitive phase from 2026) EU importers pay for the embedded emissions of these goods, so Indian producers must measure product-level emissions even though Indian law does not demand it. This is the clearest case of a developed-economy rule reshaping Indian corporate decisions.

"Compare India with..." questions reward named, specific differences; this table gives you four jurisdictions and five difference axes to choose from.

1. Is India's BRSR a disclosure-based or classification-based system?

Disclosure-based: it requires reporting against nine principles and Core KPIs but does not itself define which activities are "green"; the taxonomy (draft 2025) is the classification layer still being built.

2. Name the three EU instruments that make up its sustainable finance rulebook.

EU Taxonomy (what is sustainable), SFDR (fund classification and disclosure), CSRD with ESRS (company reporting, double materiality).

3. Give one reason India's phased approach can be defended.

Limited assurance and data capacity plus the need to keep compliance costs proportionate for a growing economy; building data before classification avoids an unenforceable rulebook.

1.18 Green finance and responsible investment: what the evidence says (course reading)

Course reading: S. Varnika and Rajveer S. Rawlin (CHRIST University), "Mapping the Landscape of Green Finance and Responsible Investment", Chapter 3 in Green Finance, Fiscal Resilience, and the Transformation of Public Financial Administration (IGI Global). It is a systematic literature review of 30 peer-reviewed articles published in 2025. The concepts below are taught in this guide's own words.

Green finance works like fertiliser: it helps the plant grow, but only in good soil. The "soil" is regulation, certification, disclosure and governance. The same green bond can transform one market and do little in another.

Green finance directs financial resources — instruments, policy frameworks and institutions — towards environmental goals such as carbon reduction, resource efficiency, climate resilience and green technology. Responsible investment incorporates environmental, social and governance factors into investment decisions, aiming to earn returns while promoting ethical and societal benefits.

1. The responsible-investment family

ApproachCore idea
Socially responsible investment (SRI)Values-based, typically screening out harmful activities
ESG investingESG factors used as inputs to risk and return analysis
Sustainable investingPortfolios aligned with long-term sustainability goals
Impact investingCapital allocated to deliver measurable environmental or social outcomes alongside financial returns

The reading notes that responsible investment has moved from a niche ethical practice to a mainstream approach to long-term value creation and risk management, driven by investor preferences, environmental awareness, stakeholder pressure and demand for transparency.

2. Four findings on green finance instruments

  1. Green bonds are the dominant instrument for renewable energy, sustainable infrastructure and low-carbon technology. They improve access to long-term funds, can lower the cost of capital and encourage green innovation and R&D.
  2. How funds are used matters as much as access: outcomes depend on how firms deploy the money, not on the label alone.
  3. Hybrid financing works better: firms combining bank-based green lending with market-based instruments (green bonds, sustainability-linked securities) show better sustainability performance than firms relying on one source, because of greater flexibility and diversified funding.
  4. Credibility infrastructure drives demand: external reviews, rigorous certification and transparent disclosure reduce information asymmetry and raise investor confidence; weak standards invite greenwashing and reduce capital flows.

3. Financial performance: mixed and context-dependent

  • No consistent evidence that green-labelled bonds outperform conventional bonds on yield, liquidity or risk-adjusted return — a green premium ("greenium") is neither universal nor stable.
  • Green instruments can offer hedging and diversification benefits in turbulent markets — value for long-term risk management rather than short-term outperformance.
  • Outcomes depend on market maturity, regulatory quality, governance, macroeconomic conditions and investor preferences. Green finance should therefore be judged on its contribution to transition, resilience and long-term value, not on short-term returns alone.

4. Governance and responsible investment

  • Board diversity (gender, expertise, independence) is associated with stronger adoption of socially responsible investment through better oversight and stakeholder representation.
  • Concentrated ownership tends to limit it, because dominant shareholders may favour short-term returns over long-term sustainability.
  • Persistent problems: no standardised ESG metrics, multiple divergent rating systems, weak comparability across sectors and greenwashing.

5. Policy and regulatory frameworks

The policy instruments most often studied are green credit guidelines, carbon pricing, fiscal (tax) incentives, sustainable finance taxonomies, mandatory ESG disclosure and public investment in climate projects. The reading finds these are especially effective in emerging economies, where they compensate for underdeveloped markets, and are associated with lower emissions, more green innovation and growth. But effectiveness depends on implementation capacity: institutional quality, coordination among regulators, monitoring and enforcement, and reliable ESG data. Without that, green finance "may remain symbolic rather than transformative", and one uniform model does not fit every country.

6. Research gaps identified

  1. Few long-term studies (most look at short- to medium-term effects).
  2. Evidence concentrated in China and a few emerging markets; limited cross-country comparison.
  3. Lack of standardised ESG measurement.
  4. Heterogeneous, context-specific performance evidence.
  5. Policy and investor behaviour rarely studied together.

Method note: a systematic literature review (SLR) follows the PRISMA protocol — identification (database search with keywords and Boolean operators), screening (titles and abstracts), eligibility (full-text quality check) and inclusion — to make a review transparent and replicable.

India shows each finding: sovereign green bonds (2023 onwards) have attracted only a small greenium; SEBI requires independent external review for green debt securities to build credibility; RBI's green deposit framework and SEBI's BRSR are the "policy and disclosure" layer; and the draft climate finance taxonomy addresses the standardisation gap.

1. According to the course reading, do green bonds consistently outperform conventional bonds?

No. The evidence is mixed; a green premium is neither universal nor stable. Their value lies more in long-term risk management, diversification and transition finance.

2. Which governance features support socially responsible investment, and which hinder it?

Board diversity (gender, expertise, independence) supports it; concentrated ownership tends to hinder it.

3. Why is green finance policy often more effective in emerging economies, and what can undermine it?

Regulation fills gaps left by underdeveloped markets; weak institutional capacity, fragmented regulators, poor data and weak enforcement undermine it.

Unit 1 exam kit

Questions most likely to be set from Unit 1, with answers written at full-marks length. Open each, then try to rewrite it from memory.

Section A style (5 marks)

Q1. Define sustainable finance and explain its scope. (5)

Definition. Sustainable finance refers to financial activities, investments and banking decisions that consider not only profit but also environmental, social and governance (ESG) factors, aiming to support long-term economic growth while protecting the environment and society.

Scope.

  1. Institutions: banks, NBFCs/MFIs, insurers, mutual funds, pension funds, DFIs (NABARD, SIDBI, IREDA), regulators (RBI, SEBI) and governments.
  2. Instruments: green, social, sustainability and sustainability-linked bonds; green loans and deposits; ESG funds; carbon credits; microfinance; impact funds.
  3. Activities: ESG integration in credit and investment decisions, stewardship, risk management, disclosure (BRSR).
  4. Objectives: climate mitigation and adaptation, social inclusion, good governance — aligned to the SDGs and Paris goals.
  5. Materiality: both how ESG affects value (financial) and how the firm affects the world (impact) — double materiality.

Example: India's Sovereign Green Bonds (2023) raised ₹16,000 crore for solar, metro and afforestation projects. Conclusion: sustainable finance widens finance from "return on capital" to "return on capital plus responsibility".

Q2. Explain the Triple Bottom Line with its limitations. (5)

Introduction. The Triple Bottom Line (TBL), coined by John Elkington in 1994, says companies should report performance on three bottom lines rather than one.

  1. Profit (economic): value created for shareholders and the economy — profits, taxes, jobs.
  2. People (social): fair wages, safety, community welfare, human rights.
  3. Planet (environmental): emissions, water, waste, biodiversity.

Limitations: (i) no common unit, so lines cannot be added; (ii) subjective measurement allows cherry-picking; (iii) no guidance on trade-offs; (iv) risk of becoming a reporting ritual — Elkington himself "recalled" the concept in 2018.

Example: ITC reports being carbon positive, water positive and solid-waste recycling positive (planet), supports farmer livelihoods through e-Choupal (people) and remains highly profitable (profit). Conclusion: TBL is the conceptual root of ESG and BRSR reporting.

Q3. Write a note on the Kyoto Protocol and its flexible mechanisms. (5)

Introduction. The Kyoto Protocol (1997, COP3, in force 2005) under the UNFCCC was the first treaty with legally binding emission-reduction targets, applicable to developed (Annex I) countries — roughly 5% below 1990 levels in 2008–2012.

Flexible mechanisms:

  1. Clean Development Mechanism: developed countries invest in emission-reduction projects in developing countries and earn Certified Emission Reductions (1 CER = 1 tCO2e). India was a major host.
  2. Joint Implementation: projects between developed countries, earning Emission Reduction Units.
  3. Emissions Trading: countries with surplus allowances (AAUs) sell to those exceeding targets.

Significance: created the first global carbon price and carbon-credit industry. Limitations: no targets for developing countries, USA did not ratify, weak additionality in some CDM projects. Conclusion: Kyoto's market ideas survive in Paris Article 6 and India's CCTS.

Q4. Explain the ESG risk management cycle. (5)

Meaning. ESG risk is the potential for environmental, social or governance events to harm a company's value; managing it is a continuous cycle, not a one-time task.

  1. Identify material ESG issues through a materiality assessment (e.g. water for a textile firm).
  2. Assess likelihood and financial impact using heat maps, scenario analysis and carbon pricing.
  3. Mitigate by avoiding, reducing (efficiency capex), transferring (insurance) or accepting.
  4. Monitor KPIs, report to the board's risk committee and disclose (BRSR, TCFD).

A diagram of a circular arrow Identify → Assess → Mitigate → Monitor earns presentation marks. Example: a Tiruppur textile firm identifies water scarcity, assesses cost of tanker water, installs zero-liquid-discharge recycling, and monitors kL per tonne of fabric. Conclusion: embedding ESG risk into enterprise risk management protects long-term value.

Q5. Distinguish between GRI and SASB (or ISSB). (5)
BasisGRISASB / ISSB
MaterialityImpact (inside-out): company's effect on economy, environment, peopleFinancial (outside-in): sustainability issues affecting enterprise value
AudienceAll stakeholdersInvestors / capital providers
StructureUniversal, sector and topic standardsSASB: 77 industry standards; ISSB: IFRS S1 general + S2 climate
Origin1997, Boston (CERES, Tellus Institute)SASB 2011; ISSB 2021 (IFRS Foundation)
StatusMost widely used voluntary standardISSB being adopted into law by many jurisdictions

They are complementary: many companies use GRI for impacts and ISSB/SASB for investor-relevant disclosures. In India, BRSR borrows from both.

Section B style (10 marks)

Q6. Trace the history and evolution of sustainable finance, globally and in India. (10)

Introduction. Sustainable finance evolved from ethical exclusion by religious investors into a mainstream, regulated discipline that integrates ESG factors into all financial decisions. Its evolution can be traced in three eras.

Era 1 — Ideas and awareness (1713–1992). 1713: Carlowitz's sustained-yield forestry. Religious "sin-stock" exclusions; 1971 Pax World Fund; 1970s–80s anti-apartheid divestment. 1962: Silent Spring exposed pollution. 1972: Stockholm conference and UNEP. 1987: Brundtland Report defined sustainable development. 1992: Rio Earth Summit (UNFCCC, CBD); UNEP FI formed.

Era 2 — Treaties and the ESG language (1997–2014). 1997: Kyoto Protocol created carbon markets. 2004: Who Cares Wins coined ESG. 2006: PRI's six principles. 2003: Equator Principles for project finance. 2007–08: first green bonds (EIB, World Bank). 2014: ICMA Green Bond Principles.

Era 3 — Products and mandatory rules (2015 onwards). 2015: SDGs and Paris Agreement (Article 2.1(c) on finance flows). 2017: TCFD recommendations; NGFS formed. 2020: SLB Principles. 2021–23: ISSB and IFRS S1/S2. Growth of green, social and sustainability-linked bonds and climate stress testing.

Indian evolution. State-led social finance (bank nationalisation, priority sector lending, NABARD's SHG-bank linkage 1992); Environment (Protection) Act 1986; Energy Conservation Act 2001; NAPCC 2008; NGT 2010; SEBI BRR 2012; PAT 2012; mandatory CSR under Companies Act 2013; Yes Bank's first green bond 2015; SEBI green debt norms 2017; NGRBC 2019; BRSR 2021 (mandatory FY 2022-23); Panchamrit and Net Zero 2070 (COP26); Sovereign Green Bonds, green deposits framework, BRSR Core, ESG fund norms, CCTS and Green Hydrogen Mission (2023).

Drivers of evolution: scientific evidence on climate change; investor recognition that ESG affects risk and return; regulatory mandates; stakeholder and consumer pressure; product innovation.

Conclusion. The journey has moved from "avoid harm" (SRI) to "manage risk" (ESG) to "create measurable impact" (impact and transition finance). India's path has been regulation-led, and it now has one of the most comprehensive disclosure regimes among emerging markets.

Q7. Discuss the role of the financial system in promoting sustainable development. (10)

Introduction. The financial system intermediates between savers and investors; by deciding which activities receive capital and at what price, it shapes the economy's path. Aligning it with sustainability is essential for the SDGs and India's Net Zero 2070.

  1. Mobilising capital: green bonds, green deposits (RBI framework 2023) and ESG funds gather savings for sustainable uses.
  2. Allocating capital: ESG screening in credit appraisal directs lending to renewable energy, clean transport, green buildings; exclusion lists restrict harmful sectors.
  3. Pricing risk: climate and ESG risks built into interest rates and valuations (e.g. higher risk premium for coal assets).
  4. Risk transfer: insurance and reinsurance protect against climate disasters; parametric insurance speeds payouts.
  5. Innovation: SLBs, blended finance, carbon credits, tokenised green bonds.
  6. Stewardship: investors engage boards and vote on climate targets (SEBI Stewardship Code).
  7. Inclusion: microfinance and SHG lending advance SDGs 1, 5 and 8.
  8. Information: ratings, disclosures and indices turn ESG information into market signals.
  9. Stability: RBI (NGFS member) guides banks on climate stress testing so shocks do not become crises.

Indian illustration: SBI and HDFC Bank's green finance, IREDA's renewable lending, NABARD as National Implementing Entity for the Adaptation Fund, SEBI BRSR.

Challenges: data gaps, greenwashing, short-termism, higher cost of capital for developing countries.

Conclusion. The financial system is both exposed to sustainability risks and a powerful lever to address them; well-designed regulation and markets can make it the engine of a just transition.

Q8. Explain international agreements on climate change and sustainable development, and compare Kyoto with Paris. (10)

Introduction. Because climate change is a global commons problem, cooperation through treaties is essential. The main agreements are UNFCCC (1992), Kyoto Protocol (1997) and Paris Agreement (2015), supported by CBD and CITES on biodiversity.

UNFCCC (1992): adopted at Rio; objective to stabilise GHG concentrations; principle of Common But Differentiated Responsibilities; annual COPs; foundation of later treaties.

Kyoto (1997): binding targets for developed countries; flexible mechanisms — CDM, JI, emissions trading.

Paris (2015): well below 2°C, pursue 1.5°C; NDCs from all countries, updated every five years; climate finance, technology transfer and capacity building for developing countries; Global Stocktake; Article 6 markets.

CBD (1992): conservation, sustainable use, fair benefit-sharing; Kunming-Montreal Framework (2022). CITES (1973): regulates trade in endangered species.

Comparison table: (use the Kyoto vs Paris table from section 1.8: who has targets, nature of targets, goal, markets, review, finance).

Relevance for finance: carbon credit markets, climate finance flows, NDC-linked sovereign green bonds and disclosure rules all derive from these agreements.

Conclusion. The shift from Kyoto's top-down approach to Paris's universal bottom-up model broadened participation; the challenge now is ambition and finance for implementation.

Section C style (15-mark case)

Case 1. GreenTex Ltd (Tiruppur) and its ESG journey (15)

Case: GreenTex Ltd, a listed Tiruppur garment exporter (revenue ₹250 crore), has just entered SEBI's top-1,000 list. European buyers demand emission data and proof of fair labour practices. Its data: diesel 50,000 L, LPG 20,000 kg, refrigerant leak 10 kg R-410A, grid electricity 1,200 MWh. A local NGO alleges effluent discharge into the Noyyal river. Questions: (a) Which ESG issues are material? (5) (b) Compute Scope 1 and 2 emissions and intensity (5) (c) Recommend a reporting and mitigation plan (5).

(a) Material ESG issues. E: water use and effluent (history of dyeing-unit pollution in the region and court orders on zero liquid discharge), energy and GHG emissions. S: labour rights, wages, working hours, safety, supply-chain human rights (buyer due diligence). G: compliance culture, transparency to buyers, board oversight of ESG. Stakeholder theory explains why buyers, workers, the river community and regulators all matter.

(b) Emissions (from worked example 1.1): Scope 1 = 134 + 59.6 + 20.88 = 214.48 tCO2e; Scope 2 = 852 tCO2; total 1,066.48 tCO2e; intensity 4.27 tCO2e per ₹ crore. Electricity is about 80% of the footprint.

(c) Plan. Reporting: prepare BRSR (now mandatory), with BRSR Core KPIs on energy, water, GHG, safety and gender; align with GRI for buyer reports; respond to CDP supply-chain requests. Mitigation: rooftop solar and a green PPA (Scope 2 can fall to about 497 t with 500 MWh covered); energy-efficient machinery; zero liquid discharge and water recycling (as Tiruppur units have adopted, per slide 3); third-party social audits; grievance mechanism. Finance: a green loan (under LMA Green Loan Principles) or SIDBI energy-efficiency finance for solar and ETP upgrades. Governance: board ESG committee, whistle-blower policy, NGO engagement.

Conclusion. Addressing material issues protects export orders, reduces energy costs and lowers financing costs — turning compliance into competitive advantage, exactly the "better decisions → sustainable growth" chain on your Unit 2 slide.

Unit 2 — CSR-ESG and Climate Change

Syllabus: define climate change and its drivers; impact of climate change on corporate financial performance; objectives of the Paris Agreement (2015) and COP 26, 27 and 28; regulations set by the Government of India to mitigate climate risks; regulatory and testing strategies (scenario analysis, stress testing, credit rating); impact of climate change and ESG on managerial decisions and corporations. (6 teaching hours; CO3.)

2.1 CSR vs ESG (the "CSR-ESG" in the unit title)

CSR is what a company gives back from its profits (a school, a health camp). ESG is how the company earns those profits in the first place (its emissions, its labour practices, its board). A company can fund a village school (good CSR) while polluting the village river (bad ESG).

Corporate Social Responsibility (CSR) is a company's commitment to contribute to social and environmental well-being beyond its legal and economic obligations; in India it is a statutory duty under Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013. ESG is a framework used mainly by investors and lenders to assess how a company manages environmental, social and governance risks and opportunities across its entire business.

BasisCSRESG
FocusGiving back / philanthropy and community projectsRisk and performance of core operations
Main audienceSociety, communities, governmentInvestors, lenders, rating agencies (and all stakeholders)
MeasurementAmount spent, projects completedQuantified metrics: tCO2e, LTIFR, board independence, scores
Indian lawCompanies Act 2013, Sec 135 + Schedule VII (MCA)SEBI LODR — BRSR / BRSR Core
Link to valueIndirect (reputation, licence to operate)Direct (cost of capital, valuation, risk)
Time frameAnnual spend cycleLong-term strategy and targets

Section 135 in brief

  • Applicability: company with net worth ≥ ₹500 crore, or turnover ≥ ₹1,000 crore, or net profit ≥ ₹5 crore in the immediately preceding financial year.
  • Spend: at least 2% of the average net profits of the three immediately preceding financial years on activities in Schedule VII (hunger and poverty, education, gender equality, environmental sustainability, rural development, PM's relief fund, etc.).
  • Governance: CSR committee of the board (not required where the obligation is up to ₹50 lakh; the board then discharges the functions); CSR policy; disclosure in the board's report. Unspent amounts must be transferred to a Schedule VII fund or, for ongoing projects, to an Unspent CSR Account.
CSR obligation = 2% × (NPt−1 + NPt−2 + NPt−3) ÷ 3

NP = net profit computed as per Section 198 for each of the three preceding years.

Worked example 2.1. A company's net profits for the last three years were ₹120 crore, ₹150 crore and ₹180 crore. Minimum CSR spend?

  1. Average = (120 + 150 + 180) ÷ 3 = 450 ÷ 3 = ₹150 crore.
  2. CSR = 2% × 150 = ₹3 crore.

Answer: ₹3 crore. (Reverse: if CSR spend required is ₹4 crore, average profit must be 4 ÷ 0.02 = ₹200 crore.)

Examiners like "CSR is a subset of ESG" questions: CSR is one part of the Social pillar; ESG covers everything the business does.

2.2 Climate change and its drivers

Greenhouse gases act like a blanket around the Earth. A thin blanket keeps us warm enough to live; burning coal, oil and gas for 200 years has thickened the blanket, so the Earth keeps more heat, and weather patterns shift.

Climate change refers to long-term changes in Earth's temperature, rainfall, wind patterns and other climatic conditions, mainly caused by increased greenhouse gas emissions due to human activities. (Course definition — Unit 2 slide 2.)

The UNFCCC (Article 1) defines climate change as a change of climate attributed directly or indirectly to human activity that alters the composition of the global atmosphere, in addition to natural variability. The IPCC uses a broader definition covering any change, natural or human-caused. Your course definition matches the UNFCCC's human-caused emphasis.

Drivers of climate change

DriverHow it warms the planetMain sectors
Burning fossil fuels (CO2)Largest source of CO2; CO2 stays in the atmosphere for centuriesPower (coal), transport (oil), industry
Deforestation and land-use changeReleases stored carbon and removes carbon sinksAgriculture expansion, mining, urbanisation
Agriculture (CH4, N2O)Methane from rice paddies and livestock; nitrous oxide from fertilisersFarming, dairy
Industrial processesChemical reactions release CO2 (cement clinker, steel blast furnaces)Cement, steel, chemicals
WasteMethane from landfills and wastewaterMunicipal solid waste
Fluorinated gasesVery high GWP (thousands of times CO2)Refrigeration, air-conditioning
Natural driversSolar cycles, volcanic eruptions, ocean cycles (El Niño) — cause short-term variability but cannot explain recent warming—

Scientific consensus: the IPCC's Sixth Assessment Report (2021) concluded that it is unequivocal that human influence has warmed the atmosphere, ocean and land; global surface temperature in 2011–2020 was about 1.1°C above 1850–1900. The World Meteorological Organization reported 2024 as the first calendar year above 1.5°C (a single year does not yet mean the Paris long-term limit is breached, since that refers to multi-decade averages).

Effects

Rising temperatures and heatwaves; changing monsoon patterns; floods and droughts; cyclones of greater intensity; sea-level rise; glacier melt (Himalayas); crop-yield loss; water stress; health impacts; biodiversity loss.

Slide 3 example — Textile industry, Tiruppur (Tamil Nadu): adopting solar energy, water recycling and energy-efficient machinery to reduce carbon emissions, conserve resources and lower operating costs — improving both environmental and business performance. Use this example in any answer on climate action by Indian businesses.

Q1. List four human drivers of climate change.

Fossil fuel combustion, deforestation/land-use change, agriculture (methane and nitrous oxide), industrial processes (cement, steel), waste, fluorinated gases.

Q2. Why is methane important despite smaller volumes?

It has a much higher warming effect per tonne (GWP about 28 over 100 years, and far higher over 20 years).

2.3 Impact of climate change on corporate financial performance

Slide 4: Climate change increases operating costs, disrupts supply chains and reduces productivity, affecting business profitability. It also raises compliance and insurance costs while encouraging companies to invest in sustainable practices to ensure long-term financial stability.

Climate-related risks (TCFD classification) are of two main types: physical risks, arising from the physical effects of climate change — acute (event-driven: floods, cyclones, heatwaves) and chronic (long-term shifts: rising temperature, sea level, changed rainfall); and transition risks, arising from the shift to a low-carbon economy — policy and legal, technology, market and reputation. A third category, liability risk, refers to claims for compensation by parties who suffer climate-related losses (Mark Carney, 2015).

Risk typeSub-typeExampleFinancial statement impact
PhysicalAcuteChennai floods (2015) shut factories; cyclones hit Odisha and Gujarat portsAsset damage (impairment), lost revenue, higher insurance premiums
ChronicHeat stress lowering labour productivity; water scarcity for thermal power and textilesHigher operating cost, lower capacity utilisation
TransitionPolicy & legalCarbon pricing (CCTS), EU CBAM on steel/aluminium exports, emission normsHigher costs, compliance capex, fines
TechnologyCheap solar and EVs displacing coal and ICE vehiclesStranded assets, write-downs, R&D spending
MarketCustomers and buyers switching to low-carbon productsLower demand, price changes, cost of raw materials
ReputationConsumer boycotts, investor divestment, talent lossLower revenue, higher cost of capital
Liability—Litigation against polluters; NGT compensation ordersProvisions, penalties, legal costs

Climate-related opportunities (TCFD)

Resource efficiency (lower energy bills); energy source (cheaper renewables); products and services (EVs, green steel); new markets (green bonds, carbon credits); resilience (adaptation products).

How climate change shows up in the financial statements

  • Income statement: revenue loss from shutdowns; higher energy, water, insurance, carbon and compliance costs; lower margins.
  • Balance sheet: impairment of assets in flood zones or of coal assets ("stranded assets"); provisions for environmental liabilities; higher capex.
  • Cash flow: volatile operating cash flow; capex for adaptation and decarbonisation.
  • Cost of capital: lenders and investors demand higher returns from high-risk firms; low-risk firms access cheaper green finance (greenium).
  • Valuation: lower expected cash flows and higher discount rates reduce firm value.

Indian steel and aluminium exporters (JSW Steel, Vedanta) face the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism: after a reporting-only transitional phase from October 2023, importers must pay for embedded emissions from 2026. High-carbon Indian steel therefore faces a price disadvantage in Europe — a transition risk turning into a market risk.

Q1. Classify: (a) a carbon tax, (b) a flood, (c) EV adoption reducing petrol demand, (d) a lawsuit by flood victims against a polluter.

(a) transition – policy; (b) physical – acute; (c) transition – technology/market; (d) liability.

Q2. What is a stranded asset?

An asset that loses value earlier than expected (write-down or early retirement) because of climate-related changes such as regulation, technology or market shifts — e.g. a new coal plant made uneconomic by cheap solar and carbon pricing.

2.4 Objectives of the Paris Agreement (2015)

Slide 7: The Paris Agreement, adopted in 2015 under the UNFCCC, aims to limit the global average temperature increase to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels while striving to keep it below 1.5°C. It encourages all countries to reduce greenhouse gas emissions through NDCs, promote renewable energy, improve energy efficiency and strengthen climate resilience. It also emphasises climate finance, technology transfer and capacity building to help developing countries.

Objectives (Article 2) and key articles

ArticleContent
2.1(a) MitigationHold warming well below 2°C and pursue efforts to limit it to 1.5°C above pre-industrial levels
2.1(b) AdaptationIncrease ability to adapt to climate impacts and foster resilience and low-emission development without threatening food production
2.1(c) FinanceMake finance flows consistent with a pathway towards low GHG emissions and climate-resilient development
2.2 EquityImplemented reflecting equity and CBDR-RC in light of national circumstances
4 NDCs & net zeroGlobal peaking of emissions as soon as possible; balance between emissions and removals (net zero) in the second half of the century; each Party prepares NDCs, updated every five years, each more ambitious than the last ("progression")
6 Cooperative approaches6.2: bilateral transfers of mitigation outcomes (ITMOs); 6.4: UN-supervised crediting mechanism (successor to CDM); 6.8: non-market approaches
7 AdaptationGlobal goal on adaptation; national adaptation plans
8 Loss and damageRecognises averting, minimising and addressing loss and damage (Warsaw International Mechanism)
9–11 Means of implementationDeveloped countries provide climate finance; technology transfer; capacity building
13 TransparencyEnhanced Transparency Framework for reporting progress
14 Global StocktakeCollective progress assessed every five years; first completed in 2023 at COP28

India's NDC and Panchamrit

India's first NDC (2015)Updated NDC (August 2022)
Reduce emissions intensity of GDP by 33–35% by 2030 from 2005 levelReduce emissions intensity of GDP by 45% by 2030 from 2005 level
About 40% of installed electric power capacity from non-fossil sources by 2030About 50% cumulative installed capacity from non-fossil sources by 2030
Additional carbon sink of 2.5–3 billion tCO2e through forest and tree cover by 2030Retained; plus promotion of a sustainable lifestyle (LiFE – Lifestyle for Environment)

Panchamrit (five nectar elements, announced by the Prime Minister at COP26, Glasgow, 2021):

  1. 500 GW of non-fossil energy capacity by 2030.
  2. 50% of energy requirements from renewable energy by 2030.
  3. Reduce total projected carbon emissions by one billion tonnes by 2030.
  4. Reduce carbon intensity of the economy by 45% by 2030 (from 2005 levels).
  5. Achieve Net Zero by 2070.

India reported reaching around 40% non-fossil installed capacity in 2021 and crossing the 50% mark in 2025, well ahead of the 2030 target. India also submitted its Long-Term Low Emission Development Strategy (LT-LEDS) at COP27 (2022).

"Emissions intensity" (emissions per unit of GDP) is not the same as "emissions". India's target is intensity-based, so absolute emissions can still grow while intensity falls. Never write "India will cut emissions by 45%".

Q1. Which article of the Paris Agreement is the legal base for sustainable finance?

Article 2.1(c): making finance flows consistent with low-GHG, climate-resilient development.

Q2. State India's updated NDC targets.

45% reduction in emissions intensity of GDP by 2030 over 2005; about 50% of installed power capacity from non-fossil sources by 2030; 2.5–3 GtCO2e additional carbon sink.

2.5 COP 26, 27 and 28 (with COP 29 and 30 for context)

The Conference of the Parties (COP) is the supreme decision-making body of the UNFCCC, where all member countries meet annually to review implementation and negotiate further action.

COPThemeMain outcomesIndia
COP26
Glasgow, UK, 2021		"Keep 1.5°C alive"Glasgow Climate Pact: first COP text to target coal — "phase down" unabated coal power and phase out inefficient fossil-fuel subsidies; countries asked to strengthen 2030 targets; Paris Rulebook completed, including Article 6 carbon-market rules and transparency; call to at least double adaptation finance by 2025 (from 2019); Global Methane Pledge (cut methane 30% by 2030 from 2020); forest declaration; GFANZ for financial institutions; ISSB announcedPanchamrit and Net Zero 2070; LiFE movement; intervened for "phase down" rather than "phase out" of coal
COP27
Sharm el-Sheikh, Egypt, 2022		Implementation, climate justice and adaptationHistoric agreement to establish a Loss and Damage Fund for vulnerable countries; Sharm el-Sheikh Implementation Plan; mitigation work programme and just-transition work programme; focus on food security, water and resilienceSubmitted LT-LEDS; pushed for phase-down of all fossil fuels, not only coal
COP28
Dubai, UAE, 2023		First Global Stocktake"UAE Consensus": call for transitioning away from fossil fuels in energy systems in a just, orderly and equitable manner to reach net zero by 2050; triple global renewable capacity and double the rate of energy-efficiency improvement by 2030; Loss and Damage Fund operationalised with initial pledges; framework for the Global Goal on Adaptation; greater private-sector participationLaunched the Green Credits initiative; proposed to host COP33 in 2028
COP29 (beyond syllabus)
Baku, Azerbaijan, 2024		"Finance COP"New Collective Quantified Goal (NCQG): developed countries to take the lead in mobilising at least US$300 billion a year by 2035 for developing countries, with a call to scale total flows to US$1.3 trillion a year; Article 6 carbon-market standards operationalisedCriticised the NCQG as inadequate
COP30 (beyond syllabus)
Belém, Brazil, 2025		Implementation; forestsHeld in the Amazon, with focus on the new round of NDCs and on forests (launch of the Tropical Forest Forever Facility)—

Slides 11, 13, 15 summary: COP26 — 1.5°C target, stronger targets, reduced coal use, methane cuts, more climate finance, renewable expansion; India's Net Zero 2070. COP27 — climate justice, adaptation, Loss and Damage Fund, food security, water, resilience. COP28 — first Global Stocktake, transition away from fossil fuels, triple renewables, double energy efficiency by 2030, more private-sector participation.

Memory hook: 26 = Commitments (Panchamrit, coal phase-down), 27 = Compensation (loss and damage), 28 = Check-up (Global Stocktake, triple renewables).

Implications for finance

  • COP26's GFANZ and ISSB pushed banks and companies to set net-zero targets and disclose.
  • COP27's Loss and Damage Fund created new public finance channels for adaptation.
  • COP28's tripling of renewables implies huge investment demand — green bonds and loans.
  • COP29's NCQG and Article 6 rules shape cross-border climate finance and carbon credit trade.

2.6 Regulations set by the Government of India to mitigate climate risks

India uses three kinds of tools: command-and-control laws (EPA, NGT — "do this or pay"), missions and schemes (NAPCC, Solar Mission, FAME, Green Hydrogen — "we will help you do this"), and market mechanisms (PAT, CCTS — "do better than your target and sell the surplus").

(1) National Action Plan on Climate Change (NAPCC), 2008

The NAPCC, released by the Prime Minister's Council on Climate Change in June 2008, is India's national strategy to promote development while addressing climate change, implemented through eight national missions.

#Mission (slide 18)PurposeNodal ministry / note
1National Solar Mission (Jawaharlal Nehru NSM, 2010)Promote solar energy and reduce fossil-fuel useMNRE; target raised from 20 GW to 100 GW of solar by 2022 in 2015
2National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency (NMEEE)Improve energy efficiency and save energyMinistry of Power/BEE; includes PAT
3National Mission on Sustainable HabitatEco-friendly cities, green buildings, waste management, public transportHousing and Urban Affairs
4National Water MissionConserve water; improve water-use efficiency (target 20%)Jal Shakti
5National Mission for Sustaining the Himalayan EcosystemProtect Himalayan glaciers, environment and biodiversityScience and Technology
6National Mission for a Green IndiaIncrease forest and tree cover; restore ecosystemsMoEFCC
7National Mission for Sustainable AgricultureClimate-resilient farming practicesAgriculture
8National Mission on Strategic Knowledge for Climate ChangeStrengthen climate research and innovationScience and Technology

Mnemonic for the 8 missions: "S-E-H-W-H-G-A-K" — "Sun Energy Houses Water Himalaya Green Agriculture Knowledge". States also prepare State Action Plans on Climate Change (SAPCCs).

(2) Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 (slide 20)

  • Umbrella legislation for environmental protection, enacted after the Bhopal gas tragedy (1984).
  • Empowers the Central Government to set standards for emissions and discharges, regulate hazardous substances and waste, restrict industrial locations, and issue directions (including closure).
  • Industries must install pollution-control equipment and obtain environmental clearances (Environmental Impact Assessment notification issued under it).
  • Many later rules derive from it: hazardous waste rules, plastic waste rules, e-waste rules, and the Green Credit Rules, 2023 (a market-based programme rewarding voluntary actions like tree plantation and water conservation).

Supporting laws: Water (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act 1974 and Air (Prevention and Control of Pollution) Act 1981 (these created the Central and State Pollution Control Boards).

(3) National Green Tribunal Act, 2010 (slide 29)

  • Established the NGT (principal bench in New Delhi, with regional benches) to handle environmental disputes quickly; it is required to endeavour to dispose of cases within six months.
  • Applies the principles of sustainable development, precaution and polluter pays; can award compensation and restitution.
  • Example: NGT has ordered industries polluting rivers to pay environmental compensation and restore the environment (e.g. orders on Ganga and Yamuna pollution).

(4) Energy Conservation Act, 2001 and the 2022 Amendment

  • Created the Bureau of Energy Efficiency (BEE); star-labelling of appliances; Energy Conservation Building Code; energy norms for "designated consumers" (large energy users).
  • The Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act, 2022 empowered the government to specify a carbon credit trading scheme, to mandate minimum use of non-fossil energy by designated consumers, and extended building codes to large residential buildings.

(5) Perform, Achieve and Trade (PAT) scheme (slide 25)

PAT is a market-based mechanism under the NMEEE (first cycle 2012–15) that sets mandatory specific energy consumption (SEC) reduction targets for energy-intensive "designated consumers" (steel, cement, aluminium, textiles, fertiliser, power, pulp and paper, chlor-alkali and others). Units that beat their target receive tradable Energy Saving Certificates (ESCerts); units that fall short must buy ESCerts or pay a penalty. 1 ESCert = 1 tonne of oil equivalent (toe) of energy saved. ESCerts trade on the power exchanges (IEX, PXIL).

Slide example: a cement company that reduces energy consumption beyond its target can sell its ESCerts to other industries. (Numerical in 2.10.)

(6) Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS), 2023 (slide 23)

The Carbon Credit Trading Scheme, 2023, notified under the Energy Conservation Act (as amended in 2022), establishes the Indian Carbon Market. Under its compliance mechanism, obligated entities in notified sectors receive GHG emission-intensity targets (tCO2e per unit of output); entities that beat the target earn Carbon Credit Certificates (1 CCC = 1 tCO2e) that can be sold to entities that miss their targets. An offset mechanism lets non-obligated entities register emission-reduction projects and earn credits voluntarily.

  • Governance: National Steering Committee for the Indian Carbon Market; BEE as administrator; Grid Controller of India as registry; CERC regulates trading on power exchanges.
  • PAT is being transitioned into CCTS; emission-intensity targets were notified for sectors such as aluminium, cement, chlor-alkali, and pulp and paper in 2025.
  • Slide example: a renewable energy company earning carbon credits by reducing emissions and selling them to industries exceeding limits (this is the offset side).

(7) National Green Hydrogen Mission (slide 22)

  • Approved in January 2023 with an initial outlay of ₹19,744 crore; target of at least 5 million metric tonnes per annum of green hydrogen production capacity by 2030.
  • Green hydrogen = hydrogen produced by electrolysis of water using renewable electricity (no CO2). "Grey" hydrogen comes from natural gas (CO2 released).
  • Aims to decarbonise hard-to-abate sectors (steel, fertilisers, refining, transport) and cut fossil-fuel imports. Includes the SIGHT programme (incentives for electrolyser manufacturing and green hydrogen production).
  • Slide example: steel plants replacing coal-based processes with green hydrogen (hydrogen-based direct reduced iron).

(8) National Solar Mission (slide 26)

One of the eight NAPCC missions; promotes solar parks, rooftop solar and solar-powered infrastructure. Related schemes: PM-KUSUM (solar pumps for farmers), PM Surya Ghar: Muft Bijli Yojana (rooftop solar for households, 2024). Slide example: schools, colleges and industries installing rooftop solar to cut electricity costs. India also co-founded the International Solar Alliance (2015).

(9) FAME India scheme (slide 27)

  • Faster Adoption and Manufacturing of (Hybrid and) Electric Vehicles: Phase I from 2015; Phase II from April 2019 with a budget of about ₹10,000 crore (later enhanced), focusing on e-2W, e-3W, e-buses and charging infrastructure. Succeeded in 2024 by the Electric Mobility Promotion Scheme and the PM E-DRIVE scheme.
  • Demand incentives (subsidies) on purchase; support for charging infrastructure; linked to domestic manufacturing (PLI for auto and advanced chemistry cell batteries).

(10) SEBI BRSR (slide 28)

Requires top listed companies to report ESG performance, enhancing transparency for investors; example: disclosure of carbon emissions, energy use, waste management and social initiatives (details in 1.13).

InstrumentTypeYearUnit / target
EPALaw (command and control)1986Standards, clearances
Energy Conservation ActLaw2001 (amended 2022)BEE, designated consumers, carbon market enabling
NAPCCPolicy (8 missions)2008—
NGT ActLaw (tribunal)2010Compensation, polluter pays
PATMarket mechanism2012ESCert = 1 toe
FAMESubsidy scheme2015 / 2019EV adoption
BRSRDisclosure regulation2021Top 1,000 listed companies
CCTSMarket mechanism2023CCC = 1 tCO2e
National Green Hydrogen MissionMission20235 MMT per annum by 2030
Green Credit ProgrammeMarket incentive (under EPA)2023Green credits for voluntary actions

2.7 RBI and SEBI measures on climate risk

Reserve Bank of India

  • NGFS membership (April 2021) and a Sustainable Finance Group within RBI.
  • Discussion Paper on Climate Risk and Sustainable Finance (July 2022), based on a survey showing most banks were at an early stage of climate-risk management.
  • Framework for Acceptance of Green Deposits (April 2023, effective June 2023): banks and deposit-taking NBFCs raising green deposits must have a board-approved policy, allocate proceeds to an eligible green list (renewable energy, energy efficiency, clean transport, climate adaptation, sustainable water and waste management, pollution prevention, green buildings, biodiversity), obtain third-party verification and publish allocation and impact reports.
  • Draft Disclosure Framework on Climate-related Financial Risks (February 2024): TCFD-style disclosures under four pillars — Governance, Strategy, Risk Management, Metrics and Targets — phased in by type of regulated entity.
  • Draft Guidance on Climate Scenario Analysis and Stress Testing (2024): regulated entities to assess resilience of their business under a range of climate scenarios.
  • Priority Sector Lending: loans for renewable energy (solar, biomass, wind, micro-hydel) and for renewable-energy-based public utilities are eligible, within specified limits.
  • Sovereign Green Bonds: auctioned by RBI on behalf of the Government since January 2023.

SEBI

  • BRSR and BRSR Core; green debt securities framework (2017, revised 2023) including blue, yellow and transition bonds; ESG fund norms; ESG rating provider regulation; Social Stock Exchange framework; Stewardship Code.

2.8 Regulatory and testing strategies: scenario analysis, stress testing, credit rating

Slides 30–33: (1) Scenario analysis evaluates the impact of different future climate scenarios on businesses and financial institutions, considering extreme weather, carbon regulation and the transition to renewables; helps develop long-term adaptation strategy. Example: assessing a company under a high carbon tax or severe drought. (2) Stress testing measures the ability of institutions and companies to withstand severe climate-related shocks — losses from floods, hurricanes, supply-chain disruptions and policy changes. Example: banks testing loan portfolios against climate disasters. (3) Credit rating evaluates creditworthiness considering climate-related risks, ESG performance and environmental vulnerabilities. Example: strong ESG practices may earn a better credit outlook.

(1) Scenario analysis

Climate scenario analysis is a forward-looking method of assessing the potential effects on an organisation of a range of plausible, internally consistent future states of the climate and economy (e.g. orderly transition, delayed transition, hot-house world), without assigning probabilities, to test the resilience of strategy.

NGFS scenario families (used by central banks and the RBI draft guidance):

CategoryDescriptionExample scenariosRisk emphasis
OrderlyEarly, gradual, ambitious policyNet Zero 2050; Below 2°CModerate transition, low physical
DisorderlyLate or uneven action, then abrupt policyDelayed TransitionHigh transition risk
Hot house worldOnly current policies/NDCs; warming well above 2°CCurrent Policies; NDCsHigh physical risk
Too little, too lateLate and insufficient actionFragmented WorldHigh physical and transition

Steps: (1) identify material climate risks; (2) select scenarios and time horizons (short, medium, long); (3) translate scenarios into variables (carbon price, temperature, demand); (4) assess impact on revenue, cost, assets, PD/LGD; (5) identify responses; (6) disclose (TCFD Strategy pillar asks for resilience "taking into consideration different climate-related scenarios, including a 2°C or lower scenario").

(2) Stress testing

Climate stress testing is a quantitative exercise that measures the losses a bank, insurer or company would suffer under a severe but plausible climate-related shock (e.g. sudden carbon price, major flood), to check whether capital and liquidity remain adequate.

BasisScenario analysisStress testing
PurposeStrategic resilience; explore uncertaintySolvency/capital adequacy under severe shock
HorizonLong (to 2050 and beyond)Shorter (typically 1–5 years), sometimes longer for climate
NatureMultiple plausible futures, often qualitative plus quantitativeQuantitative "what if worst case" losses
OutputStrategic insights, transition planLoss estimates, capital shortfall, PD/LGD shifts
UserManagement, board, investors (TCFD)Regulators, risk managers

Examples: Bank of England's Climate Biennial Exploratory Scenario (2021), ECB's climate stress test (2022); the RBI's draft guidance (2024) for Indian regulated entities.

(3) Credit rating (ESG in credit ratings)

  • Credit rating agencies now explicitly consider ESG factors that affect default risk: carbon regulation exposure, physical asset vulnerability, governance quality. Moody's publishes ESG Credit Impact Scores (CIS-1 positive to CIS-5 very highly negative); S&P publishes ESG credit indicators.
  • Distinguish: a credit rating measures default risk (can the issuer repay?), whereas an ESG rating measures ESG risk/performance. ESG only enters a credit rating to the extent it affects creditworthiness.
  • Green bonds may receive a separate green-ness evaluation (e.g. CICERO shades of green for India's sovereign green bond framework) — this is not a credit rating.

India's Sovereign Green Bond framework (November 2022) received a "Medium Green" shading with "Good" governance from CICERO Shades of Green. A lender assessing a thermal-power borrower may lower its internal rating if a carbon-price scenario sharply lowers its interest coverage.

2.9 Impact of climate change and ESG on managerial decisions and corporations

Slide 35 summary table:

AreaPointsExample
Climate change impactGuides strategic and financial decisions; encourages sustainable investment and climate resilience; helps manage environmental risksInvesting in renewable energy technologies
ESG impactSupports responsible decisions and risk management; builds trust and attracts investors; promotes transparency and accountabilityESG reporting for better governance
Impact on corporationsImproves reputation and efficiency; reduces risks and increases sustainable growth; creates competitive advantageSustainable business practices for long-term success

Climate Awareness + ESG Integration → Better Decisions → Sustainable Growth

Decision areas affected

Managerial decisionHow climate/ESG changes itExample
Capital budgetingUse an internal carbon price in NPV; climate-adjusted discount rates; scenario-tested cash flowsMahindra & Mahindra adopted an internal carbon price (US$10/tCO2e) in 2017
FinancingAccess to green bonds, SLBs, green loans at lower cost; lenders' ESG conditionsUltraTech Cement and JSW Steel sustainability-linked bonds (2021)

Supply chainSupplier ESG audits; Scope 3 targets; diversification against climate shocksBRSR Core value-chain disclosures
Product and market strategyLow-carbon products; entering EV, solar, green steel marketsTata Motors EVs; green hydrogen ventures
Risk managementClimate risk in ERM; insurance; hedging energy pricesTCFD-aligned risk registers
HR and compensationESG-linked executive pay; safety culture; talent attractionESG KPIs in variable pay
Reporting and investor relationsBRSR, CDP, ratings management; engagement with ESG investorsInfosys ESG Vision 2030 reporting
M&A and portfolioESG due diligence; divesting stranded assetsExit from coal assets by global miners

Impact on corporations (benefits and costs)

  • Benefits: lower energy and resource costs; access to cheaper capital; better ratings and index inclusion; brand and customer loyalty; talent; resilience; regulatory goodwill; new markets.
  • Costs/challenges: upfront capex; data and reporting burden; uncertain policy; short-term margin pressure; greenwashing risk if claims outrun action.

2.10 Unit 2 numericals: carbon price, PAT, CCTS, stress testing, physical risk, internal carbon price

Carbon cost = (Emissions − Free allowance) × Carbon price
ESCerts (PAT) = (SECtarget − SECachieved) × Production (baseline)
CCCs (CCTS) = (Target intensity − Achieved intensity) × Output
Expected loss EL = PD × LGD × EAD
Expected annual physical loss = Annual probability of event × Damage ratio × Asset value

SEC = specific energy consumption (toe per tonne of product). Positive result = certificates earned; negative = shortfall to buy. PD = probability of default; LGD = loss given default (%); EAD = exposure at default.

Worked example 2.2 — PAT ESCerts. Cement plant A has a notified target SEC of 0.76 toe/t and achieves 0.74 toe/t; plant B's target is also 0.76 but it achieves 0.78. Baseline production for each is 1,000,000 t. ESCert price is ₹2,000 (illustrative). Compute ESCerts and cash impact.

  1. Plant A: (0.76 − 0.74) × 1,000,000 = 0.02 × 1,000,000 = 20,000 ESCerts earned.
  2. Value = 20,000 × ₹2,000 = ₹4,00,00,000 = ₹4 crore revenue if sold.
  3. Plant B: (0.76 − 0.78) × 1,000,000 = −20,000 → must buy 20,000 ESCerts (₹4 crore) or face penalty.

A earns ₹4 crore; B pays ₹4 crore. The market moves money from under-performers to over-performers — exactly the slide's cement example.

Worked example 2.3 — CCTS. An aluminium smelter's GHG intensity target is 0.60 tCO2e per tonne; it achieves 0.57; output 2,000,000 t. CCC price ₹1,500 (illustrative). (a) Credits? (b) Value? (c) If instead it had reached 0.62, what would it owe?

  1. (a) (0.60 − 0.57) × 2,000,000 = 0.03 × 2,000,000 = 60,000 CCCs.
  2. (b) 60,000 × ₹1,500 = ₹9,00,00,000 = ₹9 crore.
  3. (c) (0.60 − 0.62) × 2,000,000 = −40,000 → must surrender 40,000 CCCs (₹6 crore at ₹1,500).

Worked example 2.4 — carbon price and EBITDA (transition risk). A cement company emits 3,000,000 tCO2 a year and receives 2,400,000 t of free allowances. The carbon price is ₹2,000/t. EBITDA is ₹1,500 crore. (a) Annual carbon cost and % of EBITDA? (b) If it passes 50% of the cost to customers?

  1. Uncovered emissions = 3,000,000 − 2,400,000 = 600,000 t.
  2. Cost = 600,000 × ₹2,000 = ₹1,20,00,00,000 = ₹120 crore.
  3. % of EBITDA = 120 ÷ 1,500 = 8.0%.
  4. With 50% pass-through: net cost ₹60 crore = 4.0% of EBITDA.

Lesson: pricing power (ability to pass on costs) is a key determinant of transition risk.

Worked example 2.5 — climate stress test on a loan book. A bank has ₹10,000 crore of loans to carbon-intensive sectors. Baseline PD = 2%, LGD = 45%. Under a disorderly-transition scenario, PD rises to 5%; under a severe version LGD also rises to 55% (collateral loses value). Compute expected loss in each case.

  1. Baseline EL = 0.02 × 0.45 × 10,000 = ₹90 crore.
  2. Scenario 1 EL = 0.05 × 0.45 × 10,000 = ₹225 crore (2.5 times baseline).
  3. Severe scenario EL = 0.05 × 0.55 × 10,000 = ₹275 crore.
  4. Extra provisions needed vs baseline = 275 − 90 = ₹185 crore.

Worked example 2.6 — physical risk: expected annual loss. A plant worth ₹800 crore sits in a flood plain. Historically a major flood occurs with 2% annual probability (1-in-50 years) and damages 25% of the asset. Under a high-warming scenario the probability becomes 5% (1-in-20) and damage 35%.

  1. Current EAL = 0.02 × 0.25 × 800 = ₹4 crore/year.
  2. Probability only rises: 0.05 × 0.25 × 800 = ₹10 crore.
  3. Both rise: 0.05 × 0.35 × 800 = ₹14 crore/year (3.5 times current).

Management response: flood defences, insurance, or relocating critical equipment.

Worked example 2.7 — internal carbon price in capital budgeting. A firm must choose a boiler for 10 years (discount rate 10%). Coal boiler A: capex ₹10 crore, fuel ₹3 crore/year, emits 20,000 tCO2/year. Biomass boiler B: capex ₹14 crore, fuel ₹3.2 crore/year, emits 2,000 tCO2/year. Compare present value (PV) of costs without and with an internal carbon price of ₹1,500/t.

  1. Annuity factor (10%, 10 years) = [1 − 1.10−10] ÷ 0.10 = 6.1446.
  2. Without carbon price: PV(A) = 10 + 3 × 6.1446 = ₹28.43 crore; PV(B) = 14 + 3.2 × 6.1446 = ₹33.66 crore → A looks cheaper.
  3. Annual carbon charge: A = 20,000 × 1,500 = ₹3.0 crore; B = 2,000 × 1,500 = ₹0.3 crore.
  4. With carbon price: PV(A) = 28.43 + 3.0 × 6.1446 = ₹46.87 crore; PV(B) = 33.66 + 0.3 × 6.1446 = ₹35.51 crore.

Decision flips to biomass boiler B, saving ₹11.36 crore in PV terms. This is how ESG changes managerial decisions.

Q1. SEC target 1.20, achieved 1.15 toe/t, baseline production 400,000 t. ESCerts?

(1.20 − 1.15) × 400,000 = 0.05 × 400,000 = 20,000 ESCerts.

Q2. EAD ₹500 crore, PD 4%, LGD 50%. EL?

0.04 × 0.50 × 500 = ₹10 crore.

Unit 2 exam kit

Section A style (5 marks)

Q1. Define climate change and explain its drivers. (5)

Definition. Climate change refers to long-term changes in Earth's temperature, rainfall, wind patterns and other climatic conditions, mainly caused by increased greenhouse gas emissions due to human activities.

Drivers: (1) burning of fossil fuels for power, transport and industry — the largest source of CO2; (2) deforestation and land-use change, which release stored carbon and destroy sinks; (3) agriculture — methane from livestock and rice, nitrous oxide from fertilisers; (4) industrial processes — cement and steel; (5) waste — landfill methane; (6) fluorinated gases from refrigeration. Natural factors (solar cycles, volcanoes) cause only short-term variability.

Evidence: IPCC AR6 found human influence unequivocal, with about 1.1°C warming. Example: Tiruppur textile units adopting solar, water recycling and efficient machinery to reduce emissions. Conclusion: since the drivers are economic activities, the solution requires redirecting finance.

Q2. State the objectives of the Paris Agreement. (5)

The Paris Agreement (COP21, 2015, in force 2016) is a legally binding treaty under the UNFCCC. Its objectives (Article 2):

  1. Temperature: hold warming well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels and pursue efforts towards 1.5°C.
  2. Adaptation: increase ability to adapt and build resilience without threatening food production.
  3. Finance: make finance flows consistent with low-emission, climate-resilient development.

Means: NDCs from every country updated every five years; net zero in the second half of the century; climate finance, technology transfer and capacity building for developing countries; transparency framework; global stocktake every five years; Article 6 carbon markets. India: updated NDC — 45% intensity reduction and 50% non-fossil capacity by 2030; net zero 2070. Conclusion: Paris made climate action universal and created the legal hook for sustainable finance.

Q3. Write short notes on COP26, COP27 and COP28. (5)

COP26 (Glasgow, 2021): Glasgow Climate Pact to keep 1.5°C alive; phase-down of unabated coal; completion of the Paris Rulebook including Article 6; methane pledge; GFANZ. India announced Panchamrit and Net Zero 2070.

COP27 (Sharm el-Sheikh, 2022): climate justice and adaptation; agreement to create the Loss and Damage Fund for vulnerable countries; focus on food security, water and resilience.

COP28 (Dubai, 2023): first Global Stocktake; agreement on transitioning away from fossil fuels in energy systems; tripling renewable capacity and doubling energy-efficiency improvement by 2030; Loss and Damage Fund operationalised; more private-sector participation.

Significance for finance: each COP increased the demand for climate finance and disclosure.

Q4. Distinguish between scenario analysis and stress testing. (5)

Both are forward-looking climate-risk tools recommended by regulators (RBI draft guidance 2024, NGFS).

BasisScenario analysisStress testing
MeaningExplores the impact of several plausible future climate pathwaysMeasures losses under a severe but plausible shock
PurposeStrategic resilience and planningCapital and solvency adequacy
HorizonLong term (2030–2050)Shorter (1–5 years typically)
OutputQualitative and quantitative insightsLoss figures, capital shortfall
ExampleCompany under a high carbon tax or severe droughtBank's loan book under a flood or carbon-price shock

Conclusion: scenario analysis asks "what might happen?"; stress testing asks "can we survive the worst of it?"

Q5. Explain the PAT scheme. (5)

Meaning. Perform, Achieve and Trade is a market-based mechanism under the National Mission for Enhanced Energy Efficiency (NAPCC), administered by BEE under the Energy Conservation Act, 2001; first cycle 2012–15.

  1. Perform: designated consumers in energy-intensive sectors (steel, cement, aluminium, textiles, fertiliser, power, etc.) get specific energy consumption reduction targets.
  2. Achieve: units implement efficiency measures; performance is verified by accredited energy auditors.
  3. Trade: over-achievers receive Energy Saving Certificates (1 ESCert = 1 toe), traded on power exchanges; under-achievers buy ESCerts or pay penalties.

Illustration: target 0.76 toe/t, achieved 0.74, production 1,000,000 t → 20,000 ESCerts. Significance: achieves savings at least cost and is the forerunner of India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme.

Section B style (10 marks)

Q6. Discuss the impact of climate change on corporate financial performance. (10)

Introduction. Climate change increases operating costs, disrupts supply chains and reduces productivity, affecting profitability; it also raises compliance and insurance costs. The TCFD classifies climate risks into physical and transition risks, plus opportunities.
