1. Physical risks. Acute events (floods, cyclones, heatwaves) damage assets and halt production — e.g. the 2015 Chennai floods; chronic shifts (heat, water scarcity, sea-level rise) reduce labour productivity and capacity utilisation. Effects: impairment, lost revenue, higher insurance.

2. Transition risks. Policy and legal (carbon pricing under CCTS, EU CBAM from 2026, stricter norms); technology (renewables and EVs making fossil assets obsolete — stranded assets); market (customer preference for low-carbon products, commodity price shifts); reputation (boycotts, divestment).

3. Liability risk. Litigation and compensation orders (NGT "polluter pays").

4. Impact on financial statements. Income statement (revenue, cost), balance sheet (asset write-downs, provisions), cash flows (capex), cost of capital (risk premium or greenium) and valuation.

5. Opportunities. Resource efficiency, renewable energy savings, new products (EVs, green steel), access to green finance, resilience.

Illustration. A cement company emitting 3 Mt with 2.4 Mt free allowances at ₹2,000/t carbon price faces ₹120 crore cost — 8% of its ₹1,500 crore EBITDA (4% if half is passed on).

Conclusion. Climate change is now a financial issue. Firms that measure, disclose and adapt (e.g. Tiruppur textile units) protect margins and gain access to cheaper capital.

Q7. Explain the regulations set by the Government of India to mitigate climate risk. (10)

Introduction. India combines laws, missions, market mechanisms and disclosure rules to meet its NDC and Net Zero 2070 goals.

  1. NAPCC (2008): eight missions — Solar, Enhanced Energy Efficiency, Sustainable Habitat, Water, Himalayan Ecosystem, Green India, Sustainable Agriculture, Strategic Knowledge.
  2. Environment (Protection) Act 1986: umbrella law; emission standards, clearances, pollution-control equipment; basis of the Green Credit Rules 2023.
  3. NGT Act 2010: fast environmental justice; polluter-pays compensation.
  4. Energy Conservation Act 2001 (amended 2022): BEE, designated consumers, enables carbon market.
  5. PAT (2012): tradable ESCerts for energy-efficiency over-achievement.
  6. CCTS (2023): Indian Carbon Market with emission-intensity targets and Carbon Credit Certificates, plus offset mechanism.
  7. National Green Hydrogen Mission (2023): 5 MMT per annum by 2030; decarbonise steel, fertilisers, transport.
  8. National Solar Mission: solar parks and rooftops; PM-KUSUM; PM Surya Ghar.
  9. FAME I and II / PM E-DRIVE: EV subsidies and charging infrastructure.
  10. SEBI BRSR: ESG disclosure by top 1,000 listed companies; RBI green deposits and climate-risk disclosure/stress-test guidance.

Evaluation. Strengths: comprehensive mix of regulation and markets. Challenges: enforcement capacity, coal dependence, carbon price discovery, finance gap.

Conclusion. Together these create both compliance obligations and financial incentives, pushing capital towards low-carbon activities.

Q8. How do climate change and ESG influence managerial decisions? (10)

Introduction. Climate awareness plus ESG integration leads to better decisions and sustainable growth (slide 35). Managers now factor climate and ESG into every functional decision.

  1. Investment: internal carbon price in NPV — e.g. a boiler choice flips from coal (PV ₹28.43 crore) to biomass once a ₹1,500/t carbon price is included (₹46.87 vs ₹35.51 crore).
  2. Financing: green bonds, SLBs, green loans at lower cost; covenants tied to ESG KPIs.
  3. Operations: energy efficiency, renewable sourcing, water recycling.
  4. Supply chain: supplier ESG audits; Scope 3 targets; diversification.
  5. Products and markets: low-carbon products; exports affected by CBAM.
  6. Risk management: scenario analysis, insurance, ERM integration.
  7. HR: ESG-linked pay, safety, diversity; attracting talent.
  8. Governance and reporting: board ESG committees, BRSR, ratings management.

Impact on corporations: better reputation, efficiency, lower risk, competitive advantage; but capex and data costs.

Conclusion. ESG has moved from a reporting exercise to a core input into strategy and capital allocation.

Section C style (15-mark case)

Case 2. Coastal Cement Ltd under a carbon market and flood risk (15)

Case: Coastal Cement Ltd (EBITDA ₹1,500 crore) emits 3.1 Mt CO2e a year. Under the CCTS its intensity target is 0.60 tCO2e/t cement; it currently achieves 0.62 on 5 Mt of output. One plant (worth ₹800 crore) is in a flood plain where flood probability may rise from 2% to 5% with damage rising from 25% to 35%. Its bank has asked for a climate stress test. (a) Identify climate risks (4). (b) Quantify CCTS obligation at ₹1,500/CCC and flood EAL (6). (c) Recommend strategy (5).

(a) Risks. Transition – policy (CCTS targets, possible CBAM on exports), technology (low-clinker cements, CCUS), market (green-cement demand), reputation. Physical – acute flood risk; chronic heat and water stress. Liability – dust/pollution litigation. Financial transmission: credit risk for its bank (higher PD/LGD).

(b) Quantification. CCTS shortfall = (0.60 − 0.62) × 5,000,000 = −100,000 CCCs; cost = 100,000 × ₹1,500 = ₹15 crore (1.0% of EBITDA). Flood expected annual loss: now 0.02 × 0.25 × 800 = ₹4 crore; under high warming 0.05 × 0.35 × 800 = ₹14 crore.

(c) Strategy. (1) Reduce clinker factor (blended cements with fly ash/slag), waste-heat recovery, alternative fuels, renewable power — turn the shortfall into surplus credits; (2) use an internal carbon price for capex; (3) flood defences, insurance, relocate critical equipment; (4) finance via a sustainability-linked loan/bond with KPI = tCO2/t cement (ICMA SLBP); (5) disclose under BRSR Core and TCFD pillars; (6) scenario analysis using NGFS orderly vs disorderly pathways; (7) board-level climate committee.

Conclusion. A modest ₹15 crore carbon cost today and a rising physical loss are early warnings; acting now converts risk into credit revenue and lower financing cost.

Unit 3 — Sustainable Finance Products, Instruments and Emerging Trends

Syllabus: overview of sustainable finance strategies and products; pillars of sustainable finance; green bonds, social bonds, sustainability-linked bonds; impact investing and microfinance; climate finance: carbon trading and green loans; performance-based instruments; introduction to impact investing; role of AI in SF; applications of ML in ESG analysis; green loans and green microfinance — fundamentals and mechanism, benefits and valuations, principles, assessment of green microfinance. (10 teaching hours; CO4.)

3.1 Overview of sustainable finance strategies

Imagine choosing a cricket team. You can refuse certain players (exclusion), pick the best in each position (best-in-class), pick anyone but study their fitness carefully (integration), build a team around a theme (all-rounders), pick players who will help young talent (impact), or coach the players you already have (engagement). Sustainable investing strategies are the same menu.

Your slides (Unit 3, slides 2–4) list eight strategies:

  1. ESG Integration — integrates ESG factors into investment and financing decisions; identifies sustainability risks and opportunities while improving long-term financial performance.
  2. Responsible Investing — invests in companies following ethical, socially responsible and sustainable practices; avoids industries with harmful environmental or social impacts.
  3. Impact Investing — seeks measurable social and environmental benefits along with financial returns (healthcare, education, renewable energy, affordable housing).
  4. Green Finance — funding for projects that protect the environment: renewable energy, energy efficiency, pollution control, green infrastructure.
  5. Climate Finance — finances projects that reduce GHG emissions and strengthen climate resilience; supports mitigation, adaptation and transition.
  6. Sustainable Lending — loans based on the borrower's ESG performance and sustainability goals.
  7. Net-Zero Investment Strategy — aligns portfolios with net-zero GHG emissions; invests in clean technology and transitioning businesses.
  8. Sustainable Portfolio Management — diversified portfolios balancing financial return with ESG performance, with continuous monitoring of sustainability risk.

The industry classification (GSIA's seven strategies) — use in long answers

StrategyHow it worksExamplePros / cons
1. Negative / exclusionary screeningExclude sectors or firms (tobacco, weapons, thermal coal, gambling)Many ESG funds exclude ITC for tobaccoSimple, values-aligned / reduces diversification, no improvement pressure
2. Positive / best-in-class screeningInvest in ESG leaders within each sectorPick the lowest-emission steel makerKeeps sector balance / "least bad" may still be harmful
3. Norms-based screeningScreen against international norms (UN Global Compact, ILO, OECD)Norway's pension fund excluded Vedanta Resources (2007)Objective benchmark / reactive
4. ESG integrationSystematically include ESG in financial analysis (valuation, credit)Adjust discount rate for carbon riskMainstream, return-focused / hard to verify
5. Sustainability-themed investingInvest in themes: clean energy, water, EVsClean-energy thematic fundsClear story / concentration risk
6. Impact / community investingTargeted investments for measurable positive impact with returnAavishkaar, microfinance fundsReal outcomes / smaller scale, measurement cost
7. Corporate engagement and shareholder action (stewardship)Use voting and dialogue to improve company behaviourInvestors pressing boards for net-zero targetsDrives change from within / slow, needs large stake

Your slides mix strategies (integration, impact) with types of finance (green finance, climate finance, lending). In an exam, list the slide's eight and, for a 10-mark answer, add the GSIA seven as the industry classification. SEBI's ESG fund categories (2023) use similar labels: exclusion, integration, best-in-class and positive screening, impact investing, sustainable objectives, transition-related investments.

Strategy choice determines what a "sustainable" portfolio really holds; two funds both labelled ESG can hold very different stocks.

3.2 Sustainable finance products: the landscape

Slide 6–7 product table:

ProductPurposeFeatureBenefitExample
Green bondsFinance green projectsFunds renewable energy and eco-friendly projects (solar panels, wind turbines, green buildings)Reduces carbon emissionsWorld Bank Green Bond, SBI Green Bond
Social bondsFinance social projectsSupports healthcare, education, housing, community developmentImproves social welfareAfrican Development Bank Social Bond
Sustainability-linked bondsAchieve ESG targetsInterest rate linked to ESG performanceEncourages sustainability improvementsEnel SLB, Suzano SLB

Slide 7 lists "wind farm, solar park, electric grid, renewable energy plant" under the Enel SLB. Strictly, an SLB is general-purpose: its proceeds are not tied to specific projects. Enel's KPI related to its share of renewable capacity, so the target is about renewables, but the money could be used for anything. State this nuance in answers.

The two families of labelled debt

FamilyWhat is "labelled"Instruments
Use-of-proceedsThe money: proceeds must go to eligible projectsGreen bonds, social bonds, sustainability bonds, blue bonds, green loans, green deposits
Performance-based / KPI-linkedThe issuer's performance: financial terms change with KPI achievementSustainability-linked bonds (SLBs), sustainability-linked loans (SLLs)

Together these are called GSS+ (green, social, sustainability and sustainability-linked) or labelled bonds. Beyond debt: ESG equity funds, impact funds, carbon credits, microfinance, blended finance, insurance products.

3.3 Green bonds

A green bond is an ordinary bond with a promise stapled to it: "every rupee you lend me will go only to green projects, and I will show you the receipts and the results." The investor's credit risk is the same as any bond of that issuer; what changes is where the money goes and how transparently it is reported.

A green bond is any type of bond instrument where the proceeds (or an equivalent amount) will be exclusively applied to finance or refinance, in part or in full, new and/or existing eligible green projects, and which is aligned with the four core components of the ICMA Green Bond Principles. (ICMA definition, paraphrased.)

ICMA Green Bond Principles (GBP) — four core components + two key recommendations

Core componentRequirement
1. Use of proceedsProceeds used for eligible green projects with clear environmental benefits, which the issuer should assess and where feasible quantify. Share of refinancing should be disclosed.
2. Process for project evaluation and selectionIssuer communicates its environmental objectives, the process to decide eligibility, and how it identifies and manages environmental and social risks of projects.
3. Management of proceedsNet proceeds credited to a sub-account or otherwise tracked; balance of unallocated proceeds disclosed, with intended temporary placement (e.g. cash, government securities).
4. ReportingAnnual reporting until full allocation (and on material developments): list of projects, amounts allocated, expected impact. Quantitative impact indicators recommended.
Key recommendation A: Green Bond FrameworkA document explaining alignment with the four components and the issuer's overall sustainability strategy.
Key recommendation B: External reviewPre-issuance review (e.g. second-party opinion) and post-issuance verification of allocation by an external auditor or verifier.

Mnemonic for the four components: "U-P-M-R" — Use, Process, Manage, Report ("Up My Report").

Eligible green project categories (GBP, 10 categories)

  1. Renewable energy (solar, wind, small hydro, geothermal)
  2. Energy efficiency (efficient buildings, grids, storage, district heating)
  3. Pollution prevention and control (emission reduction, waste recycling)
  4. Environmentally sustainable management of living natural resources and land use (sustainable agriculture, forestry)
  5. Terrestrial and aquatic biodiversity conservation
  6. Clean transportation (EVs, metro rail, charging infrastructure)
  7. Sustainable water and wastewater management
  8. Climate change adaptation (flood defences, early-warning systems)
  9. Circular economy adapted products, production technologies and processes
  10. Green buildings (certified to recognised standards, e.g. LEED, IGBC, GRIHA)

Types of green bonds (ICMA)

TypeRecourseExample
Standard green use-of-proceeds bondFull recourse to the issuer (same credit as its other bonds)SBI, IREDA green bonds
Green revenue bondRecourse only to the revenue streams/fees of the projectMunicipal water-utility bond repaid from water charges
Green project bondRecourse only to the project's assets and cash flowsSpecial-purpose vehicle for a solar park
Secured green bond (incl. green securitised and covered bonds)Backed by a pool of green assets (e.g. solar loans, green mortgages)Securitisation of rooftop-solar loans

Other variants: sovereign green bonds (issued by governments), green municipal bonds, green masala bonds (rupee-denominated, issued offshore), certified climate bonds (Climate Bonds Standard).

Green bond vs conventional bond

BasisConventional bondGreen bond
Use of proceedsGeneral corporate purposesOnly eligible green projects (or equivalent amount)
Credit riskIssuer's creditSame (for standard green bond)
DisclosureFinancialFinancial + allocation and impact reports
External reviewCredit rating onlyCredit rating + SPO/verification/certification
Investor baseGeneralGeneral + dedicated ESG/green mandates
CostMarket yieldMarket yield, sometimes slightly lower ("greenium"), plus extra reporting costs

Benefits and limitations

Issuers: diversified and larger investor base; possible greenium; reputation and signalling; internal alignment of treasury and sustainability teams; longer tenor in some markets.
Investors: same risk-return with transparency on impact; meet ESG mandates; engagement.

Limitations: greenwashing risk (weak eligibility, refinancing of old assets); additional costs (review, reporting); no penalty if impact is not achieved (in most frameworks); lack of a single definition/taxonomy; small greenium; "use of proceeds" does not change the issuer's overall carbon profile.

Indian green bond milestones: Yes Bank's ₹1,000 crore green infrastructure bond (2015) — India's first; Exim Bank's US$500 million green bond (2015); NTPC's green masala bond (2016); SBI's US$650 million certified climate bond (2018); IREDA and power-sector PSUs; corporate issuers such as ReNew and Greenko (offshore). Sovereign Green Bonds: Union Budget 2022-23 announcement; framework November 2022; first issues January and February 2023 (₹8,000 crore each, ₹16,000 crore total), with a small greenium of a few basis points versus comparable G-Secs; proceeds for solar, wind, metro rail, electric buses, afforestation and similar public projects, excluding fossil-fuel, nuclear and large hydro (above 25 MW) projects.

International: EIB Climate Awareness Bond (2007) and World Bank green bond (2008) — the pioneers; Apple, Alphabet and others issued large corporate green bonds; the EU's NextGenerationEU green bond programme; the EU Green Bond Standard (voluntary "EuGB" label, applicable from December 2024).

Allocation % = Proceeds allocated to eligible projects ÷ Net proceeds × 100
Refinancing share = Proceeds allocated to existing (refinanced) projects ÷ Total allocated × 100
Avoided emissions (tCO2/yr) = Capacity (MW) × CUF × 8,760 h × Grid EF (tCO2/MWh)
Greenium (bps) = Yieldconventional − Yieldgreen;   Annual saving = Issue size × Greenium

CUF = capacity utilisation factor (share of the year a plant effectively produces at full capacity); 8,760 = hours in a year.

Worked example 3.1 — allocation report. An NBFC issued a ₹1,000 crore green bond. Within the year it allocated ₹450 crore to solar projects, ₹250 crore to electric buses and ₹150 crore to certified green buildings. Of the allocated money, ₹300 crore refinanced loans made before issuance. Compute allocation %, unallocated balance and refinancing share.

  1. Allocated = 450 + 250 + 150 = ₹850 crore.
  2. Allocation % = 850 ÷ 1,000 = 85%.
  3. Unallocated = 1,000 − 850 = ₹150 crore, to be held in cash/G-Secs as per the framework and disclosed.
  4. Refinancing share = 300 ÷ 850 = 35.3%.

A high refinancing share is legal but must be disclosed; investors prefer new ("additional") projects.

Worked example 3.2 — impact report. The ₹450 crore solar allocation finances 300 MW at a CUF of 20%. Grid EF = 0.71 tCO2/MWh. Estimate annual generation, avoided emissions and impact per ₹ crore.

  1. Generation = 300 × 0.20 × 8,760 = 525,600 MWh/year.
  2. Avoided emissions = 525,600 × 0.71 = 373,176 tCO2/year.
  3. Per ₹ crore = 373,176 ÷ 450 = 829.3 tCO2 per ₹ crore per year.

Worked example 3.3 — greenium. A sovereign green bond is auctioned at a yield of 7.10% when the comparable conventional G-Sec yields 7.16%. Issue size ₹8,000 crore. Greenium and annual interest saving?

  1. Greenium = 7.16% − 7.10% = 0.06% = 6 basis points.
  2. Annual saving = 8,000 × 0.0006 = ₹4.8 crore.
  3. Reverse view: a 7.10% coupon, 10-year bond priced at the conventional 7.16% yield would be worth ₹99.58 per ₹100; investors paying ₹100 accept 6 bps less yield for the green label.
Q1. Name the four core components of the GBP.

Use of proceeds; process for project evaluation and selection; management of proceeds; reporting.

Q2. Does a green bond have lower credit risk than the same issuer's ordinary bond?

No. A standard green use-of-proceeds bond has full recourse to the issuer and ranks equally with its other debt; credit risk is identical.

Q3. What is a green revenue bond?

A non-recourse-to-issuer bond whose repayment comes from pledged revenues (fees, taxes) of the green project, e.g. water charges.

3.4 Social bonds and sustainability bonds

A social bond is a use-of-proceeds bond whose proceeds are exclusively applied to finance or refinance eligible social projects that address or mitigate a specific social issue and/or seek positive social outcomes, especially but not exclusively for a target population, aligned with ICMA's Social Bond Principles (same four core components as the GBP).

A sustainability bond finances a combination of green and social projects (ICMA Sustainability Bond Guidelines).

Eligible social project categories (SBP)

  • Affordable basic infrastructure (clean drinking water, sanitation, transport, energy)
  • Access to essential services (health, education, financial services)
  • Affordable housing
  • Employment generation, including through SME financing and microfinance
  • Food security and sustainable food systems
  • Socioeconomic advancement and empowerment (e.g. gender equality)

Target populations: people below the poverty line, excluded or marginalised communities, persons with disabilities, migrants, the under-educated, underserved (lacking access to essential services), unemployed, women and gender minorities, ageing and vulnerable youth, people affected by natural disasters.

Slide example: African Development Bank social bonds (hospitals, schools, affordable housing, community development). Social bond issuance surged globally in 2020 for COVID-19 relief (e.g. the EU's SURE programme social bonds, and development bank pandemic bonds). In India, affordable-housing lenders and microfinance lenders have raised social loans and bonds from international investors; the Social Stock Exchange is a related platform for non-profits.

Worked example 3.4. A housing finance company issues a ₹750 crore sustainability bond, allocating 60% to green buildings and 40% to affordable housing loans averaging ₹12 lakh. (a) Split? (b) Number of affordable homes financed?

  1. Green = 0.60 × 750 = ₹450 crore; Social = 0.40 × 750 = ₹300 crore.
  2. Homes = ₹300 crore ÷ ₹12 lakh = 3,000,000,000 ÷ 1,200,000 = 2,500 homes.

3.5 Sustainability-linked bonds (SLBs) — performance-based instruments

A green bond says "I will spend your money on green things." An SLB says "Spend the money on anything, but I promise to hit a sustainability target by a certain date — and if I miss it, I will pay you extra interest." It is a bet on the company's overall behaviour, with a financial penalty for failure.

A sustainability-linked bond is any type of bond instrument for which the financial and/or structural characteristics (typically the coupon) can vary depending on whether the issuer achieves predefined sustainability or ESG objectives, measured through Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) against Sustainability Performance Targets (SPTs) within a predefined timeline. Proceeds are for general purposes. (ICMA Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles, June 2020, paraphrased.)

The five core components of the SLBP

  1. Selection of KPIs: relevant, core and material to the issuer's business; measurable/quantifiable on a consistent basis; externally verifiable; benchmarkable. Example: Scope 1+2 emission intensity, % renewable energy, water intensity, % women in management.
  2. Calibration of SPTs: ambitious — a material improvement beyond "business as usual"; compared to a baseline, peers and science (e.g. SBTi, Paris-aligned); set with a timeline and trigger (observation) date.
  3. Bond characteristics: the financial/structural change (coupon step-up is most common, e.g. +25 bps; also step-down, premium payment at maturity, change in redemption price).
  4. Reporting: at least annually — KPI performance, verification report, relevant information.
  5. Verification: independent external verification of KPI performance against each SPT at least annually and for any date relevant to a coupon change (mandatory).
Extra interest from step-up = Face value × Step-up (in decimal) × Number of years the step-up applies
Bond price = Σ Ct / (1 + y)t + F / (1 + y)n
Expected price = p × Priceif missed + (1 − p) × Priceif met

Ct = coupon in year t (changes after a missed SPT); y = market yield; F = face value; n = years; p = probability the SPT is missed.

Worked example 3.5 — cost of missing the target (issuer view). A steel company issues a ₹500 crore, 5-year SLB at 7.50%. If its emission-intensity SPT is missed at the end of year 3, the coupon steps up by 25 bps for years 4 and 5. Compute annual coupon before and after, and the total extra cost.

  1. Normal annual interest = 500 × 7.50% = ₹37.50 crore.
  2. Stepped-up coupon = 7.75% → 500 × 7.75% = ₹38.75 crore.
  3. Extra cost = 500 × 0.0025 × 2 years = ₹2.5 crore.

The penalty (₹2.5 crore) is small relative to the bond; critics argue step-ups of 25 bps are too weak to change behaviour.

Further worked examples on SLB pricing, expected value and investor return are in Unit 5 topic 3 (SLB pricing).

Enel (Italy) issued the first SDG-linked (sustainability-linked) bond in 2019 — US$1.5 billion, with a 25 bps step-up if its renewable share of installed capacity did not reach the target by end-2021. Suzano (Brazil, pulp and paper) issued an SLB in 2020 linked to GHG-intensity reduction. In India, UltraTech Cement issued India's first SLB (US$400 million, 2021) linked to emission-intensity reduction, and JSW Steel issued a US$500 million SLB (2021) linked to reducing CO2 emission intensity at its Vijayanagar works by 2030.

Q1. What are the five SLBP components?

KPI selection, SPT calibration, bond characteristics, reporting, verification.

Q2. Are SLB proceeds ring-fenced for green projects?

No, they are general-purpose; the sustainability commitment is at issuer level through KPIs/SPTs.

3.6 Other labelled instruments: transition, blue, yellow, orange, sovereign, green deposits

InstrumentMeaningExample / note
Transition bondFinances the decarbonisation of high-emitting ("hard-to-abate") sectors that may not qualify as green; guided by ICMA's Climate Transition Finance Handbook (2020) — issuer's transition strategy, materiality, science-based targets, transparencyJapan's Climate Transition Bonds (2024); SEBI's 2023 framework includes transition bonds with a transition plan disclosure
Blue bondUse-of-proceeds bond for ocean and water projects (sustainable fisheries, marine protection, water management)Seychelles' sovereign blue bond (2018); SEBI recognises blue bonds (2023)
Yellow bondSEBI sub-category for solar-energy projectsIndia-specific label (2023)
Orange / gender bondFinances gender-equality outcomesEmerging label in Asia
Sovereign green bondGovernment green bond financing public green expenditureIndia since 2023
Green depositsBank deposits whose funds are allocated to green lendingRBI framework, 2023
Catastrophe (cat) bondInsurance-linked bond: investors lose principal if a defined disaster occurs; transfers physical climate riskWorld Bank-issued cat bonds for disaster-prone countries
Social/development impact bondOutcome-based contract: investors fund a programme and are repaid by an outcome funder only if agreed results are achieved (not really a bond)Educate Girls Development Impact Bond, Rajasthan (2015–18), one of the first DIBs
Digital / tokenised green bondGreen bond issued and settled on a distributed ledgerHong Kong's tokenised green bond (2023); see Unit 5 topic 11

3.7 Impact investing

An ESG investor asks "Is this company well-behaved?" An impact investor asks "Did my money make something good happen that would not have happened otherwise — and can I prove it?" It is the difference between avoiding harm and deliberately creating benefit.

Impact investments are investments made with the intention to generate positive, measurable social and environmental impact alongside a financial return. (Global Impact Investing Network, GIIN.) Slide 2: impact investing "seeks measurable social and environmental benefits along with financial returns" in healthcare, education, renewable energy and affordable housing.

Core characteristics

  1. Intentionality — the investor deliberately seeks positive impact (not a side-effect).
  2. Financial return expectation — at least return of capital; distinguishes it from philanthropy.
  3. Range of return expectations and asset classes — from below-market (concessionary, "impact-first") to market-rate ("finance-first"), across private equity, debt, real assets, and listed markets.
  4. Impact measurement and management — commitment to measure and report impact.
  5. Additionality / contribution — the investor's capital makes a difference that would not happen otherwise.

Impact measurement and management (IMM)

  • Theory of change: Inputs (money, staff) → Activities (lend to women, install solar) → Outputs (loans disbursed, systems installed) → Outcomes (higher income, less kerosene) → Impact (poverty reduction, lower emissions). Outcomes and impact matter, not just outputs.
  • IRIS+ (GIIN): a free catalogue of standard impact metrics (e.g. "number of clients", "tCO2e avoided").
  • Five dimensions of impact (Impact Management Project): What outcome, Who experiences it, How much (scale, depth, duration), Contribution (what would have happened anyway), Risk that impact differs from expectation.
  • SROI (Social Return on Investment): monetised social value ÷ investment.
  • Operating Principles for Impact Management (IFC, 2019): nine principles on strategy, origination, portfolio management, exit and independent verification.
SROI ratio = Present value of social benefits ÷ Value of investment

Social benefits are monetised outcomes (e.g. extra income, health-cost savings), discounted; adjust for deadweight (what would have happened anyway) and attribution.

Worked example 3.6 — SROI. A ₹50 lakh skills programme produces monetised social benefits of ₹20 lakh, ₹25 lakh and ₹30 lakh in years 1–3. Discount rate 8%. Find SROI.

  1. PV = 20/1.08 + 25/1.082 + 30/1.083 = 18.52 + 21.43 + 23.82 = ₹63.77 lakh.
  2. SROI = 63.77 ÷ 50 = 1.28 : 1 (₹1.28 of social value per rupee).

India has one of the most active impact-investing markets among emerging economies: Aavishkaar (pioneer rural-focused impact fund, early 2000s), Lok Capital, Omidyar Network India, Acumen, and development finance institutions such as SIDBI. Microfinance (e.g. early equity in Ujjivan, Equitas) is a classic impact-investing success. SEBI's Social Stock Exchange framework (2022) created a segment on NSE and BSE where non-profits can raise funds through Zero Coupon Zero Principal (ZCZP) instruments and must publish annual impact reports with social audit.

BasisESG investingImpact investingPhilanthropy
Primary goalRisk-adjusted return, avoid ESG riskMeasurable positive impact + returnImpact only
ReturnMarket-rateBelow-market to market-rateNone (capital given away)
MeasurementESG scoresOutcome/impact metrics (IRIS+)Programme evaluation
IntentionalityNot requiredEssentialEssential
AssetsMostly listedMostly private (PE, debt)Grants

3.8 Microfinance

A vegetable vendor needs ₹10,000 to buy stock but has no collateral, no credit history and lives far from a bank branch. Microfinance lends her small sums without collateral, often through a group of women who guarantee each other ("social collateral"), and collects small weekly instalments at her doorstep.

Microfinance is the provision of financial services — small collateral-free loans, savings, insurance, remittances and pensions — to low-income households and micro-entrepreneurs who lack access to formal banking. Under the RBI's regulatory framework for microfinance loans (effective 1 April 2022), a microfinance loan is a collateral-free loan to a household with annual income up to ₹3,00,000, and a household's total loan repayment obligations are capped at 50% of its monthly household income.

Delivery models

ModelHow it worksIndian example
SHG–Bank Linkage ProgrammeSelf-help groups of 10–20 women save together, lend internally, and then borrow from banksNABARD's programme (since 1992); DAY-NRLM
Joint Liability Group (JLG)Small groups (typically 4–10) jointly guarantee each member's loanMost NBFC-MFIs; Grameen-style
Grameen modelGroups of five, weekly centre meetings, women focusMuhammad Yunus's Grameen Bank, Bangladesh (Nobel Peace Prize 2006)
Individual lendingLarger loans to graduated clients using cash-flow assessmentSmall finance banks
Institutional formsNBFC-MFIs (at least 75% of assets in microfinance loans), small finance banks, banks, cooperatives, Section 8 companiesBandhan (became a bank in 2015), Ujjivan SFB, Equitas SFB, CreditAccess Grameen

Features, benefits and criticisms

Features: small ticket; no collateral; group guarantee; frequent small repayments; doorstep service; women focus; high repayment rates.

Benefits: financial inclusion (SDG 1, 5, 8); women's empowerment; self-employment; consumption smoothing; reduces dependence on moneylenders.

Criticisms: high interest rates (operating costs of small loans); over-indebtedness and multiple borrowing; coercive recovery (the 2010 Andhra Pradesh crisis led to a state ordinance and a sector collapse there); mixed evidence of poverty reduction in randomised studies; mission drift towards profit.

Regulatory response: RBI's 2022 framework — household-income-based assessment, 50% repayment cap, board-approved pricing policy, transparent disclosure (standardised factsheet), no prepayment penalty, fair recovery practices.

EMI (reducing balance) = L × r ÷ [1 − (1 + r)−n]
Flat-rate instalment = L × (1 + R × T) ÷ n
Max new EMI = 50% × Monthly household income − Existing EMIs

L = loan; r = periodic (monthly) rate; n = number of instalments; R = annual flat rate; T = years. A flat rate hides the true cost because interest is charged on the original amount even as principal is repaid.

Worked example 3.7 — EMI and total interest. A ₹50,000 microfinance loan at 24% p.a. reducing balance for 12 months. Find EMI and total interest.

  1. r = 24% ÷ 12 = 2% = 0.02; n = 12.
  2. EMI = 50,000 × 0.02 ÷ [1 − 1.02−12] = 1,000 ÷ 0.211506 = ₹4,727.98.
  3. Total paid = 4,727.98 × 12 = ₹56,735.76; interest = ₹6,735.76.

Worked example 3.8 — flat rate trap (reverse problem). Another lender advertises "only 12% flat" on ₹50,000 for 12 months. Find the instalment and the true annual rate.

  1. Total repayable = 50,000 × (1 + 0.12 × 1) = ₹56,000; monthly instalment = 56,000 ÷ 12 = ₹4,666.67.
  2. Solve for r in 50,000 = 4,666.67 × [1 − (1 + r)−12] ÷ r (Excel: =RATE(12,-4666.67,50000)) → r = 1.788% per month.
  3. Annualised = 1.788% × 12 = 21.46% p.a. nominal (effective annual rate = 1.0178812 − 1 = 23.70%).

"12% flat" is really about 21.5% on a reducing basis — which is why RBI requires disclosure of the effective annualised rate.

Worked example 3.9 — RBI 50% cap. A household earns ₹20,000 per month and already pays EMIs of ₹6,000. Can it take the ₹50,000 loan above (EMI ₹4,728)? If not, what is the maximum loan at 2% monthly for 12 months?

  1. Cap = 50% × 20,000 = ₹10,000; room = 10,000 − 6,000 = ₹4,000.
  2. EMI of ₹4,728 exceeds ₹4,000 → not permitted.
  3. Max loan = 4,000 × [1 − 1.02−12] ÷ 0.02 = ₹42,301.

3.9 Climate finance: carbon trading and carbon markets

Pollution is "free" for the polluter but costly for society. A carbon market puts a price tag on each tonne of CO2. Firms that can cut emissions cheaply cut more and sell their spare permits; firms for which cutting is expensive buy permits. The total cut is achieved at the lowest total cost.

Climate finance is local, national or transnational financing — from public, private and alternative sources — that supports mitigation and adaptation actions addressing climate change (UNFCCC). Carbon trading is a market-based mechanism in which rights to emit (allowances) or certified emission reductions (credits), each usually equal to one tonne of CO2-equivalent, are bought and sold, creating a price for carbon.

Two market designs

BasisCap-and-trade (allowance market)Baseline-and-credit (credit market)
HowGovernment caps total emissions and issues allowances (free or auctioned); firms must surrender allowances equal to emissionsProjects/firms that reduce emissions below a baseline or target earn credits
UnitAllowance (e.g. EU Allowance, EUA)Credit (CER, VCU, CCC, ESCert-type)
ExamplesEU ETS (2005, the first large one), China's national ETS (2021), California, KoreaCDM, Verra/Gold Standard projects, India's CCTS (intensity-based), PAT

Compliance vs voluntary carbon markets

BasisCompliance marketVoluntary carbon market (VCM)
DriverLegal obligationVoluntary corporate targets (net zero, carbon neutral claims)
RegulatorGovernment (EU, BEE/CERC in India)Private standards: Verra (VCS), Gold Standard; integrity bodies ICVCM (Core Carbon Principles, 2023) and VCMI
ParticipantsObligated entitiesCompanies, individuals, airlines (CORSIA is a compliance-like scheme for aviation)
PriceGenerally higherWide range; depends on project type and quality
IndiaCCTS compliance mechanism, PATIndian project developers are major suppliers of voluntary credits (renewables, cookstoves); CCTS offset mechanism

Quality of a carbon credit

  • Additionality — the reduction would not have happened without carbon finance.
  • Permanence — the stored carbon will not be released (forest fires threaten forest credits).
  • No leakage — emissions do not simply move elsewhere.
  • No double counting — one tonne counted once (Article 6 "corresponding adjustments").
  • Robust MRV — monitoring, reporting and verification by accredited third parties.
  • Co-benefits — SDG benefits (jobs, health).

Avoidance credits (renewables, avoided deforestation) vs removal credits (afforestation, direct air capture). In 2023 media investigations questioned the integrity of some forest-protection (REDD+) credits, strengthening calls for ICVCM-labelled credits.

Carbon tax vs emissions trading

BasisCarbon taxEmissions trading
CertaintyPrice certain, quantity uncertainQuantity (cap) certain, price uncertain
AdministrationSimple (existing tax system)Needs registry, MRV, trading platform
IndiaThe former coal cess (clean energy cess, rising to ₹400/tonne of coal) acted like an implicit carbon tax until subsumed into GST compensation cess in 2017PAT, CCTS

Worked example 3.10 — why trading lowers cost. Firms A and B must each cut 100 t. A's abatement cost is ₹500/t; B's is ₹2,000/t. Allowances trade at ₹1,200/t.

  1. Without trading: A = 100 × 500 = ₹50,000; B = 100 × 2,000 = ₹2,00,000; total = ₹2,50,000.
  2. With trading: A cuts 200 t (cost 200 × 500 = ₹1,00,000) and sells 100 allowances to B for 100 × 1,200 = ₹1,20,000. A's net = 1,00,000 − 1,20,000 = −₹20,000 (a gain). B pays ₹1,20,000 instead of ₹2,00,000.
  3. Society's total abatement cost = ₹1,00,000 (only A's cuts), the same 200 t reduction at ₹1,50,000 less.

Worked example 3.11 — carbon credits make a project viable (NPV). A 10 MW solar plant costs ₹40 crore, runs at 19% CUF, sells power at ₹3.00/kWh, has O&M of ₹0.60 crore/year, life 25 years, discount rate 10%. Carbon credits: 1 credit per tCO2 avoided (grid EF 0.71), sold at ₹400.

  1. Generation = 10 × 0.19 × 8,760 = 16,644 MWh/year.
  2. Revenue = 16,644,000 kWh × ₹3 = ₹4.9932 crore; net cash flow = 4.9932 − 0.60 = ₹4.3932 crore.
  3. Annuity factor (10%, 25 years) = 9.0770.
  4. NPV without credits = 4.3932 × 9.0770 − 40 = −₹0.12 crore (reject).
  5. Credits = 16,644 × 0.71 = 11,817 t; revenue = 11,817 × 400 = ₹0.4727 crore/year.
  6. NPV with credits = (4.3932 + 0.4727) × 9.0770 − 40 = +₹4.17 crore (accept).

Carbon revenue turned a marginally negative project positive — the logic of CDM "additionality".

Worked example 3.12 — CDM credits. A 50 MW wind farm (CUF 20%) displaces grid power with a CDM baseline emission factor of 0.90 tCO2/MWh (baselines in the 2000s were higher than today's grid factor). 1 CER = 1 tCO2e. How many CERs does it earn per year? If CERs sell for ₹50 each, what is the annual carbon revenue?

  1. Generation = 50 × 0.20 × 8,760 = 87,600 MWh.
  2. CERs = 87,600 × 0.90 = 78,840 CERs/year.
  3. Revenue = 78,840 × ₹50 = ₹39,42,000 = ₹39.42 lakh/year (illustrative price).

India was among the largest CDM host countries, with many renewable-energy projects earning CERs in the 2000s. Today India is building its own compliance market (CCTS) and is a leading supplier of voluntary credits; under Article 6.2 India has listed activities (such as green hydrogen, offshore wind, energy storage) for which it may authorise credits for transfer abroad.

3.10 Green loans and sustainability-linked loans

A green loan is any type of loan instrument (term loan, revolving facility, project finance) made available exclusively to finance or refinance, in whole or in part, new and/or existing eligible green projects, aligned with the four core components of the Green Loan Principles (LMA, APLMA and LSTA, first issued 2018): use of proceeds, process for project evaluation and selection, management of proceeds, reporting — with recommendations for a green loan framework and external review.

A sustainability-linked loan (SLL) is a loan whose margin (interest rate) is adjusted according to the borrower's performance against predetermined sustainability performance targets, irrespective of use of proceeds (Sustainability-Linked Loan Principles, 2019: KPI selection, SPT calibration, loan characteristics, reporting, verification).

Mechanism of a green loan

  1. Borrower identifies eligible green project and prepares green loan framework.
  2. Lender (or its ESG team) reviews eligibility and E&S risks; optional external review.
  3. Loan sanctioned; proceeds disbursed to a designated account or tracked.
  4. Borrower reports annually on use and impact (e.g. kWh saved).
  5. Lender monitors covenants; may offer a pricing benefit.

Benefits and valuation

  • Borrower: access to dedicated green credit lines, sometimes lower pricing, reputational benefit, operating cost savings from the project itself.
  • Lender: portfolio decarbonisation, lower transition risk, meeting green deposit allocation needs, priority-sector eligibility for some renewable loans, new business.
  • Valuation: a green project is valued like any project (NPV/IRR) but includes (i) energy/resource savings, (ii) carbon revenues or avoided carbon costs, (iii) lower financing cost (greenium, margin ratchet), (iv) reduced risk (lower discount rate) — see worked example 3.11 and the SLL margin example below.

Worked example 3.13 — SLL margin ratchet. A ₹300 crore, 3-year SLL has a 10 bps margin reduction for each year the borrower meets its renewable-energy SPT (and a 10 bps increase if missed). Annual saving if met? Total over three years if met every year? Swing between best and worst case per year?

  1. Annual saving = 300 × 0.0010 = ₹0.30 crore.
  2. Three years = ₹0.90 crore.
  3. Swing per year (met vs missed) = 20 bps × 300 = ₹0.60 crore.
BasisGreen loanSustainability-linked loan
FocusUse of proceeds (specific green project)Borrower's overall sustainability performance
ProceedsRing-fenced/trackedGeneral corporate purposes
PricingUsually standard (sometimes concessional)Margin ratchet up/down with SPTs
PrinciplesGreen Loan Principles (4 components)SLL Principles (5 components)
Suited toFirms with identifiable green projectsFirms without enough green projects (e.g. hard-to-abate, services)

Indian banks (SBI, HDFC Bank, Axis Bank and others) offer green loans for renewable energy, EV and green-building projects; SIDBI runs energy-efficiency and cleaner-production lines for MSMEs, often co-funded by multilateral lenders. Several Indian corporates (in cement, renewable energy and IT services) have signed SLLs with KPIs such as emission intensity or renewable share. RBI's green deposits framework (2023) requires banks to deploy green deposit money into eligible green loans.

3.11 Green microfinance: fundamentals, mechanism, benefits and valuation, principles, assessment

A poor household spends on kerosene for lamps and firewood for cooking — costly, smoky and polluting. A solar lamp or clean cookstove would pay for itself, but the household cannot afford the upfront price. Green microfinance lends the price and lets the household repay from what it saves on fuel.

Green microfinance is the provision of microfinance services that (i) finance environmentally beneficial products and practices for low-income clients (renewable energy, energy efficiency, clean cooking, water and sanitation, climate-resilient agriculture), (ii) manage the environmental risks of the microfinance institution's (MFI's) loan portfolio, and (iii) reduce the MFI's own environmental footprint — thereby combining financial inclusion with environmental sustainability.

Fundamentals — three dimensions

  1. Green products (opportunities): loans for solar home systems, solar lanterns, solar pumps, biogas plants, improved cookstoves, water filters, rainwater harvesting, drip irrigation, organic farming, climate-resilient seeds, energy-efficient equipment for micro-enterprises.
  2. Environmental risk management: exclusion lists (no loans for illegal logging, wildlife trade, hazardous chemicals), screening of client activities (tanneries, brick kilns), advice on safer practices.
  3. Institutional greening: the MFI reduces its own energy and paper use.

Mechanism

  1. Partnership: MFI partners with a product supplier/energy enterprise (e.g. SELCO India, founded 1995, pioneered solar lighting for poor households with bank and MFI financing), which provides products, installation and after-sales service.
  2. Awareness and demand creation at group meetings; product demonstration.
  3. Credit appraisal including household cash-flow and fuel savings.
  4. Loan disbursement (often directly to the supplier) and delivery of the product.
  5. Repayment from savings on kerosene/diesel/firewood and from additional income (longer working hours, phone charging).
  6. Monitoring product use and maintenance; collecting environmental and social data (tCO2 avoided, households reached).
  7. Refinancing of the MFI's green portfolio by green bonds, DFIs, climate funds or green deposits.

Benefits

  • Clients: lower energy costs; health (no indoor smoke); safety (no kerosene fires); study hours for children; income from extra working hours; climate resilience (drip irrigation).
  • Environment: lower emissions and deforestation; less indoor and outdoor pollution.
  • MFI: product diversification; client loyalty; access to green refinancing; better portfolio resilience to climate shocks.
  • SDGs: 1, 3, 5, 7, 13.

Valuation (household and MFI level)

Simple payback (months) = Product cost ÷ Monthly savings
NPV (household) = Σ Savingst / (1 + r)t − Cost

At the MFI level: portfolio yield, portfolio at risk (PAR 30), operating cost and the environmental impact per rupee lent.

Worked example 3.14 — valuing a solar home system loan. A solar home system costs ₹12,000, financed over 12 months at 2% per month. It saves ₹900/month of kerosene and earns ₹150/month from mobile charging (total ₹1,050). Life 5 years (60 months). Kerosene use avoided: 5 litres/month at an illustrative 2.5 kg CO2/litre.

  1. EMI = 12,000 × 0.02 ÷ [1 − 1.02−12] = ₹1,134.72.
  2. Net monthly cost during loan = 1,134.72 − 1,050 = ₹84.72 — nearly self-financing.
  3. Simple payback = 12,000 ÷ 1,050 = 11.4 months.
  4. NPV of savings over 60 months at 2%/month = 1,050 × [1 − 1.02−60] ÷ 0.02 = ₹36,498.93; NPV = 36,498.93 − 12,000 = ₹24,498.93.
  5. CO2 avoided = 5 × 12 × 2.5 = 150 kg/year per household (7.5 t per year for 50 households).

Principles of green microfinance

  1. Client protection first — affordability, transparent pricing, no over-indebtedness (RBI 50% cap), fair treatment (Client Protection Principles).
  2. Product quality and after-sales service — products must work, or clients default.
  3. Appropriate loan design — tenor matched to savings; flexible repayment for seasonal income.
  4. Environmental risk management — exclusion list and screening.
  5. Measurable outcomes — track environmental and social indicators.
  6. Partnerships — suppliers, NGOs, DFIs, government schemes (e.g. PM-KUSUM for solar pumps).
  7. Awareness and capacity building of staff and clients.

Assessment of green microfinance

  • Institutional assessment tools: the Green Index, developed by the European Microfinance Platform's action group on green inclusive and climate-smart finance, scores MFIs on environmental strategy and management, environmental risk management, and green opportunities (products and services). Social performance tools (e.g. Cerise SPI) include environmental dimensions.
  • Portfolio indicators: share of green loans in portfolio (%), number of green clients, PAR of green loans vs others, average ticket size.
  • Impact indicators: tCO2e avoided, kerosene/firewood saved, households with clean energy, water saved, income change (IRIS+ metrics).
  • Risk indicators: share of portfolio in climate-vulnerable activities/regions, losses from disasters.
  • Process: baseline survey → targets → monitoring → third-party evaluation → reporting to funders.

SELCO India's financing partnerships with regional rural banks and cooperative banks made solar lighting affordable for millions; several Indian MFIs and SFBs offer loans for solar lamps, clean cookstoves and water purifiers; NABARD supports climate-resilient agriculture finance through SHGs and FPOs.

3.12 Blended finance (catalytic capital)

Blended finance is the strategic use of catalytic capital from public or philanthropic sources (concessional loans, first-loss capital, guarantees, grants) to increase private-sector investment in sustainable development projects that would otherwise be too risky or low-return for private investors.

  • Structures: (1) concessional debt/equity in a junior or first-loss position; (2) guarantees and risk insurance; (3) technical-assistance grants; (4) design-stage/project-preparation grants.
  • Why: SDG and climate projects in developing countries face high perceived risk (currency, policy, new technology); blending adjusts the risk-return profile so private capital enters.
  • Indian context: multilateral banks and DFIs partner with Indian lenders (e.g. credit lines to SIDBI, IREDA); the National Investment and Infrastructure Fund (NIIF) uses government anchor capital to crowd in global investors.

Worked example 3.15 — first-loss protection. A ₹100 crore clean-energy fund has a ₹10 crore first-loss tranche (philanthropic) and ₹90 crore senior tranche (private). Portfolio losses of (a) ₹8 crore; (b) ₹15 crore. How is the loss shared?

  1. (a) ₹8 crore ≤ first-loss ₹10 crore → first-loss absorbs all; senior loss = 0.
  2. (b) First-loss absorbs ₹10 crore (wiped out); senior bears 15 − 10 = ₹5 crore = 5 ÷ 90 = 5.56% of senior capital (versus 15% without blending).

3.13 Role of AI in sustainable finance and applications of ML in ESG analysis

ESG data is messy: thousands of reports, news articles, satellite images, supplier lists, in many languages. Humans cannot read it all. AI reads, sees and finds patterns at scale — like having a million analysts who never sleep, though they can still make mistakes.

Artificial intelligence (AI) in sustainable finance is the use of computer systems that perform tasks requiring human-like intelligence — reading text, recognising images, predicting outcomes — to collect, analyse and act on ESG and climate information. Machine learning (ML), a subset of AI, uses algorithms that learn patterns from data to make predictions or classifications without being explicitly programmed for each rule.

Applications of ML in ESG analysis

ApplicationTechniqueWhat it does
Reading sustainability reportsNatural language processing (NLP), transformer language models (e.g. ClimateBERT)Extracts KPIs from BRSR/annual reports; classifies climate disclosures against TCFD; flags vague or unsupported claims (greenwashing detection)
Controversy and news monitoringNLP sentiment analysis, named-entity recognitionScans news and social media daily for spills, strikes, fraud; providers such as RepRisk combine AI screening with human analysts
Satellite and geospatial analysisComputer vision on satellite imageryDetects deforestation, methane leaks, flaring, water stress, flood exposure of assets; initiatives such as Climate TRACE estimate emissions independently
Estimating missing dataSupervised regression (random forests, gradient boosting)Predicts emissions of non-disclosing companies from size, sector, geography (important for PCAF and Scope 3)
ESG score construction and predictionSupervised learning, feature importancePredicts rating changes; identifies which factors drive ratings
Peer grouping and materialityUnsupervised clusteringGroups companies by actual activity rather than industry code; finds hidden patterns
Credit and climate riskClassification modelsDefault prediction including climate variables; alternative-data credit scoring for microfinance clients
Portfolio constructionOptimisation, robo-advisersBuilds portfolios meeting ESG constraints and investor preferences
Supply-chain traceabilityAI + IoT (+ blockchain)Tracks origin of raw materials; flags forced-labour risk

Benefits and limitations

Benefits: speed and scale; real-time monitoring; independent verification of company claims; fills data gaps; lower cost of ESG analysis; early warning of risks.

Limitations: garbage in, garbage out (data quality); bias in training data; "black box" models are hard to explain to regulators; models can be gamed by companies writing reports for algorithms; AI's own energy and water footprint (data centres); privacy concerns; regulatory uncertainty.

Indian banks and fintechs use ML-based alternative-data credit scoring (mobile, utility payments) to lend to thin-file borrowers, including microfinance clients. ESG data and ratings providers in India use NLP to extract BRSR data at scale. AI in risk management is covered further in 4.6.

Q1. Give two ML applications in ESG analysis.

NLP to extract and assess disclosures/detect greenwashing; satellite image analysis for deforestation or methane; ML regression to estimate missing emissions data.

Q2. State one key limitation of AI in ESG.

Lack of explainability ("black box"), or biased/poor-quality training data.

Unit 3 exam kit

Section A style (5 marks)

Q1. What are green bonds? Explain the Green Bond Principles. (5)

Meaning. A green bond is a bond whose proceeds are used exclusively to finance or refinance eligible green projects (renewable energy, clean transport, green buildings), aligned with ICMA's Green Bond Principles.

GBP core components: (1) Use of proceeds — eligible green categories with clear environmental benefits; (2) Process for project evaluation and selection — objectives, criteria, E&S risk management; (3) Management of proceeds — sub-account or tracking, disclosure of unallocated funds; (4) Reporting — annual allocation and impact reports. Key recommendations: a green bond framework and external review (second-party opinion, verification, certification).

Example: India's Sovereign Green Bonds (₹16,000 crore, 2023) for solar, metro rail and afforestation. Conclusion: the principles give investors confidence and guard against greenwashing.

Q2. Distinguish between green bonds and sustainability-linked bonds. (5)
BasisGreen bondSLB
ApproachUse of proceedsPerformance-based (KPI/SPT)
ProceedsOnly eligible green projectsGeneral corporate purposes
CouponFixed, unaffected by impactSteps up (or down) if SPT missed (met)
PrinciplesICMA GBP (4 components)ICMA SLBP (5 components)
VerificationRecommended (allocation)Mandatory (KPI performance)
Suitable forIssuers with green projectsAny issuer, including hard-to-abate
ExampleSBI, Sovereign Green BondsEnel (2019), UltraTech, JSW Steel (2021)

Conclusion: green bonds finance green assets; SLBs finance a green transition of the whole company.

Q3. Explain impact investing and its characteristics. (5)

Meaning. Investments made with the intention to generate positive, measurable social and environmental impact alongside a financial return (GIIN).

Characteristics: intentionality; expectation of financial return (at least capital); range of returns (impact-first to finance-first) and asset classes; impact measurement (IRIS+, theory of change, five dimensions: what, who, how much, contribution, risk); additionality.

Examples: Aavishkaar, Acumen, microfinance equity, affordable housing and off-grid solar funds; Social Stock Exchange (ZCZP instruments). Conclusion: it sits between philanthropy and ESG investing on the spectrum of capital.

Q4. What is carbon trading? Distinguish compliance and voluntary carbon markets. (5)

Meaning. Carbon trading is the buying and selling of allowances or credits, each typically 1 tCO2e, creating a price on carbon so reductions happen where cheapest.

Designs: cap-and-trade (EU ETS) and baseline-and-credit (CDM, CCTS, PAT).

BasisComplianceVoluntary
DriverLawVoluntary corporate targets
GovernanceGovernment regulatorPrivate standards (Verra, Gold Standard, ICVCM)
IndiaCCTS compliance, PATIndian projects supplying VCM; CCTS offsets

Illustration: trading cut the cost of a 200 t reduction from ₹2,50,000 to ₹1,00,000 in worked example 3.10. Conclusion: credibility (additionality, permanence, no double counting) is essential.

Q5. Explain green microfinance and its benefits. (5)

Meaning. Microfinance that funds environmentally beneficial products for low-income clients (solar lamps, clean cookstoves, drip irrigation), manages environmental risks in the MFI portfolio and reduces the MFI's own footprint.

Mechanism: MFI–supplier partnership → awareness → appraisal including fuel savings → loan paid to supplier → repayment from savings → monitoring → refinancing by green funds.

Benefits: lower energy costs, health, safety, income, resilience; lower emissions; MFI diversification and green refinancing. Illustration: a ₹12,000 solar home system pays back in about 11.4 months from ₹1,050 monthly savings. Example: SELCO India's financing partnerships. Conclusion: it joins SDG 1 (poverty) and SDG 7/13 (clean energy, climate).

Section B style (10 marks)

Q6. Explain the various sustainable finance products and instruments. (10)

Introduction. Sustainable finance products channel capital to environmental and social goals. They fall into use-of-proceeds instruments, performance-linked instruments, equity/fund products, market mechanisms and inclusive finance.

  1. Green bonds — proceeds for green projects; ICMA GBP. Examples: World Bank, SBI, India's Sovereign Green Bonds.
  2. Social bonds — proceeds for social projects and target populations (health, education, housing). Example: African Development Bank.
  3. Sustainability bonds — mix of green and social.
  4. Sustainability-linked bonds — coupon linked to KPI/SPT performance. Examples: Enel, Suzano, UltraTech, JSW Steel.
  5. Transition, blue, yellow bonds — decarbonisation of hard-to-abate sectors; ocean/water; solar (SEBI 2023).
  6. Green loans and SLLs — loan equivalents (LMA principles); green deposits (RBI 2023).
  7. ESG and impact funds — SEBI ESG fund category; impact funds (Aavishkaar).
  8. Carbon credits — compliance (CCTS, PAT) and voluntary markets.
  9. Microfinance and green microfinance — inclusive finance.
  10. Blended finance, impact bonds, cat bonds, tokenised green bonds — innovative structures.

Comparison table (product – purpose – feature – benefit – example) as on slide 6.

Challenges: greenwashing, lack of taxonomy, reporting costs, small greenium, measurement of impact.

Conclusion. A mature market needs both use-of-proceeds instruments (for green assets) and performance-linked ones (for transition of whole firms), backed by credible verification.

Q7. Discuss microfinance as a tool of sustainable finance in India. (10)

Introduction. Microfinance provides small collateral-free loans and other financial services to low-income households. RBI's 2022 framework defines a microfinance loan as a collateral-free loan to a household with annual income up to ₹3 lakh.

Models: SHG–Bank Linkage (NABARD, 1992), JLGs, Grameen model, individual lending; institutions — NBFC-MFIs (75% qualifying assets), SFBs, banks, cooperatives.

Contribution to sustainability: SDG 1 (poverty), SDG 5 (women's empowerment), SDG 8 (livelihoods), SDG 10; green microfinance adds SDG 7 and 13.

Growth and examples: Bandhan's transition to a bank (2015), Ujjivan and Equitas SFBs; MUDRA loans for micro-enterprises.

Issues: high interest rates, flat-rate opacity (12% flat ≈ 21.5% reducing), over-indebtedness, 2010 Andhra Pradesh crisis, coercive recovery, mission drift.

RBI 2022 reforms: 50% of monthly household income repayment cap, board-approved pricing, factsheet disclosure, no prepayment penalty, fair recovery. Worked example: household earning ₹20,000 with ₹6,000 existing EMI can borrow at most ₹42,301 at 2%/month for 12 months.

Conclusion. Responsible microfinance — with client protection and green products — is a powerful social pillar of sustainable finance.

Q8. Explain the role of AI and ML in ESG analysis. (10)

Introduction. ESG analysis suffers from inconsistent, unstructured and missing data; AI and ML help process it at scale.

  1. NLP on reports (BRSR, sustainability reports) to extract KPIs and assess TCFD alignment.
  2. Greenwashing detection by comparing claims with evidence.
  3. News/social media controversy monitoring (e.g. RepRisk).
  4. Satellite imagery for deforestation, methane, flaring, flood exposure (Climate TRACE).
  5. Estimating missing emissions data for financed-emissions (PCAF) and Scope 3.
  6. Predicting ESG ratings and identifying drivers; clustering peers.
  7. Climate-risk modelling of physical hazards at asset level.
  8. Alternative-data credit scoring for inclusion.
  9. Portfolio construction and robo-advice with ESG preferences.

Limitations: data quality, bias, explainability, gaming, AI's own footprint, privacy, regulation.

Conclusion. AI augments, not replaces, human judgement; combined with verification and blockchain-based traceability it can make ESG data more reliable.

Section C style (15-mark case)

Case 3. SunRise Housing Finance chooses between a green bond and an SLB (15)

Case: SunRise Housing Finance wants to raise ₹500 crore for 5 years. It has ₹300 crore of IGBC-certified green-building loans and a pipeline of affordable-housing loans; its own emissions are small. Its bankers propose (A) a green bond at 7.40% or (B) an SLB at 7.45% with a 25 bps step-up from year 4 if the share of green-certified homes in its book does not reach 30% by year 3. (a) Evaluate both (6). (b) Compute the cost of B if the SPT is missed (4). (c) Recommend (5).

(a) Evaluation. A green bond requires eligible assets: SunRise has only ₹300 crore of green loans, so it could allocate 60% immediately and would need ₹200 crore more of eligible loans (unallocated funds must be disclosed). A sustainability bond (green + affordable housing under SBP) would fit its pipeline better. An SLB is general-purpose; its KPI (green-certified share) is material to a housing lender, but the SPT must be ambitious relative to baseline and peers and verified annually.

(b) Cost of B if missed: base interest = 500 × 7.45% = ₹37.25 crore a year; with step-up 7.70% = ₹38.50 crore in years 4–5; extra = 500 × 0.0025 × 2 = ₹2.5 crore. Total interest over 5 years: 37.25 × 5 = ₹186.25 crore if met; ₹188.75 crore if missed. Green bond A: 500 × 7.40% × 5 = ₹185 crore.

(c) Recommendation. Issue a sustainability bond (green + social) under ICMA guidelines at around the green-bond price, allocating ₹300 crore to green-building loans and ₹200 crore to affordable-housing loans; obtain a second-party opinion; report allocation and impact (homes financed, energy saved). Alternatively, if the company wants to commit to a transformation target, an SLB is suitable, but only with a robust KPI and verified baseline. Governance: board-approved framework; SEBI green debt disclosures; avoid greenwashing by not counting uncertified homes.

Conclusion. The instrument must fit the issuer's assets and strategy; the cheapest label is not the best if it cannot be credibly delivered.

Unit 4 — Risk Management, Reporting and Future of Sustainable Finance

Syllabus: risk identification in sustainable finance (market, credit, operational risks); ESG risk quantification and reporting frameworks; sustainable finance risk metrics and corporate disclosure practices; future trends — AI in risk management, blockchain for ESG transparency; emerging trends in ESG analysis. (12 teaching hours — the heaviest unit; CO3, CO4.)

4.1 Risk identification in sustainable finance

ESG and climate issues are not a new "type" of risk sitting next to credit or market risk. They are drivers that show up through the traditional risk types. A flood (E) becomes credit risk when a borrower's factory is destroyed; a bribery scandal (G) becomes market risk when the share price falls.

Risk identification in sustainable finance is the process of recognising how environmental, social and governance factors, including climate change, can cause financial losses to a financial institution or investor through market, credit, operational, liquidity and reputational risk channels. The Basel Committee (2022) describes climate-related financial risks as risk drivers that materialise through these existing categories.

Risk typeDefinitionHow ESG/climate drives itExample
Market riskLoss from changes in market prices (equity, bond, commodity, currency, interest rates)Repricing of carbon-intensive stocks and bonds when policy tightens; commodity price shocks from droughts; sudden ESG downgrades causing sell-offsCoal-company shares falling after a carbon-price announcement; bond spreads widening after a governance scandal
Credit riskLoss when a borrower or counterparty fails to repayBorrower's cash flows hit by carbon costs, stranded assets or disasters (higher PD); collateral in flood zones loses value (higher LGD)Farm loans after drought; loans to thermal power plants; mortgages in coastal areas
Operational riskLoss from inadequate or failed processes, people, systems or external eventsFloods damaging branches/data centres; supply-chain disruption; conduct risk (mis-selling "green" products); fraud; legal actionChennai 2015 floods disrupting bank operations; greenwashing fines on asset managers
Liquidity riskInability to meet obligations or sell assets without large lossesDeposit withdrawals after a disaster; fire sales of stranded assets; drawdowns of credit lines by affected firmsInsurers selling assets to pay catastrophe claims
Reputational riskLoss of trust damaging businessFinancing controversial projects; human-rights abuses in value chain; greenwashingGlobal banks facing campaigns over coal financing
Legal / liability riskLosses from litigation or regulatory actionClimate litigation; NGT compensation; mis-disclosureVedanta's Sterlite Copper plant closure (see Unit 5)
Strategic / business-model riskBusiness model becomes unviableTechnology shift (EVs), changing consumer preferenceICE-component makers
Underwriting risk (insurers)Claims exceed premiumsMore frequent and severe catastrophesRising cyclone claims on the east coast

E, S and G risk drivers mapped to financial risks

DriverMarketCreditOperational
E — climate, pollution, waterStranded-asset repricingHigher PD of pollutersPhysical damage to own assets
S — labour, safety, community, data privacyShare-price drop after accidentsStrikes hurting borrower's cash flowData breach, mis-selling
G — fraud, board failure, briberyCollapse in valuationSudden default (e.g. IL&FS 2018)Internal fraud, sanctions

Tools for identification

Materiality assessment; sector heat maps (exposure to carbon-intensive or climate-vulnerable sectors); exclusion and watch lists; E&S due diligence (Equator Principles, IFC Performance Standards); client questionnaires; geolocation of assets for physical hazards; controversy screening; scenario analysis.

Risk score (heat map) = Likelihood (1–5) × Impact (1–5)

Scores of 15–25 = high (red), 8–14 = medium (amber), 1–7 = low (green) — a common but not universal banding.

Worked example 4.1 — ESG risk heat map. A bank rates: (a) carbon-price risk on its steel loans — likelihood 4, impact 5; (b) data-privacy breach at its card business — likelihood 2, impact 3.

  1. (a) 4 × 5 = 20 → high: priority for scenario analysis and engagement with borrowers.
  2. (b) 2 × 3 = 6 → low: monitor through existing controls.

4.2 ESG risk quantification

Identification says "this could hurt". Quantification says "by how much". The main tools: carbon metrics (how much emission is in my portfolio?), loss metrics (how much money could I lose under a scenario?) and scores (how does this company rank?).

(a) Financed emissions — PCAF standard

Financed emissions are the greenhouse gas emissions of borrowers and investee companies attributed to a financial institution in proportion to its financing. The Partnership for Carbon Accounting Financials (PCAF) Global GHG Accounting and Reporting Standard (2020) sets the method: financed emissions = attribution factor × company emissions.

Attribution factor (AF) = Outstanding amount ÷ EVIC (listed companies) or ÷ (Total equity + debt) (unlisted)
Financed emissions = Σ AFi × Emissionsi
Portfolio carbon footprint = Financed emissions ÷ Amount invested/lent (tCO2e per ₹ crore)
WACI = Σ (Portfolio weighti × Emissionsi / Revenuei)

EVIC = enterprise value including cash (market capitalisation + total debt, without subtracting cash). WACI = Weighted Average Carbon Intensity (TCFD-recommended), in tCO2e per ₹ crore of revenue. PCAF also scores data quality from 1 (verified reported emissions) to 5 (estimates based on sector averages).

Worked example 4.2 — PCAF financed emissions and WACI. A bank's portfolio (₹ crore):

BorrowerOutstandingEVICEmissions (tCO2e)Revenue
Steel Co2002,000500,0001,200
Cement Co15060090,000800
IT Co1002,50040,0003,000
  1. AF: Steel = 200/2,000 = 0.10; Cement = 150/600 = 0.25; IT = 100/2,500 = 0.04.
  2. Financed emissions: Steel = 0.10 × 500,000 = 50,000; Cement = 0.25 × 90,000 = 22,500; IT = 0.04 × 40,000 = 1,600.
  3. Total financed emissions = 50,000 + 22,500 + 1,600 = 74,100 tCO2e.
  4. Carbon footprint = 74,100 ÷ 450 = 164.67 tCO2e per ₹ crore lent.
  5. Weights: 200/450 = 0.4444; 150/450 = 0.3333; 100/450 = 0.2222. Intensities: 500,000/1,200 = 416.67; 90,000/800 = 112.50; 40,000/3,000 = 13.33.
  6. WACI = 0.4444 × 416.67 + 0.3333 × 112.50 + 0.2222 × 13.33 = 185.19 + 37.50 + 2.96 = 225.65 tCO2e per ₹ crore revenue.

Steel is 67% of financed emissions (50,000/74,100) with 44% of the lending — the obvious target for engagement or SLL structures.

(b) Climate value-at-risk / scenario loss

Scenario portfolio change = Σ (Weighti × Scenario returni)

Worked example 4.3. A ₹1,000 crore equity portfolio: 20% energy, 15% utilities, 65% others. Under an NGFS-style Net Zero 2050 repricing: energy −30%, utilities −10%, others +2%.

  1. Energy: 0.20 × (−30%) = −6.0% → −₹60 crore.
  2. Utilities: 0.15 × (−10%) = −1.5% → −₹15 crore.
  3. Others: 0.65 × (+2%) = +1.3% → +₹13 crore.
  4. Total = −6.2% → −₹62 crore.

(c) ESG-adjusted valuation

Worked example 4.4 — ESG risk premium in the discount rate. Next year's free cash flow is ₹100 crore, growing 4% forever; cost of capital 10%. Investors add 0.5% for poor governance. Value change?

  1. Value at 10% = 100 ÷ (0.10 − 0.04) = ₹1,666.67 crore.
  2. Value at 10.5% = 100 ÷ (0.105 − 0.04) = ₹1,538.46 crore.
  3. Loss = 1,538.46 ÷ 1,666.67 − 1 = −7.7%.

A half-percent ESG risk premium destroys 7.7% of value — the financial case for good governance.

(d) Other quantification approaches

  • ESG scores and ratings (weighted pillar scores; see worked example 1.6).
  • Implied Temperature Rise (ITR): the global warming outcome implied if the whole economy had the same emissions trajectory as the company or portfolio (e.g. "2.7°C-aligned").
  • Carbon earnings-at-risk: carbon cost ÷ EBITDA (worked example 2.4).
  • PD/LGD adjustment under stress (worked example 2.5).
  • Physical risk expected annual loss (worked example 2.6).
  • Green asset ratio / taxonomy alignment: share of a bank's assets financing taxonomy-aligned activities.

4.3 Reporting frameworks (in depth)

Think of the frameworks as different exam boards. GRI marks you on how you affect the world; SASB/ISSB on what investors need to value you; TCFD on climate risk specifically; CDP runs an annual environmental questionnaire; Integrated Reporting asks you to tell one joined-up story; BRSR is India's mandatory board.

(1) GRI — Global Reporting Initiative

  • Founded 1997 in Boston (by CERES and the Tellus Institute); independent, now headquartered in Amsterdam; the most widely used sustainability reporting standards worldwide.
  • Impact materiality (inside-out); audience: all stakeholders.
  • Structure (2021 revision): Universal Standards (GRI 1 Foundation, GRI 2 General Disclosures, GRI 3 Material Topics), Sector Standards (e.g. oil and gas, coal, agriculture, mining), Topic Standards (e.g. GRI 305 Emissions, GRI 403 Occupational Health and Safety).

(2) SASB Standards

  • Sustainability Accounting Standards Board (2011); 77 industry-specific standards under the Sustainable Industry Classification System; financial materiality; audience: investors.
  • Merged with IIRC into the Value Reporting Foundation (2021), consolidated into the IFRS Foundation (2022); ISSB now maintains SASB and uses it for industry-based metrics.

(3) TCFD — Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures

  • Created by the Financial Stability Board (2015); recommendations published 2017; disbanded in 2023 after its work was absorbed by the ISSB. Still the template for RBI's draft climate disclosure framework.
  • Four pillars and 11 recommended disclosures:

Mnemonic: "Good Students Read Maps" = Governance, Strategy, Risk management, Metrics & targets. Count 2-3-3-3 = 11 disclosures.

(4) ISSB — IFRS S1 and IFRS S2

  • International Sustainability Standards Board, announced by the IFRS Foundation at COP26 (November 2021); standards issued June 2023, effective for annual periods beginning on or after 1 January 2024 where adopted by jurisdictions.
  • IFRS S1 — General Requirements for Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information: disclose sustainability-related risks and opportunities that could reasonably be expected to affect the entity's cash flows, access to finance or cost of capital; same four content areas as TCFD; use SASB standards for guidance.
  • IFRS S2 — Climate-related Disclosures: builds on TCFD; requires Scope 1, 2 and 3 emissions, climate scenario analysis, transition plans, industry-based metrics; transition reliefs in the first year (e.g. Scope 3 relief).
  • Financial (investor) materiality; designed as a "global baseline" that jurisdictions can add to. Many jurisdictions have adopted or are adopting it; India's ICAI and regulators are examining convergence, and BRSR already covers much of the content.

(5) CDP

Founded in 2000 as the Carbon Disclosure Project; runs annual questionnaires on climate change, water security and forests on behalf of investors and large purchasers; scores A to D− (F for non-disclosure). Aligned with TCFD/ISSB. Many Indian companies (including Infosys, ITC, Tata group firms) respond, and some score in the "A" band.

(6) Integrated Reporting (<IR>)

  • International Integrated Reporting Framework (IIRC, 2013; revised 2021; now under the IFRS Foundation). An integrated report is a concise communication about how an organisation's strategy, governance, performance and prospects lead to the creation, preservation or erosion of value over the short, medium and long term.
  • Six capitals: financial, manufactured, intellectual, human, social and relationship, natural.
  • Guiding principles: strategic focus and future orientation; connectivity of information; stakeholder relationships; materiality; conciseness; reliability and completeness; consistency and comparability.
  • SEBI (2017) advised the top 500 listed companies to adopt integrated reporting voluntarily; many large Indian companies (e.g. Infosys, ITC, Tata Steel) publish integrated reports.

(7) BRSR / BRSR Core (India)

See 1.13: mandatory for top 1,000 listed companies; 9 NGRBC principles; essential and leadership indicators; BRSR Core with assurance/assessment; value chain.

(8) Others to know

  • EU CSRD and ESRS — mandatory EU sustainability reporting on a double materiality basis (adopted 2022; phased from FY2024; scope being simplified under the EU's 2025 "Omnibus" proposals). Relevant to Indian companies with large EU operations.
  • TNFD — Taskforce on Nature-related Financial Disclosures (final recommendations 2023), mirrors TCFD for nature; LEAP approach: Locate, Evaluate, Assess, Prepare.
  • GHG Protocol (emission accounting), PCAF (financed emissions), SBTi (target validation), EU Taxonomy and SFDR (fund classification: Article 6, 8 "light green", 9 "dark green").
FrameworkMaterialityScopeMandatory?Primary user
GRIImpactAll ESG topicsVoluntary (referenced by laws)All stakeholders
SASBFinancialIndustry-specific ESGVoluntaryInvestors
TCFDFinancialClimate onlyAdopted into several regulationsInvestors, lenders
ISSB S1/S2FinancialSustainability (S1) + climate (S2)Where jurisdictions adoptInvestors
CDPBoth (environment)Climate, water, forestsVoluntary questionnaireInvestors, buyers
<IR>Value creationSix capitalsVoluntaryInvestors
BRSRBoth9 NGRBC principlesMandatory (top 1,000 listed)Investors, regulators, stakeholders

4.4 Sustainable finance risk metrics

Sustainable finance risk metrics are quantitative indicators used to measure, monitor and disclose the exposure of a company, portfolio or financial institution to ESG and climate risks and its sustainability performance.

CategoryMetricFormula / unitUse
Carbon / climateAbsolute emissionstCO2e (Scope 1, 2, 3)Footprint, targets

Implied temperature rise°C alignmentParis alignment
Climate VaR / scenario loss% or ₹ loss under scenarioStress testing
Green activityGreen revenue / capex shareGreen revenue ÷ total revenueTransition progress
Green asset ratioTaxonomy-aligned assets ÷ covered assetsBank's green share
Allocation % (green bonds)Allocated ÷ proceedsUse-of-proceeds reporting
Physical riskExpected annual lossProbability × damage × valueAsset resilience
% assets in high-hazard zonesShare of exposureConcentration
Water stress exposure% operations in high-stress basinsOperational risk
SocialLTIFRLost-time injuries × 1,000,000 ÷ hours workedSafety
