Customer complaintsNumber, resolution rateConduct risk
GovernanceBoard independence% independent directorsOversight quality
CEO pay ratioCEO pay ÷ median employee payFairness
Controversy scoreProvider scaleReputational risk
CreditESG-adjusted PD, LGD, ELPD × LGD × EADProvisions, capital

Worked example 4.5 — LTIFR. A plant recorded 6 lost-time injuries in 2,400,000 hours worked. LTIFR (per million hours)?

  1. LTIFR = 6 × 1,000,000 ÷ 2,400,000 = 2.5 lost-time injuries per million hours.

4.5 Corporate disclosure practices, greenwashing and credibility

Good disclosure practices

  1. Materiality-based — report what matters (materiality assessment disclosed).
  2. Framework-aligned — BRSR plus GRI/ISSB/TCFD index tables.
  3. Quantitative and comparable — multi-year data, intensity metrics, consistent boundaries.
  4. Forward-looking — targets, transition plans, capex plans, scenario analysis.
  5. Balanced — report failures (fatalities, spills) as well as successes.
  6. Assured — independent limited or reasonable assurance (BRSR Core).
  7. Value-chain coverage — Scope 3 and supplier ESG.
  8. Connected — linked to financial statements (integrated reporting).
  9. Timely and accessible — in annual reports, machine-readable (XBRL filings of BRSR on exchanges).

Greenwashing

Greenwashing is making false, misleading, unsubstantiated or incomplete claims about the environmental or sustainability credentials of a product, service, company or investment, to appear more sustainable than it is. (Slide 35: "exaggerating or faking environmental claims"; SEBI's 2023 green debt framework describes greenwashing in similar terms.)

TypeMeaningExample
Hidden trade-offHighlighting one green attribute while ignoring larger harms"Recycled packaging" on a high-emission product
No proofClaims without evidence or certification"Carbon neutral" without verified offsets
VaguenessUndefined terms"Eco-friendly", "natural"
IrrelevanceTrue but unimportant claim"CFC-free" (CFCs are banned anyway)
Lesser of two evilsGreen claim within a harmful category"Green" cigarettes
FibbingOutright false claimsFake certifications
False labelsLabel that implies third-party endorsementSelf-made "eco" seal
GreenhushingUnder-reporting sustainability to avoid scrutinyDropping targets quietly
Greenwishing / greenlighting / greenshiftingUnrealistic targets; spotlighting a small green activity; blaming consumersNet-zero pledges without plans
Financial-product greenwashingESG funds/bonds that do not do what they claimGreen bond refinancing old assets without disclosure; ESG fund holding excluded stocks

The first seven are TerraChoice's "Seven Sins of Greenwashing".

Real cases

  • Volkswagen "Dieselgate" (2015): software cheated emission tests while "clean diesel" was marketed — huge fines and recalls; governance failure behind an environmental claim.
  • DWS (Deutsche Bank's asset manager): after whistle-blower allegations and 2022 raids by German authorities, the US SEC in 2023 fined it US$19 million for misstatements about its ESG integration process.
  • Goldman Sachs Asset Management (US$4 million, 2022) and BNY Mellon Investment Adviser (US$1.5 million, 2022) were penalised by the SEC over ESG fund policies and procedures or misstatements.

Regulatory responses

SEBI's green debt framework (do's and don'ts against greenwashing, 2023), ESG fund norms (65% in BRSR-assured companies, assurance of compliance) and ERP regulation; RBI green deposit verification; the EU's SFDR and Green Bond Standard; the UK FCA's anti-greenwashing rule (2024); independent assurance of BRSR Core. Detailed treatment for green bonds is in Unit 5 topic 8.

4.6 Future trend: AI in risk management

AI in risk management is the use of machine learning, natural language processing and related techniques to identify, measure, predict and monitor risks — including ESG and climate risks — faster and more accurately than traditional rule-based methods.

Risk areaAI application
Credit riskML default prediction using financial, alternative and climate data; early-warning signals from transaction patterns
Market riskNLP sentiment on news and policy announcements to anticipate repricing; scenario generation
Operational riskFraud and anomaly detection; cyber-threat detection; predicting supply-chain disruption from weather and logistics data
Physical climate riskDownscaled hazard models (flood, heat, cyclone) at asset level using satellite data and AI
ESG/reputational riskReal-time controversy monitoring; greenwashing detection; supplier-risk screening
Compliance (RegTech)Automated KYC/AML; automated extraction of BRSR data; checking disclosures against regulations

Benefits: speed, coverage, early warnings, cost efficiency, pattern detection across unstructured data. Risks of AI itself (model risk): bias and discrimination in lending; explainability ("black box"); overfitting to past data (climate is non-stationary — the past is a poor guide); data privacy; concentration on a few AI vendors; cyber vulnerabilities; energy consumption. Regulators expect model governance, human oversight and validation; RBI has discussed a framework for responsible and ethical AI use in the financial sector (FREE-AI committee report, 2025).

4.7 Future trend: blockchain for ESG transparency

A blockchain is a shared notebook copied on many computers; once a line is written and agreed, nobody can secretly erase it. For ESG, that means a supplier's certificate, a green bond's spending or a carbon credit's retirement can be recorded once and seen by everyone — making it much harder to double-count or fake.

Blockchain is a type of distributed ledger technology (DLT) in which transactions are recorded in cryptographically linked blocks replicated across a network of participants, making records tamper-evident, transparent and verifiable without a single central record-keeper.

Use caseHow blockchain helpsExample
Supply-chain traceabilityRecords origin and custody of raw materials (cobalt, cotton, palm oil, tea) to verify ethical and low-carbon sourcingTraceability pilots in food and minerals; farm-to-fork tracking
Green bond proceeds trackingProceeds and project milestones recorded on-chain; investors see allocation in near real timeBIS Innovation Hub and HKMA's Project Genesis (2021) prototyped tokenised green bonds with real-time impact data; Hong Kong's tokenised green bond (2023)
Carbon credit registriesUnique tokens for each credit prevent double counting and double selling; retirements are publicWorld Bank's Climate Warehouse work on connecting registries; tokenised carbon-credit platforms
Renewable energy certificates and P2P energy tradingTraceable origin of green power; micro-trading between rooftop ownersP2P solar trading pilots by Indian state regulators and utilities
ESG data and reportingImmutable, time-stamped sensor (IoT) data feeding ESG reports; reduces manipulationEmission monitoring linked to smart meters
Smart contracts for SLBsAutomatic coupon step-up when verified KPI data (oracle) shows a missed targetSee Unit 5 topic 12
Impact and aid disbursementTransparent flow of donor funds to beneficiariesPilots by UN agencies

Limitations: "garbage in, garbage out" — blockchain secures records, not the truth of what was entered (needs trusted oracles and verification); energy use of proof-of-work chains (most financial pilots use permissioned, low-energy chains); scalability; interoperability; legal recognition of tokens; privacy of commercial data; cost and skills.

4.8 Emerging trends in ESG analysis and sustainable finance

  1. Convergence of standards — ISSB as a global baseline; jurisdictions adopting it; interoperability with GRI and ESRS.
  2. Mandatory assurance — BRSR Core in India; a global shift from limited towards reasonable assurance.
  3. Value-chain and Scope 3 focus — supplier data platforms; CBAM pushing product-level carbon footprints.
  4. Transition finance and transition plans — credible plans for hard-to-abate sectors (steel, cement, aluminium); transition bonds; India's climate finance taxonomy work.
  5. Nature and biodiversity — TNFD, biodiversity credits, nature-positive targets.
  6. Just transition — social impacts of coal phase-down on workers and regions (e.g. Jharkhand, Chhattisgarh coal belts).
  7. Carbon markets — India's CCTS, Article 6 trading, integrity labels (ICVCM).
  8. Regulation of ESG ratings — SEBI ERP regulation; EU ESG ratings regulation; IOSCO recommendations.
  9. AI and big data — satellite verification, NLP, AI-driven ratings (4.6).
  10. Tokenisation and DLT — digital green bonds, tokenised carbon credits (Unit 5 topics 10–13).
  11. Anti-greenwashing enforcement and "greenhushing".
  12. Political backlash — "anti-ESG" movements in parts of the US and exits from some net-zero alliances; a shift in language towards "transition" and "resilience".
  13. Adaptation and resilience finance — growing after COP27/28; parametric insurance.
  14. Retail participation — sovereign green bonds accessible via RBI Retail Direct; ESG mutual funds.

4.9 Lesson-plan research readings and the class debate

Your course plan assigns two research papers (Weeks 7 and 8) and a Week 10 debate: "Technology improves ESG transparency more than regulation." It also lists a NITI Aayog (2026) policy report on scenarios towards Viksit Bharat and Net Zero and their financing needs as a Week 4 source; its figures are not in your files, so none are quoted here — use it as a named source when writing about India's transition-finance needs.

Reading 1 (Week 7): stock price reactions to ESG news

Serafeim and Yoon, "Stock Price Reactions to ESG News: The Role of ESG Ratings and Disagreement", Review of Accounting Studies (2023).

  • Question: do markets react to ESG news, and what role do ESG ratings play?
  • Main ideas: share prices react to ESG news that is financially material for the company's industry, with little reaction to immaterial news; consensus ESG ratings carry information that helps predict future ESG news; and when rating providers disagree strongly about a company, the ratings are less informative and the market's reactions differ.
  • Exam use: supports the argument that materiality (SASB/ISSB idea) matters more than volume of ESG disclosure, and that ratings divergence (1.14) is a real cost to investors.

Reading 2 (Week 8): climate risks and institutional investors

Krueger, Sautner and Starks, "The Importance of Climate Risks for Institutional Investors", Review of Financial Studies 33(3) (2020).

  • Method: a survey of institutional investors worldwide.
  • Main ideas: investors believe climate risks have financial implications for their portfolios and that regulatory (transition) risks are already beginning to materialise, ahead of physical risks; many see risk management and engagement with companies, rather than divestment, as the better response; long-term, larger and ESG-oriented investors engage more.
  • Exam use: evidence for "role of the financial system" (1.9), for engagement in responsible investment (1.15) and for transition risk in Unit 2.

The Week 10 debate: "Technology improves ESG transparency more than regulation"

For the motion (technology)Against the motion (regulation)
Satellites and IoT sensors measure emissions, deforestation and water use independently of what companies report (4.6).Without a legal duty to disclose, companies can simply stay silent; BRSR made ESG reporting universal for the top 1,000 overnight.
Blockchain gives tamper-evident records of green bond proceeds and carbon credits (4.7).Technology records data, but regulation defines what to record (BRSR Core KPIs, taxonomies) so that data is comparable.
NLP flags vague or greenwashing language across thousands of reports.Assurance and enforcement (SEBI penalties, the UK anti-greenwashing rule) create consequences; technology alone does not.
Real-time data beats annual reports."Garbage in, garbage out": sensors and models are only as good as standards and verification.

Balanced conclusion for an exam: regulation creates the obligation and the common language; technology makes compliance cheaper and verification independent. Transparency is highest where both work together — e.g. regulated disclosure (BRSR Core) verified with technology-enabled assurance.

1. What does the Week 7 paper say about which ESG news moves share prices?

Financially material news for the firm's industry moves prices; immaterial news has little effect; rating disagreement reduces the information in ESG ratings.

2. According to the Week 8 survey, which climate risk had institutional investors seen materialising first?

Regulatory (transition) risk, ahead of physical risk; and many preferred engagement to divestment.

Unit 4 exam kit

Section A style (5 marks)

Q1. Explain the four pillars of TCFD. (5)

The TCFD (FSB, recommendations 2017) organised climate disclosure into four pillars with 11 recommended disclosures:

  1. Governance — board oversight and management's role in climate risks/opportunities.
  2. Strategy — risks and opportunities over short, medium, long term; impact on business and financial planning; resilience under scenarios including 2°C or lower.
  3. Risk management — processes to identify, assess and manage climate risks and integrate them into overall risk management.
  4. Metrics and targets — metrics used; Scope 1, 2 (and 3) emissions; targets and performance.

A nested-box diagram adds value. Significance: the basis for ISSB's IFRS S2 and RBI's 2024 draft disclosure framework.

Q2. How are market, credit and operational risks affected by ESG factors? (5)

ESG factors are risk drivers that act through traditional risk categories (Basel Committee, 2022).

  1. Market risk: repricing of carbon-intensive assets after policy change; ESG downgrades causing sell-offs; commodity shocks from droughts.
  2. Credit risk: higher PD from carbon costs or disaster losses; higher LGD as collateral in hazard zones loses value; e.g. a stress test raising EL from ₹90 crore to ₹225 crore on a ₹10,000 crore book.
  3. Operational risk: damage to branches and data centres, supply-chain disruption, mis-selling of "green" products, fraud and legal penalties.

Also liquidity and reputational risks. Conclusion: banks must integrate ESG into existing risk frameworks rather than treat it separately.

Q3. What is greenwashing? Explain its types. (5)

Meaning. Making false, misleading, unsubstantiated or incomplete sustainability claims to appear greener than one is.

Types (TerraChoice's seven sins + newer forms): hidden trade-off; no proof; vagueness; irrelevance; lesser of two evils; fibbing; false labels; greenhushing; greenwishing; financial-product greenwashing (ESG funds, green bonds refinancing old assets).

Examples: Volkswagen Dieselgate (2015); SEC's US$19 million penalty on DWS (2023). Controls: SEBI green debt do's and don'ts, ESG fund norms, BRSR Core assurance, external reviews. Conclusion: greenwashing destroys trust and invites regulation.

Q4. Explain the use of blockchain for ESG transparency. (5)

Blockchain is a distributed, tamper-evident ledger. Uses: (1) supply-chain traceability of raw materials; (2) tracking green bond proceeds and impact (Project Genesis, Hong Kong tokenised green bond); (3) carbon credit registries preventing double counting; (4) renewable energy certificates and P2P trading; (5) immutable IoT-based ESG data; (6) smart contracts automating SLB coupon adjustments. Limitations: garbage-in-garbage-out, energy use of some chains, scalability, legal recognition, privacy. Conclusion: blockchain improves trust in ESG data when paired with verified inputs.

Section B style (10 marks)

Q5. Explain the major ESG reporting frameworks and compare them. (10)

Introduction. Reporting has shifted from retrospective financial reporting to forward-looking sustainability reporting. Frameworks differ mainly by materiality lens and audience.

  1. GRI — 1997; impact materiality; Universal, Sector and Topic Standards; all stakeholders.
  2. SASB — 2011; 77 industry standards; financial materiality; now under IFRS Foundation.
  3. TCFD — FSB 2017; climate; four pillars, 11 disclosures.
  4. ISSB — IFRS S1 (general) and S2 (climate), 2023; global baseline for investors.
  5. CDP — environmental questionnaire; A to D− scores.
  6. <IR> — six capitals, integrated value-creation story.
  7. BRSR — SEBI, mandatory for top 1,000 listed companies; 9 NGRBC principles; BRSR Core assurance.
  8. CSRD/ESRS — EU, double materiality.

Comparison table (framework, materiality, scope, mandatory, user) as in 4.3.

Indian practice: large companies (Infosys, ITC) publish BRSR, GRI-referenced sustainability reports, TCFD sections, CDP responses and integrated reports.

Conclusion. The trend is convergence towards ISSB for investor reporting, with GRI for impacts and BRSR as India's legal baseline.

Q6. Discuss ESG risk quantification methods with an illustration. (10)

Introduction. Quantification converts ESG exposures into numbers used for pricing, capital and disclosure.

  1. Carbon accounting — Scope 1/2/3 (activity × EF).
  2. Financed emissions (PCAF) — AF = outstanding ÷ EVIC; financed emissions = Σ AF × emissions.
  3. WACI — weighted emissions/revenue.
  4. Scenario/climate VaR — portfolio loss under NGFS scenarios.
  5. Credit stress testing — PD/LGD shifts, EL = PD × LGD × EAD.
  6. Physical risk EAL.
  7. ESG scores and ESG-adjusted valuation (risk premium in discount rate).
  8. ITR and taxonomy alignment.

Illustration: loans of ₹200, 150 and 100 crore to steel, cement and IT companies give financed emissions of 50,000 + 22,500 + 1,600 = 74,100 tCO2e and WACI 225.65 tCO2e/₹ crore revenue; steel is 67% of financed emissions.

Challenges: data gaps (PCAF data quality scores), estimation, model risk, rating divergence.

Conclusion. Quantification lets institutions set targets, price risk and disclose credibly.

Section C style (15-mark case)

Case 4. Bharat Commercial Bank prepares its first climate-risk disclosure (15)

Case: A mid-sized Indian bank must prepare for RBI's climate-risk disclosure framework. Its loan book includes ₹200 crore to a steel firm, ₹150 crore to a cement firm, ₹100 crore to an IT firm (data as in worked example 4.2) and a large agricultural book in drought-prone districts. The board asks: (a) What should the disclosure contain? (5) (b) Compute financed emissions and WACI (5). (c) What actions should the bank take? (5)

(a) Following the TCFD-aligned four pillars: Governance (board risk committee oversight, management climate committee); Strategy (transition risk in steel/cement, physical risk in agriculture, opportunities in green lending; scenario analysis with orderly/disorderly/hot-house scenarios); Risk management (integration of climate into credit appraisal, ICAAP, sector limits); Metrics and targets (financed emissions, WACI, green loan share, exposure to climate-sensitive sectors, targets).

(b) Financed emissions = 50,000 + 22,500 + 1,600 = 74,100 tCO2e; footprint 164.67 t per ₹ crore lent; WACI 225.65 tCO2e/₹ crore revenue.

(c) Engage steel and cement borrowers on transition plans; offer SLLs tied to emission intensity; set sector-level decarbonisation targets; expand green loans and raise green deposits under RBI's framework; stress-test the agricultural book for drought (PD/LGD shocks); promote crop insurance and climate-resilient agriculture lending with NABARD; improve data via PCAF data-quality upgrades and AI-based estimation; publish disclosures with assurance.

Conclusion. Early, credible disclosure positions the bank for regulation, lowers risk and attracts ESG investors.

Unit 4 exam kit — extended practice set

Unit 4 carries the most teaching hours (12), so expect two or three questions from it. The set below adds to Q1–Q6 and Case 4 above. Each answer is written at full-marks length and structure.

Section A style (5 marks)

Q7. Distinguish between single materiality and double materiality. (5)

Introduction. Materiality decides which sustainability issues a company must report. The two lenses differ in whose interest they protect.

BasisSingle (financial) materialityDouble materiality
Question askedHow do sustainability issues affect the company's value? ("outside-in")Outside-in and how the company affects people and planet ("inside-out")
Primary usersInvestors, lendersInvestors plus all stakeholders
FrameworksISSB (IFRS S1/S2), SASB, TCFDEU CSRD/ESRS; GRI covers the impact side
Example (cement firm)Carbon price raising costsCarbon price raising costs + dust and emissions harming nearby villages

Indian angle. SEBI's BRSR asks for material responsible-business issues with both risks and opportunities and covers impacts through the nine NGRBC principles, so it leans towards a broad (impact-inclusive) view. Conclusion. Double materiality gives a fuller picture; single materiality is narrower but directly decision-useful for investors.

Q8. What is BRSR Core? Why was it introduced? (5)

Meaning. BRSR Core is a subset of the BRSR containing a limited set of key performance indicators under nine ESG attributes (for example GHG footprint, water footprint, energy footprint, waste management, employee well-being and safety, gender diversity, inclusive development, fairness in engaging customers and suppliers, openness of business) for which SEBI requires reasonable assurance by an independent provider, phased in from FY2023-24 for the largest listed companies by market capitalisation.

Why introduced.

  1. Credibility: self-reported ESG data invited scepticism and greenwashing.
  2. Comparability: a fixed set of quantified KPIs with defined methods makes companies comparable.
  3. Investor use: ESG funds and lenders need assured numbers.
  4. Value chain: SEBI also introduced ESG disclosures for the value chain of the largest companies, recognising that much impact sits with suppliers and customers.
  5. Global alignment: moves India towards assured sustainability reporting as seen in ISSB and CSRD regimes.

Conclusion. BRSR Core converts BRSR from a disclosure form into a verified data set.

Q9. Explain three sustainable finance risk metrics with formulas. (5)
  1. Financed emissions (PCAF): Σ (Outstanding ÷ EVIC) × Company emissions. Measures the emissions a bank or investor "owns" through its financing. Example: ₹50 crore in a company with EVIC ₹1,000 crore and 4,00,000 tCO2e gives 0.05 × 4,00,000 = 20,000 tCO2e.
  2. WACI: Σ weight × (emissions ÷ revenue). Measures the portfolio's exposure to carbon-intensive companies; recommended by TCFD for asset owners and managers.
  3. LTIFR: lost-time injuries × 1,000,000 ÷ hours worked. A social metric that is also a common SLB/SLL KPI in heavy industry.

Others: expected loss (PD × LGD × EAD) under climate stress, green asset ratio, implied temperature rise. Conclusion. Metrics make ESG risk comparable, target-able and auditable.

Q10. How does AI help in ESG risk management? State limitations. (5)

Uses. (1) NLP reads annual reports, BRSRs and news to extract ESG data and flag controversies in near real time; (2) satellite and remote-sensing analytics detect deforestation, flaring, methane plumes and flood exposure of assets; (3) ML estimation fills gaps in emissions data for companies that do not disclose (useful for PCAF data-quality improvement); (4) greenwashing detection by comparing claims with actual data; (5) credit models that add climate variables to PD estimation; (6) scenario engines that run thousands of climate paths quickly.

Limitations. Biased or incomplete training data, "black-box" models that are hard to explain to regulators and boards, model risk, data privacy, and the energy use of large models. Conclusion. AI speeds up and widens ESG risk analysis but needs human oversight and model governance.

Q11. What is PCAF data quality scoring? (5)

Meaning. The Partnership for Carbon Accounting Financials (PCAF) standard asks financial institutions to grade the quality of the emissions data behind each financed-emissions figure on a scale of 1 (best) to 5 (weakest).

  • Score 1–2: reported emissions of the borrower/investee (verified = 1, unverified = 2).
  • Score 3: emissions estimated from physical activity data (e.g. energy consumed, tonnes produced) with emission factors.
  • Score 4–5: estimates based on economic activity (revenue or asset value × sector average emission factor), the least precise.

Why it matters. It tells readers how reliable the number is and gives the institution a roadmap to improve data (engaging clients to report). A weighted average data-quality score is usually disclosed. Conclusion. Transparency about data quality is itself a disclosure best practice.

Section B style (10 marks)

Q12. Explain the identification of market, credit and operational risks in sustainable finance with examples. (10)

Introduction. Sustainability risks are not a separate "bucket"; they are risk drivers that show up through traditional financial risk categories. Identification is the first step of the risk cycle (identify → assess → mitigate → monitor), as shown on your Unit 1 slides.

1. Market risk — loss from changes in market prices.

  • Transition shocks: carbon pricing, coal phase-out policy and cheaper renewables reprice thermal power, oil and gas and auto (ICE) stocks ("stranded assets").
  • ESG rating downgrades or index exclusion can trigger passive and active selling.
  • Commodity price spikes after droughts or floods.

2. Credit risk — borrower default.

  • Higher PD when carbon costs squeeze margins or disasters hit borrowers' cash flows.
  • Higher LGD when collateral (coastal property, farmland) loses value.
  • Concentration: agricultural loans in drought-prone districts; MSME clusters on flood plains.

3. Operational risk — loss from failed processes, people, systems or external events.

  • Physical damage to branches, data centres, plants; supply-chain disruption.
  • Conduct risk: mis-selling "green" or "ESG" products (greenwashing penalties).
  • Governance failures: fraud, bribery, data breaches.

4. Other channels: liquidity risk (deposit withdrawals after disasters), reputational risk (financing controversial projects), legal/liability risk (climate litigation).

Identification tools. Materiality assessment; risk registers; heat maps (likelihood × impact); sector exclusion and watch lists; ESG due-diligence questionnaires; scenario analysis (NGFS); geospatial hazard mapping; controversy screening.

Indian example. RBI's draft Disclosure Framework on Climate-related Financial Risks (2024) and its guidance on climate scenario analysis and stress testing ask regulated entities to identify how physical and transition risks flow into credit, market, liquidity and operational risk.

Conclusion. Proper identification lets banks and investors use existing risk tools (PD/LGD, VaR, operational-loss databases) to price and manage sustainability risk.

Q13. "Corporate disclosure practices determine the credibility of sustainable finance." Discuss. (10)

Introduction. Sustainable finance runs on information: investors price green bonds, ESG funds pick stocks and banks price SLLs on the basis of what companies disclose. Weak disclosure leads to mispricing and greenwashing.

Features of good disclosure practice.

  1. Framework-based: BRSR (mandatory), GRI, ISSB/TCFD, SASB, CDP.
  2. Material: focuses on issues that matter for the sector.
  3. Quantified and comparable: Scope 1, 2 and material Scope 3; intensities; multi-year trends; stated methodology.
  4. Forward-looking: targets, transition plans, scenario results.
  5. Balanced: reports negatives (spills, fatalities, fines), not only successes.
  6. Assured: third-party assurance (BRSR Core reasonable assurance).
  7. Consistent across channels: annual report, sustainability report, website, investor presentations say the same thing.
  8. Timely and accessible.

How poor disclosure harms credibility. Rating divergence, greenwashing (Volkswagen, DWS), selective disclosure, "greenhushing".

Regulatory responses. SEBI's BRSR and BRSR Core; SEBI's green debt framework with post-issue allocation and impact reporting; SEBI ESG fund rules (at least 65% in listed entities with assured BRSR Core disclosure for certain categories); RBI's draft climate disclosure framework; ISSB adoption in many jurisdictions.

Indian practice. Infosys, ITC and HDFC Bank publish integrated or ESG reports referencing GRI and TCFD alongside BRSR (see Unit 5 cases).

Conclusion. Disclosure is the "infrastructure" of sustainable finance; credible, assured and comparable disclosure is what turns ESG claims into investable information.

Q14. Discuss the future trends in sustainable finance: AI, blockchain and emerging ESG analysis. (10)

Introduction. Unit 4 and Unit 5 both list future trends; the exam can ask them together.

A. AI in risk management — NLP on disclosures, satellite monitoring, ML-based emissions estimation, greenwashing detection, climate credit scoring, faster scenario analysis. Limits: bias, explainability, model risk.

B. Blockchain for ESG transparency — tamper-evident records of green bond proceeds and impact (Hong Kong's tokenised green bonds, 2023 and 2024); supply-chain traceability; carbon-credit registries avoiding double counting; smart contracts that automate SLB coupon adjustments. Limits: data entering the chain may still be wrong, legal recognition, scalability.

C. Emerging trends in ESG analysis — ISSB global baseline; mandatory assurance (BRSR Core); transition finance and transition plans; nature and biodiversity (TNFD); double materiality; Scope 3 and value-chain data; taxonomies (including India's work on a climate finance taxonomy); carbon markets (India's CCTS); just transition; anti-greenwashing rules; digital bonds.

Indian example. RBI's wholesale CBDC pilot (2022) settled government-securities transactions; SEBI's BRSR Core; India's sovereign green bonds.

Conclusion. Technology plus regulation is making ESG data faster, cheaper and more trustworthy, which is the precondition for scaling sustainable finance.

Section C style (15-mark case)

Case 5. Greenfield Asset Management's ESG fund (15)

Case. Greenfield AMC runs an ESG equity fund of ₹100 crore. Holdings: Company A (cement) ₹50 crore, EVIC ₹1,000 crore, emissions 4,00,000 tCO2e, revenue ₹2,000 crore; Company B (auto parts) ₹30 crore, EVIC ₹600 crore, emissions 60,000 tCO2e, revenue ₹900 crore; Company C (IT services) ₹20 crore, EVIC ₹800 crore, emissions 8,000 tCO2e, revenue ₹1,600 crore. A newspaper alleges the fund is "greenwashing" because half its money is in cement. (a) Compute financed emissions, carbon footprint and WACI. (6) (b) Is the fund greenwashing? (5) (c) Recommend actions. (4)

(a) Computation

Co.AF = Invested ÷ EVICFinanced emissions (tCO2e)WeightIntensity (t/₹ cr revenue)Weight × intensity
A50 ÷ 1,000 = 0.050.05 × 4,00,000 = 20,0000.504,00,000 ÷ 2,000 = 200100
B30 ÷ 600 = 0.050.05 × 60,000 = 3,0000.3060,000 ÷ 900 = 66.6720
C20 ÷ 800 = 0.0250.025 × 8,000 = 2000.208,000 ÷ 1,600 = 51
Total23,2001.00121

Carbon footprint = 23,200 ÷ 100 = 232 tCO2e per ₹ crore invested. WACI = 121 tCO2e per ₹ crore of revenue. Company A contributes 20,000 ÷ 23,200 = 86.2% of financed emissions.

(b) Is it greenwashing? Not automatically. Greenwashing is a mismatch between claims and reality. Holding cement is consistent with an ESG integration / best-in-class strategy if A is a sector leader with a credible transition plan (alternative fuels, clinker substitution, waste-heat recovery, CCUS pilots) and the fund's documents say so. It is greenwashing if the fund is marketed as "low-carbon" or "climate-aligned" while its WACI is well above its benchmark, or if its name implies exclusions it does not apply. SEBI's ESG fund rules require the scheme's name to reflect its strategy (e.g. exclusion, integration, best-in-class, impact, transition), portfolio holdings in companies with BRSR disclosures, and stewardship reporting.

(c) Recommendations. (1) Disclose financed emissions, footprint, WACI and data-quality scores against a benchmark; (2) state clearly that the fund follows a best-in-class/transition strategy; (3) engage with A on an SBTi-style target and vote on climate resolutions; (4) set a WACI reduction target (e.g. glide path); (5) consider tilting weight to lower-intensity leaders if the transition plan is weak; (6) obtain independent review of ESG processes.

Conclusion. The numbers show concentrated carbon exposure; credibility depends on honest labelling, engagement and transparent metrics rather than simply divesting.

Unit 5 — Case Studies

Syllabus: future trends (AI in risk management, blockchain for ESG transparency, emerging trends in ESG analysis) and the sustainable-investment perspectives of HDFC Bank, Infosys, ITC, JSW Steel and Vedanta Ltd. (9 teaching hours; CO5.) Your professor's "Unit 5 – Student Presentation Topics: Indicative Pointers" lists 15 topics; each is profiled below, pointer by pointer, followed by a model exam answer. The five company cases come after the topics.

Section C (15 marks) is a compulsory case study. Expect either a company case (one of the five corporates) or an instrument case (an SLB or green bond with numbers). Practise both types from the Unit 5 case kit.

The 15 presentation topics

From your course material: Unit 5 – Presentation Topics Indicative Pointers (topics 1–15). Every pointer listed by the professor is covered under its own heading below.

Topic 1. Sustainability-Linked Bonds: concept, evolution and structure

Meaning and concept

An SLB is a bond whose financial or structural characteristics (usually the coupon) change depending on whether the issuer achieves predefined sustainability performance targets (SPTs) measured by key performance indicators (KPIs) by a set date. It is forward-looking and performance-based, and its proceeds are for general corporate purposes.

Evolution of the SLB market

  • 2019: Enel (Italy) issues the first "SDG-linked" bond (US$1.5 billion) with a 25 bps step-up if its renewable-capacity target was missed.
  • June 2020: ICMA publishes the Sustainability-Linked Bond Principles, standardising the market.
  • 2021: the European Central Bank begins accepting SLBs as collateral and for asset purchases when KPIs relate to EU Taxonomy objectives or SDGs — a major boost. Rapid growth; Indian issuers UltraTech Cement and JSW Steel issue US-dollar SLBs.
  • 2022: first sovereign SLBs — Chile and Uruguay (Uruguay's includes both step-up and step-down).
  • 2023 onwards: issuance slows amid criticism of weak targets, small penalties and call options that let issuers redeem before the observation date; ICMA adds guidance, and investors scrutinise ambition more closely.

Key characteristics

  • General-purpose proceeds; no ring-fencing.
  • One or more KPIs that are core, material, measurable, verifiable and benchmarkable.
  • SPTs with a baseline, a target level and an observation (trigger) date.
  • A financial consequence (step-up/step-down/premium payment).
  • Annual reporting and mandatory external verification of KPI performance.

Basic structure

ElementIllustration (steel company)
Issue₹500 crore, 5-year, 7.50% coupon, annual
KPIScope 1 + 2 emission intensity (tCO2/tonne crude steel)
Baseline2.50 tCO2/tcs in FY2020 (verified)
SPT2.20 tCO2/tcs by end of year 3 (observation date)
Consequence+25 bps coupon for years 4 and 5 if SPT missed
VerificationIndependent assurance provider, annually and at observation date
ReportingAnnual SLB progress report

Coupon and financial mechanisms

Step-up (penalty if missed, most common: 25 bps); step-down (reward if met; rarer because investors lose yield); premium payment at maturity or on redemption; change in redemption price. The step-up usually applies from the coupon after the observation date to maturity.

Conventional bond vs SLB

BasisConventionalSLB
CouponFixedContingent on KPI performance
Sustainability commitmentNoneKPI/SPT with verification
DisclosureFinancialFinancial + annual KPI reporting
Investor baseGeneralGeneral + ESG investors
PricingCredit riskCredit risk + value of the contingent step-up

Roles

  • Issuer: selects KPIs, sets SPTs, publishes an SLB framework, reports annually, obtains verification, pays step-up if missed.
  • Investors: assess ambition and materiality, price the bond, engage with the issuer, monitor reports.
  • External reviewers: pre-issuance second-party opinion on KPI relevance and SPT ambition; post-issuance verification of KPI performance (mandatory under SLBP).
  • Arrangers/underwriters and trustees: structure the bond, enforce terms.

Illustrative example

JSW Steel's US$500 million SLB (2021) was linked to reducing CO2 emission intensity at its Vijayanagar works by 2030; UltraTech Cement's US$400 million SLB (2021) was India's first, linked to cutting emission intensity. Numerically (worked example 3.5): a ₹500 crore SLB missing its SPT pays an extra ₹2.5 crore over two years.

Model exam answer: "Explain the concept, evolution and structure of Sustainability-Linked Bonds." (10)

Introduction. Sustainability-linked bonds are performance-based debt instruments whose coupon or other terms vary with the issuer's achievement of predefined sustainability targets. Unlike green bonds, proceeds are general-purpose; the sustainability commitment applies to the issuer as a whole.

Evolution. Enel's 2019 SDG-linked bond; ICMA SLB Principles (June 2020); ECB eligibility (2021); corporate issuance boom including India's UltraTech and JSW Steel (2021); sovereign SLBs by Chile and Uruguay (2022); slower growth since 2023 amid concerns about ambition.

Characteristics. General-purpose proceeds; material KPIs; ambitious SPTs with a baseline and observation date; coupon step-up/step-down; annual reporting; mandatory verification.

Structure (draw the flow: Issue → KPI + baseline → SPT + date → verification → coupon unchanged or stepped up). ICMA's five components: KPI selection, SPT calibration, bond characteristics, reporting, verification.

Roles. Issuer, investors, external reviewers (SPO and verifiers), arrangers.

Comparison with conventional bond (table).

Illustration. ₹500 crore, 5-year, 7.50% SLB with 25 bps step-up for years 4–5 if missed: coupon rises from ₹37.50 crore to ₹38.75 crore; extra cost ₹2.5 crore.

Conclusion. SLBs let any issuer, including hard-to-abate sectors, raise sustainability-themed capital, but their credibility depends on ambitious SPTs, meaningful penalties and independent verification.

Topic 2. Sustainability Performance Targets and KPIs in SLBs

Meaning of SPTs

A Sustainability Performance Target (SPT) is a measurable, time-bound improvement target on a KPI that the issuer commits to achieve by a specified observation date (e.g. "reduce Scope 1+2 intensity to 2.20 tCO2/tcs by 31 March 2027").

Key Performance Indicators

A KPI is the metric used to measure sustainability performance (e.g. tCO2/tonne, % renewable electricity, water use per unit, % women in senior management). ICMA requires KPIs to be relevant, core and material to the issuer's business; measurable/quantifiable on a consistent methodological basis; externally verifiable; and benchmarkable.

Selecting appropriate KPIs

  1. Start from the materiality assessment: the issue with the largest sustainability impact and financial relevance (for steel: GHG intensity; for a bank: financed emissions or green lending; for an IT firm: renewable share and diversity).
  2. Prefer KPIs already disclosed with historical data (at least 3 years).
  3. Cover the largest share of emissions (a Scope 1+2 KPI where Scope 3 dominates may be weak).
  4. Align with science-based pathways and sector standards.

Baseline determination

The baseline is the KPI's verified value in a reference year, ideally recent and representative (not an unusually bad year, which would make targets easy). Methodology, boundary (which plants), and recalculation policy for acquisitions/divestments must be fixed in the framework.

Target-setting methodology

  • Ambition test: beyond business-as-usual trajectory; compared to own past performance, peers and science (e.g. SBTi, IEA net-zero sector pathways).
  • Calibration: absolute or intensity; linear or compound path; interim targets.
  • Timeline: observation date(s) before maturity, leaving enough coupons for the step-up to bite.
Required annual reduction (linear) = (Baseline − Target) ÷ Years
Compound annual rate = (Target ÷ Baseline)1/Years − 1

Worked example U5.1. Baseline 2.50 tCO2/tcs (FY2020). SPT: 30% reduction by FY2030. Find the target level, the linear annual reduction and the compound annual rate.

  1. Target = 2.50 × (1 − 0.30) = 1.75 tCO2/tcs.
  2. Linear reduction = (2.50 − 1.75) ÷ 10 = 0.075 tCO2/tcs per year.
  3. Compound rate = (1.75 ÷ 2.50)1/10 − 1 = 0.70.1 − 1 = −3.50% per year.
  4. Check at observation: if verified FY2030 intensity is 1.80, then 1.80 > 1.75 → SPT missed → step-up applies.

Measurement and verification

KPI data collected under recognised methodologies (GHG Protocol; ISO 14064), reported annually, and verified by an independent assurance provider (limited or reasonable assurance) at least annually and at every observation date. Verification report is published.

Consequences of achieving or missing targets

  • Achieved: coupon unchanged (or step-down where structured); reputational gain; possibly lower future cost of capital.
  • Missed: coupon step-up (e.g. +25 bps) for remaining coupons, or premium payment at maturity; reputational damage; investor engagement.
  • Failure to report/verify: generally treated as a miss (triggers the step-up).

Examples of KPIs used

IssuerKPI
EnelShare of renewable installed capacity; later also GHG intensity
SuzanoGHG emission intensity
UltraTech CementScope 1 emission intensity (per tonne of cementitious material)
JSW SteelCO2 emission intensity at Vijayanagar works
Chile (sovereign)Absolute GHG emissions; share of non-conventional renewable electricity
Uruguay (sovereign)GHG emission intensity of GDP; native forest area

Challenges in designing meaningful SPTs

  • Low ambition (targets the issuer would meet anyway).
  • Cherry-picked KPIs (immaterial or narrow boundary).
  • Convenient baselines (high-emission year).
  • Observation dates near maturity (step-up applies to few coupons) or call options before the observation date.
  • Small step-ups relative to the cost of real transition.
  • Data quality and verification capacity; Scope 3 omission.
  • Methodology changes and M&A distorting comparisons.
Model exam answer: "Discuss the role of KPIs and SPTs in SLBs and the challenges in designing them." (10)

Introduction. KPIs and SPTs are the heart of an SLB: the KPI is what is measured; the SPT is the level to be achieved by the observation date; missing it triggers a financial consequence.

KPI criteria (ICMA): relevant, core and material; measurable; externally verifiable; benchmarkable. Selection: from materiality; historical data; coverage of main emissions; science alignment.

Baseline: verified, representative year; fixed boundary and recalculation policy. Target-setting: beyond BAU; benchmarked to peers and science; timeline with observation date.

Illustration: baseline 2.50, 30% cut by FY2030 → SPT 1.75; linear 0.075/year; compound −3.5% a year; verified 1.80 → missed → step-up.

Measurement and verification: GHG Protocol, annual reporting, independent assurance.

Consequences: step-up/step-down/premium; reputation.

Examples: Enel, Suzano, UltraTech, JSW Steel, Chile, Uruguay.

Challenges: low ambition, cherry-picking, baselines, late observation dates, call options, small penalties, data.

Conclusion. An SLB is only as credible as its SPT; investors should test ambition against science and peers.

Topic 3. SLBs: pricing, coupon adjustments and investor returns

Relationship between sustainability performance and bond economics

The coupon of an SLB is a function of verified KPI performance. Poor sustainability performance raises the issuer's interest cost; good performance keeps it low (or lowers it with step-down). The bond therefore embeds a contingent payoff — like an option the issuer "writes" to investors on its own sustainability performance.

Coupon step-up and step-down mechanisms

  • Step-up: coupon increases by a fixed spread (commonly 25 bps; sometimes 12.5–75 bps) from the first coupon after the observation date until maturity if the SPT is missed.
  • Step-down: coupon decreases if the SPT is met or exceeded (e.g. Uruguay's sovereign SLB has both).
  • Multiple KPIs: each missed SPT can add its own step-up (e.g. 12.5 bps each).

Financial incentives and penalties

The penalty = step-up × face value × remaining years. For the issuer it is a cost of failure; for investors it is compensation for the higher sustainability (and possibly credit) risk revealed by the miss.

Impact on investor returns

If the SPT is missed, investors receive higher coupons, so their yield rises. Paradoxically, investors gain when the issuer fails — one reason critics say the structure has misaligned incentives. Market price also reacts: the bond's price rises when the step-up becomes likely (higher future cash flows), all else equal.

Credit risk and sustainability risk

Missing an SPT may signal weaker transition management, higher carbon-cost exposure and therefore higher credit risk. So the step-up may be offset by a wider credit spread (lower price). Credit risk dominates pricing; sustainability performance adds a second, smaller layer.

Pricing considerations

  • Issuer's credit rating and yield curve (main driver).
  • Probability of missing the SPT (ambition) and size/timing of step-up.
  • "Greenium" from ESG demand (may lower yield slightly).
  • Call options before the observation date (reduce value of step-up).
  • Liquidity and issue size.
Price = Σ Ct/(1 + y)t + F/(1 + y)n, with Ct = base coupon before observation and base + step-up after (if missed)
Expected price = p × Pmissed + (1 − p) × Pmet

Worked example U5.2 — numerical illustration of coupon adjustment. Face ₹1,000, 5-year, 6% annual coupon SLB; step-up 50 bps for years 4 and 5 if the SPT (tested at end of year 3) is missed. Market yield for the issuer is 6%. (a) Price if the SPT is met; (b) price if missed; (c) expected price if the market assigns a 40% probability of a miss; (d) investor's yield if they paid par and the SPT is missed.

  1. (a) Met: cash flows 60, 60, 60, 60, 1,060 at 6% → price = ₹1,000.00 (coupon = yield).
  2. (b) Missed: cash flows 60, 60, 60, 65, 1,065. PVs at 6%: 56.6038 + 53.3998 + 50.3772 + 51.4861 + 795.8300 = ₹1,007.70. The step-up is worth ₹7.70 per bond today.
  3. (c) Expected price = 0.4 × 1,007.70 + 0.6 × 1,000.00 = ₹1,003.08.
  4. (d) Yield to maturity on cash flows 60, 60, 60, 65, 1,065 bought at ₹1,000 = 6.18% (Excel: =IRR({-1000,60,60,60,65,1065})), about 18 bps above the 6.00% if met.

Worked example U5.3 — step-down (reverse structure). A 5-year 7% SLB steps down by 25 bps in years 4–5 if the SPT is met. Market yield 7%. Price if met?

  1. Cash flows if met: 70, 70, 70, 67.5, 1,067.5.
  2. Price at 7% = ₹996.31 (vs ₹1,000 if not met) — investors lose ₹3.69 of value when the issuer succeeds, which is why step-downs are rare.

Worked example U5.4 — issuer's present-value cost. ₹1,000 crore, 7-year, 8% SLB with 50 bps step-up in years 5–7 if missed. Extra cash cost and PV at 8%?

  1. Extra coupon = 1,000 × 0.005 = ₹5 crore per year for 3 years = ₹15 crore.
  2. PV = 5/1.085 + 5/1.086 + 5/1.087 = ₹9.47 crore.

Risk-return characteristics

Return = base coupon + contingent step-up; risk = issuer credit risk + sustainability-performance uncertainty + verification risk + call risk. The distribution is slightly positively skewed for investors (they get more if the issuer misses).

Investor considerations when evaluating SLBs

  1. Materiality and ambition of KPIs/SPTs (versus peers and science).
  2. Size and timing of step-up (does it bite?).
  3. Call features before observation.
  4. Quality of verifier and reporting.
  5. Issuer's credible transition plan and capex.
  6. Credit fundamentals.
  7. Fit with the investor's ESG mandate (some funds do not count SLBs as "green").
Model exam answer: "Explain SLB pricing, coupon adjustments and investor returns with a numerical illustration." (10)

Introduction. An SLB links bond economics to sustainability: missing an SPT triggers a coupon step-up; meeting it may trigger a step-down.

Mechanisms: step-up (common, 25 bps), step-down (rare), premium payment. Incentive: penalty = step-up × face × remaining years.

Numerical: ₹1,000, 6%, 5-year SLB, +50 bps in years 4–5 if missed, yield 6%: price if met ₹1,000; if missed ₹1,007.70; expected at 40% miss probability ₹1,003.08; investor yield if missed 6.18%.

Credit vs sustainability risk: a miss may widen the credit spread, offsetting the step-up.

Pricing factors: rating, miss probability, step-up size/timing, greenium, calls, liquidity.

Investor considerations: ambition, materiality, verification, call features, transition plan.

Conclusion. The coupon mechanism creates accountability but is a modest penalty; credibility rests on ambitious SPTs and meaningful step-ups.

Topic 4. Green Bonds: concept, characteristics and market development

Definition and purpose

A green bond is a fixed-income instrument whose proceeds are exclusively used to finance or refinance eligible green projects, aligned with ICMA's Green Bond Principles. Purpose: channel capital to climate and environmental projects, give investors transparency, and signal the issuer's commitment.

Evolution

  • 2007: EIB's Climate Awareness Bond; 2008: World Bank's first green bond (with SEB, for Scandinavian investors).
  • 2013–14: first large corporate issues; ICMA Green Bond Principles (2014); Climate Bonds Standard.
  • 2015: India's first (Yes Bank, Exim Bank); Paris Agreement drives growth.
  • 2016–17: first sovereign green bonds (Poland 2016, France 2017); SEBI's green debt norms (2017).
  • 2020s: NextGenerationEU green bonds; EU Green Bond Standard; India's Sovereign Green Bonds (2023); tokenised green bonds (Hong Kong, 2023).

Characteristics

Use-of-proceeds; same credit risk as issuer's other debt (standard type); framework; external review; annual allocation and impact reporting; often listed on dedicated green segments.

Green vs conventional bond

See comparison table in 3.3.

Types

Standard green use-of-proceeds; green revenue; green project; secured green (incl. securitised, covered); plus sovereign, municipal, masala, certified climate bonds.

Major issuers and investors

Issuers: supranationals and DFIs (EIB, World Bank, ADB), sovereigns (France, Germany, Italy, UK, India), municipalities, banks (SBI, Yes Bank), utilities and renewable developers (NTPC, IREDA, ReNew, Greenko), corporates (Apple, Alphabet). Investors: pension funds, insurers, asset managers with ESG mandates, central banks, banks' treasuries, retail (via RBI Retail Direct for SGrBs).

Market growth and development

Green bonds are the largest segment of the labelled-bond market; cumulative global issuance runs into trillions of US dollars according to Climate Bonds Initiative data, with Europe the largest issuing region. India's market is smaller but growing, led by renewable developers (often offshore in dollars), PSUs and the sovereign.

Benefits and limitations

See 3.3: diversified investors, possible greenium, reputation vs greenwashing risk, costs, lack of penalty, taxonomy gaps.

Indian and international examples

  • India: Yes Bank (2015, first), Exim Bank (2015, first dollar), NTPC green masala (2016), SBI certified climate bond (2018), IREDA, Sovereign Green Bonds (2023, ₹16,000 crore).
  • International: EIB (2007), World Bank (2008), France's sovereign green OAT (2017), NextGenerationEU green bonds, Hong Kong tokenised green bond (2023).
Model exam answer: "Explain the concept, characteristics and market development of green bonds, with Indian examples." (10)

Definition and purpose (ICMA). Evolution from EIB 2007 → World Bank 2008 → GBP 2014 → India 2015 → sovereigns 2016–17 → SEBI norms 2017 → India SGrB 2023. Characteristics (use of proceeds, same credit risk, framework, external review, reporting). Comparison with conventional bonds (table). Types (four ICMA types + sovereign, masala). Issuers and investors. Market growth (trillions globally; India growing). Benefits and limitations. Indian examples (Yes Bank, Exim, NTPC, SBI, IREDA, SGrB ₹16,000 crore with a small greenium). Conclusion: green bonds are the flagship sustainable-finance instrument, but credibility depends on principles, review and reporting.

Topic 5. Green Bond Principles and the Green Bond issuance framework

Green Bond Principles

Voluntary process guidelines published by ICMA (first 2014, updated periodically) that recommend transparency, disclosure and reporting for green bonds. Four core components + two key recommendations (see 3.3).

Core components of issuance

  1. Use of proceeds — eligible categories; environmental benefits assessed and quantified; share of refinancing and look-back period disclosed.
  2. Process for project evaluation and selection — environmental objectives; eligibility criteria and exclusion criteria; processes to identify and manage E&S risks; alignment with taxonomies; a green bond committee (treasury + sustainability + business).
  3. Management of proceeds — sub-account/sub-portfolio or other tracking; periodic adjustment to match allocations; temporary placement of unallocated funds (e.g. cash, G-Secs) disclosed; recommended verification by auditor.
  4. Reporting — annual allocation report (projects, amounts) and impact report (expected/actual outcomes using quantitative indicators, methodology and assumptions), until full allocation and on material developments.

External review and certification

FormWhat it does
Second-party opinion (SPO)Independent institution assesses alignment of framework with GBP and the issuer's strategy
VerificationChecks against a set of criteria, e.g. post-issuance allocation
CertificationAgainst a recognised standard (e.g. CBI Climate Bonds Standard)
Scoring/ratingGrades the bond or framework (e.g. CICERO shades of green for India's SGrB framework)

Green Bond Framework

A public document summarising the issuer's sustainability strategy, the four components (eligible categories, selection process, proceeds management, reporting), and external review arrangements. SEBI's 2023 framework requires Indian issuers to disclose similar information in the offer document.

Importance of transparency and disclosure

Investors cannot observe where money goes; transparency substitutes for trust. Disclosure prevents greenwashing, allows impact comparison, supports index inclusion and regulatory compliance, and builds a track record that lowers future issuance costs.

Issuance process

Framework → SPO → rating and documentation → listing (SEBI disclosures) → allocation → reporting → post-issuance verification (Figure 6).

Model exam answer: "Explain the Green Bond Principles and the issuance framework." (10)

Introduction — ICMA's voluntary GBP promote integrity. Four components with detail (use of proceeds; evaluation and selection; management of proceeds; reporting). Key recommendations — green bond framework and external review (SPO, verification, certification, scoring). Issuance process flow diagram. Indian regulation — SEBI green debt framework (2017, revised 2023) with greenwashing do's and don'ts. Example — India's Sovereign Green Bond framework: eligible categories, Green Finance Working Committee for project selection, CICERO "Medium Green" review, annual allocation and impact reporting. Importance of transparency. Conclusion — the principles convert a promise into a verifiable process.

Topic 6. Green bond eligible projects and environmental impact assessment

Categories of eligible green projects (and impact indicators)

CategoryTypical projectsImpact indicators
Renewable energySolar parks, wind farms, small hydro, rooftop solar, transmission for REMW installed; MWh generated; tCO2e avoided
Energy efficiencyLED street lighting, efficient motors, smart grids, storageMWh saved; % reduction; tCO2e avoided
Clean transportationMetro rail, electric buses, EV charging, rail electrificationPassengers/passenger-km; tCO2e avoided; number of EVs
Sustainable water and waste managementWastewater treatment, desalination, recycling plants, waste-to-energym³ water treated/saved; tonnes waste recycled or diverted
Climate adaptationFlood defences, early-warning systems, drought-resistant irrigationPeople/hectares protected; losses avoided
Green buildingsIGBC/GRIHA/LEED-certified buildingsCertification level; kWh/m²; water saved
Others (GBP)Pollution control, sustainable land use, biodiversity, circular economyEmissions reduced, hectares restored, materials recovered

Measuring environmental outcomes

Use ICMA's Harmonised Framework for Impact Reporting: report expected (ex-ante) and actual (ex-post) impacts, methodology, baselines (e.g. grid emission factor), share of project financed (pro-rata attribution), and assumptions. Distinguish outputs (MW installed) from outcomes (MWh, tCO2e avoided).

Attributed avoided emissions = Project avoided emissions × (Bond financing ÷ Total project cost)

Worked example U5.5. A 300 MW solar project (CUF 20%, grid EF 0.71) avoids 373,176 tCO2/year (worked example 3.2). The green bond financed ₹450 crore of a ₹1,500 crore total project cost. Attributed impact?

  1. Share = 450 ÷ 1,500 = 30%.
  2. Attributed = 373,176 × 0.30 = 111,952.8 tCO2/year.

Reporting 100% of the project's impact when the bond financed 30% overstates impact — a common reporting error.

Environmental impact assessment (EIA)

Projects must also be screened for negative impacts: land acquisition, biodiversity (e.g. solar parks on grasslands), water use, displacement. India's EIA Notification (under EPA 1986) requires clearance for specified projects. Good frameworks exclude projects with severe E&S harm and apply "do no significant harm" logic.

Examples of projects financed through green bonds

India's Sovereign Green Bond framework covers solar and wind, green hydrogen, metro rail and electric buses, afforestation, energy efficiency, and sustainable water and waste projects. Corporate green bonds by Indian renewable developers finance solar and wind parks; SBI's certified climate bond financed solar and wind projects.

Model exam answer: "Explain green-bond eligible projects and how environmental impact is measured." (10)

Introduction (use-of-proceeds principle) → category table with projects and indicators → outputs vs outcomes → impact methodology (Harmonised Framework, baselines, pro-rata attribution) → numerical: 373,176 t avoided, 30% financed → 111,952.8 t attributed → EIA and "do no significant harm" → Indian examples (SGrB categories) → challenges (estimation, double counting, data) → conclusion.

Topic 7. Green bonds and the UN Sustainable Development Goals

Overview of SDGs

17 goals, 169 targets, adopted 2015 for 2030 (full list in 1.7).

Relationship between sustainable finance and SDGs

The SDGs provide the "what" (outcomes); sustainable finance provides the "how" (capital). Investors use SDG mapping to communicate impact; ICMA publishes a high-level mapping of GBP/SBP categories to SDGs.

Mapping green bond projects to SDGs

SDGGreen bond projectsIndicators
SDG 7 — Affordable and Clean EnergySolar, wind, rooftop solar, energy efficiencyMW, MWh, households electrified
SDG 9 — Industry, Innovation and InfrastructureGreen hydrogen, efficient industry, resilient infrastructure, transmissionCapacity built, energy saved
SDG 11 — Sustainable Cities and CommunitiesMetro rail, e-buses, green buildings, urban flood controlPassengers, certified m², people protected
SDG 12 — Responsible Consumption and ProductionCircular economy, recycling, waste-to-resourceTonnes recycled, material efficiency
SDG 13 — Climate ActionAll mitigation and adaptation projectstCO2e avoided; resilience outcomes
Also 6, 14, 15Water treatment; marine protection (blue bonds); afforestationm³, hectares

Examples

India's Sovereign Green Bonds (solar → SDG 7, 13; metro → SDG 11; afforestation → SDG 15); World Bank green bonds reporting SDG contributions; municipal green bonds for water and sewage (SDG 6, 11).

Challenges in measuring SDG contribution

  • No single standard metric per SDG; indicators designed for countries, not projects.
  • Attribution and double counting (several financiers claim the same impact).
  • "SDG-washing" — claiming contribution to many SDGs superficially.
  • Trade-offs between SDGs (hydro dams help SDG 7 but may harm SDG 15).
  • Long-term and indirect outcomes hard to observe; data gaps in developing countries.
Model exam answer: "How do green bonds contribute to the SDGs? Discuss the measurement challenges." (10)

Introduction (SDGs + financing gap) → relationship between SF and SDGs → mapping table for SDGs 7, 9, 11, 12, 13 (+6, 14, 15) → Indian and global examples → measurement approach (IRIS+, ICMA mapping, impact reports) → challenges (metrics, attribution, SDG-washing, trade-offs, data) → conclusion: disciplined impact reporting turns SDG labels into evidence.

Topic 8. Greenwashing, transparency and credibility in green bond markets

Meaning of greenwashing

False, misleading, unsubstantiated or incomplete claims about the environmental benefit of a bond, issuer or project (see 4.5).

Why it is a concern in green bonds

Investors pay (via greenium and extra due diligence) for environmental benefit they cannot directly see; if proceeds are misused, capital is misallocated, investors are misled, and the whole market's credibility suffers ("market for lemons").

Misallocation of proceeds

  • Funding ineligible or marginally green projects ("efficiency" at fossil-fuel plants).
  • Refinancing old assets without disclosure (no new impact).
  • Long periods of unallocated proceeds.
  • Fungibility: green bond funds free up other money for brown activities.

Environmental claims and their verification

Claims must be backed by eligibility criteria, taxonomy alignment, measured impact with stated methodology, and third-party verification.

Transparency and disclosure

Published framework, allocation reports by project, impact reports with methodology, disclosure of refinancing share and unallocated funds.

Role of external reviewers

SPO before issuance; verification of allocation after; certification (CBI); reviewers themselves face conflict-of-interest concerns (paid by issuer), so their independence and methodology matter.

Impact reporting

Quantified, comparable outcomes (tCO2e avoided, MWh), pro-rata attribution, ex-post verification.

Regulatory responses

  • SEBI (2023): revised green debt framework with explicit do's and don'ts: e.g. do not use misleading labels, do not select only favourable data, do not make exaggerated claims; ongoing disclosure; independent reviewer.
  • EU: Green Bond Standard (EuGB) requiring taxonomy alignment and registered external reviewers; SFDR.
  • RBI: green deposit framework requires third-party verification and impact reports.
  • IOSCO recommendations on ESG ratings and data providers; SEBI's ERP regulation.

Examples of greenwashing concerns

  • Repsol's 2017 green bond financing efficiency upgrades at its oil refineries was excluded from some green bond indices because investors did not consider fossil-fuel efficiency "green".
  • China's earlier green bond catalogue included "clean coal" projects; the 2021 revision removed them to align with international norms.
  • Asset managers penalised for ESG misstatements (DWS, 2023).

Measures to improve credibility

  1. Adopt a recognised taxonomy and exclusion list.
  2. Independent pre- and post-issuance review; certification.
  3. Project-level allocation and impact reporting with methodology.
  4. Limit and disclose refinancing and look-back periods.
  5. Link green bonds to a credible whole-company transition plan.
  6. Regulatory enforcement and investor stewardship.
  7. Use technology (blockchain tracking of proceeds, satellite verification).
Model exam answer: "Discuss greenwashing in green bond markets and measures to improve credibility." (10)

Definition → why it matters in green bonds (information asymmetry, greenium) → forms: misallocation, refinancing, vague claims, unverified impact → role of transparency, external reviewers, impact reporting → regulatory responses (SEBI 2023 do's and don'ts, EuGB, RBI green deposit verification) → examples (Repsol 2017, China's clean coal removal 2021, DWS) → measures (taxonomy, review, reporting, transition plans, enforcement, technology) → conclusion.

Topic 9. Sustainability-Linked Bonds vs Green Bonds: a comparative analysis

PointerGreen bondSustainability-linked bond
Fundamental conceptUse-of-proceeds: money for green projectsPerformance-based: issuer-level sustainability targets
ApproachWhat the money financesHow the issuer performs
Project eligibilityMust identify eligible green projects (GBP categories)No project eligibility; proceeds general-purpose
KPIs and SPTsNot required (impact indicators reported)Core: material KPIs and ambitious SPTs
Coupon mechanismFixed; unaffected by impactStep-up/step-down linked to SPT

Comparative case study: India's Sovereign Green Bond (2023) vs JSW Steel's SLB (2021)
