Lesson: green bonds suit issuers with green assets; SLBs suit issuers needing a transition. They are complements, not substitutes (some issuers now combine both: "sustainability-linked green bonds").

Model exam answer: "Compare SLBs with green bonds using a case study." (15)

Introduction — both are labelled bonds under ICMA principles; they differ in what is labelled. Concepts of each. Comparison table across the eleven pointers above. Numerical contrast: a ₹500 crore 7.50% green bond costs ₹37.50 crore a year regardless of impact; the same as an SLB with +25 bps if missed costs ₹38.75 crore a year in years 4–5 (extra ₹2.5 crore). Case study (SGrB vs JSW Steel SLB table). Issuer and investor perspectives. Recommendation — choose green bonds when there is an eligible asset pipeline; SLBs for transition; combine for maximum credibility. Conclusion.

Topics 10–13 are the technology block of Unit 5. Think of them as four layers of one stack: blockchain/DLT (Topic 13) is the shared record-keeping ledger; tokenisation (Topic 11) represents a bond as digital tokens on that ledger; smart contracts (Topic 12) are the programs on the ledger that pay coupons and redemptions automatically; a digital bond (Topic 10) is the end product — a bond issued, recorded and settled digitally. The link to sustainable finance: these tools make green-bond proceeds and SLB KPIs traceable and make small-ticket green investing possible.

Topic 10. Digital Bonds: concept, features and transformation of bond markets

Meaning of digital bonds

A digital bond is a debt security whose issuance, ownership record, transfer, settlement and servicing (coupons, redemption) are carried out through digital infrastructure — typically a distributed ledger — so that the bond "lives" natively as a digital record rather than as a paper certificate or as an entry reconciled across several separate intermediary databases.

In India, almost all bonds are already dematerialised (held electronically with NSDL/CDSL, or in RBI's SGL/CSGL accounts for government securities). In the industry sense, "digital bond" usually means something more: a DLT-native bond where the ledger itself is the record of ownership and settlement happens on-chain. In exam answers, mention both meanings: "dematerialised" (electronic records at a central depository) versus "digitally native" (DLT-based).

Traditional vs digital bond issuance

StageTraditional issuanceDigital (DLT) issuance
DocumentationPaper/PDF term sheets, manual book-buildingDigital term sheet; terms coded into smart contract
IntermediariesArrangers, registrar, depository, custodians, paying agent, clearing houseFewer; some roles automated or performed by the ledger operator
Ownership recordCentral depository + custodian books (reconciled)Single shared ledger ("golden record")
SettlementT+1 or longer; separate cash and securities legsNear-instant, atomic delivery-versus-payment possible (T+0)
Coupon paymentPaying agent computes and pays; manual reconciliationSmart contract computes and pays automatically
Minimum ticketOften large (wholesale)Can be fractional
TransparencyLimited, periodic reportsReal-time, auditable trail (to permitted parties)

Digital issuance lifecycle

  1. Structuring: issuer and arranger fix terms; legal framework confirmed (which law recognises the ledger record).
  2. Onboarding: investors complete KYC; wallets/accounts created on a permissioned ledger.
  3. Token/record creation ("minting"): the bond is created on the ledger with its smart contract.
  4. Primary distribution and settlement: investors pay (commercial bank money, tokenised deposits or wholesale CBDC); bond tokens delivered simultaneously (DvP).
  5. Servicing: coupons calculated and paid automatically; corporate actions and reporting on-chain; for green bonds, allocation and impact data can be attached.
  6. Secondary transfer: tokens transferred between whitelisted wallets; ledger updates ownership instantly.
  7. Redemption ("burning"): principal repaid at maturity and tokens destroyed.

Electronic ownership records

Ownership is a ledger entry linked to an investor's digital identity/wallet. Because every node holds the same synchronised copy, there is no need for multiple parties to reconcile their own books. Legal recognition is essential: for example, Germany's Electronic Securities Act (eWpG, 2021) allows electronic securities to be registered in crypto-securities registers; Siemens used it for a €60 million digital bond on a public blockchain in 2023.

Digital settlement

Settlement is the exchange of cash for securities. On a ledger, both legs can be programmed to move together ("atomic" DvP), eliminating the risk that one party delivers and the other does not (principal/settlement risk). The cash leg may be a tokenised deposit, a stablecoin (in some jurisdictions) or a wholesale central bank digital currency. In India, RBI's wholesale e₹ (e₹-W) pilot launched in November 2022 used the digital rupee for settling secondary-market transactions in government securities.

Advantages of digital issuance

  • Lower issuance and servicing costs (fewer intermediaries, less reconciliation).
  • Faster settlement (T+0) and lower counterparty and liquidity needs.
  • Transparency and a single audit trail.
  • Programmability: coupons, step-ups, green reporting automated.
  • Fractional access for smaller investors; wider distribution.
  • Possible 24/7 operation.

Reduction in transaction and settlement time

Traditional corporate bond settlement in India is T+1 on exchanges' RFQ platforms, and cross-border Eurobonds typically settle a few days after pricing. Pilots such as the European Investment Bank's €100 million two-year digital bond (April 2021, with Banque de France settling in a wholesale CBDC experiment) demonstrated same-day issuance and settlement.

Transparency and operational efficiency

One version of the truth, automated corporate actions, instant regulator access (a "regulator node"), and fewer manual errors. For green bonds, investors can see proceeds allocation as it happens.

Challenges and risks

  • Legal and regulatory: recognition of ledger records as legal title; insolvency treatment; cross-border enforceability.
  • Interoperability: multiple ledgers ("digital islands") that do not talk to each other or to legacy systems.
  • Cash leg: lack of on-chain central bank money in most markets.
  • Liquidity: small issue sizes; few secondary markets.
  • Technology and cyber risk: smart-contract bugs, key management, outages.
  • Privacy vs transparency; data-protection laws.
  • Cost of transition and investor unfamiliarity.

Examples of digital bond issuance

IssuerYearFeature
World Bank "bond-i" (with Commonwealth Bank of Australia)2018First bond created, allocated, transferred and managed through its life on a blockchain (A$110 million)
European Investment Bank2021€100 million digital bond on a public blockchain; cash leg via Banque de France CBDC experiment
Government of Hong Kong SAR2023HK$800 million tokenised green bond — first tokenised green bond by a government
Government of Hong Kong SAR2024Second, larger multi-currency digital green bond issue (around HK$6 billion equivalent)
Siemens2023€60 million digital bond under Germany's eWpG
India (related steps)2021–2022RBI Retail Direct (online G-sec access for individuals, 2021); SEBI regulation of Online Bond Platform Providers (2022); RBI e₹-W pilot for G-sec settlement (2022)

Self-check

1. Is a dematerialised NCD held in NSDL a "digital bond" in the DLT sense?

No. It is electronic, but ownership is recorded in a central depository's database and reconciled with depository participants. A DLT-native digital bond uses a shared ledger as the record and can settle atomically.

2. What is atomic DvP?

Delivery-versus-payment where the securities leg and cash leg are executed together in one indivisible transaction on the ledger — either both happen or neither does — removing settlement (principal) risk.

3. Why was Hong Kong's 2023 bond important for sustainable finance?

It combined the green label with tokenisation, showing that DLT can carry green-bond issuance and potentially real-time allocation and impact data, improving transparency.

Model exam answer: "Explain digital bonds and how they are transforming bond markets." (10)

Introduction. A digital bond is a debt security issued, recorded, settled and serviced on digital (usually DLT) infrastructure, so the ledger itself is the record of ownership.

Traditional vs digital (table: documentation, intermediaries, record, settlement, coupons, ticket size, transparency).

Lifecycle: structuring → KYC/onboarding → minting → primary DvP settlement → automated servicing → secondary transfer → redemption/burning (draw a flow).

Transformation of bond markets: (1) settlement from T+1/T+2 towards T+0; (2) lower costs through fewer intermediaries; (3) single golden record ends reconciliation; (4) programmable coupons and SLB step-ups; (5) fractional access widening the investor base; (6) real-time transparency for regulators and green-bond investors.

Examples: World Bank bond-i (2018), EIB (2021), Hong Kong tokenised green bonds (2023, 2024), Siemens (2023); India's RBI Retail Direct, OBPP regulation and wholesale e₹ pilot for G-sec settlement.

Challenges: legal recognition, interoperability, on-chain cash, liquidity, cyber risk, privacy.

Conclusion. Digital bonds are moving from pilots to programmes; for sustainable finance they promise cheaper issuance and verifiable green claims, provided legal and interoperability issues are solved.

Topic 11. Tokenized Bonds: structure, fractional ownership and trading

Concept of tokenization

Tokenization is the process of representing rights to an asset (a bond, share, property, fund unit, carbon credit) as digital tokens on a programmable ledger, so that ownership and transfer of the token represent ownership and transfer of the underlying right.

Tokenized securities and tokenized bonds

A tokenized security is a security whose ownership is recorded as tokens. Two routes: (1) native — the security is issued directly as tokens (digital-native bond); (2) wrapped/backed — an existing conventional security is held by a custodian and tokens representing it are issued. A tokenized bond is a bond represented this way; it remains a bond legally (coupon, maturity, seniority), only the record-keeping and transfer technology change. Distinguish from crypto-assets, which have no underlying legal claim on an issuer.

Structure of a tokenized bond

Representation of ownership through digital tokens

Each token is a unit of the bond with a defined face value. The ledger maps tokens to wallet addresses linked to verified identities. The smart contract encodes rights: coupon rate, dates, maturity, transfer restrictions (only whitelisted investors), and for SLBs the step-up rule. The legal documents must state that the ledger entry is conclusive evidence of title.

Fractional ownership

Because the bond is split into many small tokens, investors can buy a fraction of what would otherwise be a large minimum lot. This widens access (retail and small institutions) and can improve liquidity. The Indian parallel without DLT: SEBI's progressive reduction of the minimum face value of privately placed debt securities (from ₹10 lakh to ₹1 lakh in 2022, and to ₹10,000 for certain plain-vanilla issues in 2024) with the stated aim of widening retail participation.

Worked example U5.6 — tokenising a green bond. A company issues a ₹10 crore green bond with a 7.5% coupon paid half-yearly and tokenises it into tokens of ₹1,000 each. (a) How many tokens? (b) Coupon per token each half-year? (c) An investor buys 25 tokens: investment and half-yearly coupon?

  1. ₹10 crore = ₹10,00,00,000. Tokens = 10,00,00,000 ÷ 1,000 = 1,00,000 tokens.
  2. Annual coupon per token = 1,000 × 7.5% = ₹75; half-yearly = 75 ÷ 2 = ₹37.50 per token.
  3. Investment = 25 × 1,000 = ₹25,000; half-yearly coupon = 25 × 37.50 = ₹937.50.

Contrast: with a conventional private placement at ₹1 lakh face value, the same investor could not buy less than ₹1,00,000.

Issuance and distribution

Issuer appoints arranger and technology provider; legal framework settled; smart contract audited; investors onboarded (KYC/AML) and wallets whitelisted; tokens minted and distributed against payment (DvP); listing on a regulated digital-securities venue where available (e.g. SIX Digital Exchange in Switzerland). Distribution can be direct to a wider investor base.

Trading and transfer of tokens

Transfers are peer-to-peer between whitelisted wallets or on a regulated venue; the smart contract checks eligibility (compliance rules) before allowing a transfer; the ledger updates ownership immediately. Coupons are paid to whoever holds the token on the record date, automatically.

Settlement mechanisms

(1) On-chain DvP with tokenised cash or wholesale CBDC — atomic, T+0; (2) hybrid — token leg on-chain, cash leg through traditional payment systems (RTGS), with the smart contract releasing tokens on confirmation of payment; (3) cross-chain settlement using interoperability protocols (still developing).

Benefits and limitations

BenefitsLimitations
Fractional ownership and wider accessUnclear legal status in many jurisdictions (India has no specific tokenised-securities law yet)
Faster settlement, lower costsThin secondary liquidity despite fractionalisation
Programmable coupons and complianceSmart-contract bugs, key loss, cyber attacks
Transparent, auditable ownership historyInteroperability with legacy depositories
Potential 24/7 transferCustody and investor-protection questions
Green-bond proceeds and impact traceableGarbage-in-garbage-out: the ledger cannot verify off-chain impact data by itself

Real-world tokenized bond examples

  • Hong Kong SAR Government tokenised green bonds (HK$800 million, 2023; multi-currency issue in 2024).
  • European Investment Bank digital bonds (first in 2021, followed by several further digital issues).
  • Siemens €60 million (2023).
  • UBS digital bond listed on SIX Digital Exchange and SIX Swiss Exchange (2022).
  • World Bank bond-i (2018).

Self-check

1. A ₹50 crore bond is tokenised into ₹500 tokens. How many tokens?

50,00,00,000 ÷ 500 = 10,00,000 tokens.

2. Is a tokenized bond riskier in credit terms than the same issuer's conventional bond?

No — credit risk depends on the issuer. Tokenisation adds technology and legal risks, not credit risk.

Model exam answer: "Explain the structure of tokenized bonds and the role of fractional ownership." (10)

Introduction — tokenisation represents asset rights as digital tokens on a ledger; a tokenized bond is a legal bond recorded and transferred as tokens.

Structure (diagram): issuer → smart contract mints tokens → ledger → whitelisted investor wallets; supporting roles (trustee, custodian, oracle, regulator); cash leg (bank money/tokenised deposits/CBDC).

Ownership representation — token-to-wallet mapping; rights coded; legal recognition.

Fractional ownership — worked illustration: ₹10 crore bond → 1,00,000 tokens of ₹1,000; 25 tokens cost ₹25,000 and earn ₹937.50 each half-year at 7.5%. Benefits: access, diversification, liquidity; Indian parallel: SEBI's lower face values for debt securities.

Issuance, trading and settlement — KYC, minting, DvP; peer-to-peer or venue trading with compliance checks; atomic or hybrid settlement.

Benefits and limitations (table). Examples: Hong Kong green bonds, EIB, Siemens, UBS, World Bank bond-i.

Conclusion. Tokenisation can democratise bond investing and make green finance traceable, but legal clarity, liquidity and cyber resilience must come first.

Topic 12. Smart Contracts and automated bond market operations

Concept of smart contracts

A smart contract is a computer program stored on a blockchain that automatically executes pre-agreed actions when specified conditions are met ("if X, then Y"), with the result recorded on the ledger. It is "smart" only in being self-executing; its legal force comes from the underlying legal agreement.

A vending machine: insert the right amount (condition) and the product drops (execution) — no shopkeeper needed. A bond smart contract: on the coupon date (condition), coupon moves to every holder (execution) — no paying agent needed.

Smart contracts in financial markets

Used for automatic DvP settlement, collateral management and margin calls, escrow, trade finance (release payment on digital bill of lading), insurance (parametric payouts when rainfall falls below a threshold), carbon-credit issuance and retirement, and fund-unit subscriptions/redemptions.

Application to bond issuance

The term sheet is translated into code: face value, coupon rate and frequency, day count, record and payment dates, maturity, call options, transfer restrictions, and for SLBs the KPI, SPT, observation date and step-up. At issuance the contract mints tokens against investor payments.

Automated coupon payments

// Illustrative logic (pseudo-code), not production code
on each couponDate:
    rate = baseCoupon                              // e.g. 7.50%
    if bondType == "SLB" and couponDate > observationDate:
        if verifiedKPI(oracle) > SPT:              // target missed
            rate = baseCoupon + stepUp             // e.g. +0.25%
    for each holder on recordDate:
        pay holder.units * faceValue * rate / frequency
on maturityDate:
    pay holder.units * faceValue to each holder; burn tokens

Worked example U5.7 — smart contract applying an SLB step-up. A ₹500 crore, 5-year SLB pays 7.50% annually. If the verified emission intensity at the end of year 3 is above the SPT, the contract adds 25 bps from year 4. The oracle reports the target was missed. An investor holds bonds of face value ₹1 crore. What does the contract pay the investor each year?

  1. Years 1–3: ₹1,00,00,000 × 7.50% = ₹7,50,000 a year.
  2. Years 4–5 (missed): rate = 7.50% + 0.25% = 7.75%; ₹1,00,00,000 × 7.75% = ₹7,75,000 a year.
  3. Year 5 also: principal ₹1,00,00,000 returned; tokens burned.
  4. Issuer's total extra cost on the full issue = 500 × 0.25% × 2 = ₹2.5 crore (matches worked example 3.5).

Automated maturity/redemption

At maturity the contract pays principal (and final coupon) to recorded holders and destroys ("burns") the tokens, closing the bond. Early redemption (call) can be coded with notice periods and call prices.

Compliance and contractual conditions

"Compliance by design": transfers only to KYC-verified, eligible wallets; investor limits (e.g. foreign-investor caps); lock-in periods; tax deduction at source computed automatically; covenants monitored from data feeds; regulator node with read access.

Reduction of manual intervention and operational efficiency

Removes manual coupon calculations, reconciliation between registrar, paying agent and custodians, and paper-based corporate actions. Results: fewer errors, lower cost, faster payments, full audit trail.

Risks associated with smart contracts

  • Code risk: bugs or vulnerabilities (the 2016 "DAO" exploit on Ethereum is the classic example of flawed code being exploited).
  • Oracle risk: the contract trusts external data (KPI values, rates); wrong or manipulated data produce wrong payments.
  • Legal risk: mismatch between code and legal text; which prevails?
  • Rigidity: hard to amend for unforeseen events (restructuring, force majeure).
  • Operational/cyber risk: private-key loss, network outages.
  • Governance: who can upgrade or pause the contract?

Illustrative bond-market application

A green SLB is issued on a permissioned ledger. A verifier signs the annual KPI report; its digital signature is the oracle input. The contract automatically (a) pays coupons, (b) applies a step-up if the SPT is missed, (c) publishes the KPI and allocation data to all holders and the regulator node, and (d) redeems at maturity. This removes the common investor worry that step-ups may be disputed or delayed.

Self-check

1. What is an oracle?

A trusted data feed that brings off-chain information (e.g. a verified KPI, an interest-rate benchmark, rainfall) onto the blockchain for smart contracts to use.

2. Name two risks unique to smart-contract bonds.

Code (bug) risk and oracle (data) risk; also code-versus-legal-text mismatch.

Model exam answer: "How do smart contracts automate bond market operations? Discuss the risks." (10)

Introduction — definition; if-then self-executing code on a ledger; vending-machine analogy.

Applications in financial markets — DvP, collateral, trade finance, parametric insurance, carbon credits.

Bond lifecycle automation: (1) issuance — minting against payment; (2) coupons — automatic computation and payment on record dates; (3) SLB step-ups via oracle (illustration: ₹1 crore holding receives ₹7.50 lakh a year, then ₹7.75 lakh in years 4–5 when the SPT is missed; issuer's extra cost ₹2.5 crore on a ₹500 crore issue); (4) maturity and burning; (5) compliance by design (whitelists, caps, TDS).

Benefits — less manual intervention, lower cost, faster, fewer errors, transparent audit trail, credibility of sustainability-linked terms.

Risks — code bugs (DAO 2016), oracle manipulation, legal enforceability, rigidity, cyber/key risk, governance of upgrades.

Conclusion. Smart contracts can make bond markets "self-servicing" and SLB penalties automatic, but need audited code, trusted oracles and legal backing.

Topic 13. Blockchain and Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT) in bond markets

Fundamentals of blockchain

A blockchain is a type of distributed ledger in which transactions are grouped into blocks, each block containing a cryptographic hash of the previous block, forming a chain; copies are held by many participants (nodes), and new blocks are added only when nodes agree through a consensus mechanism. Changing a past record would change its hash and break the chain, making tampering evident.

Key ideas: decentralisation (no single record keeper), immutability/tamper-evidence, transparency (to permitted parties), cryptographic security (public-private key pairs, digital signatures), and consensus (proof of work, proof of stake, or voting-based methods in permissioned ledgers).

Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT)

DLT is the broader family: a database shared, replicated and synchronised across multiple sites or institutions without a central administrator. Every blockchain is a DLT; not every DLT uses blocks (some use other data structures, e.g. directed acyclic graphs).

Blockchain architecture relevant to financial markets

TypeWho can join / validateUse in bond markets
Public permissionless (e.g. Ethereum)AnyoneSome pilots (EIB 2021, Siemens 2023) with permissioned tokens on top
Permissioned / private (e.g. Hyperledger Fabric, R3 Corda)Only approved institutions; known validatorsPreferred by banks and regulators for privacy, speed and control (e.g. HSBC Orion, Goldman Sachs GS DAP)
ConsortiumGroup of institutions share governanceMarket infrastructures, trade finance networks

Layers: network/infrastructure → consensus → ledger/data → smart contracts → applications (issuance platform, wallets) → interfaces with payment systems and depositories.

Bond issuance using blockchain

Covered in Topics 10–12: smart contract defines the bond; tokens minted; distributed to KYC-verified investors; cash leg settled; servicing automated.

Ownership and transaction records

The ledger stores who owns how many units and every transfer since issuance, time-stamped and signed. This "golden record" replaces separate registers at the registrar, depository, custodians and paying agent.

Clearing and settlement

On DLT, trade execution, clearing and settlement can collapse into a single step: the smart contract checks that the seller has the tokens and the buyer has the cash, then swaps both simultaneously. Net effect: shorter settlement cycles, lower counterparty and settlement risk, lower collateral needs. Clearing houses may still be useful for netting large volumes.

Transparency and auditability

Every permitted participant sees the same time-stamped, signed history; auditors and regulators can be given read-only nodes; green-bond allocations can be linked to project disbursements and impact data, reducing the scope for misallocation (see Topic 8 on greenwashing).

Reduction of intermediaries

Roles of registrar, paying agent and some custodial and reconciliation functions can be automated. Intermediaries do not disappear entirely: arrangers, trustees, KYC providers, custodians of private keys, and technology operators remain, but their costs and hand-offs fall.

Security and privacy considerations

  • Security strengths: cryptographic signatures, replication (no single point of failure), tamper-evidence.
  • Security weaknesses: private-key theft or loss, smart-contract bugs, 51% attacks on small public chains, endpoint (wallet/exchange) hacks.
  • Privacy: public chains expose transactions; financial markets need confidentiality of positions and compliance with data-protection laws (India's Digital Personal Data Protection Act, 2023). Solutions: permissioned ledgers, private channels, zero-knowledge proofs, off-chain storage of personal data.

Challenges to blockchain adoption in bond markets

  1. Legal recognition of DLT records as title; insolvency treatment.
  2. Regulatory uncertainty and differences across jurisdictions.
  3. Interoperability with depositories, payment systems and other ledgers.
  4. Availability of on-chain central bank money (CBDC) for the cash leg.
  5. Scalability and speed for high volumes.
  6. Network effects: benefits need many participants to join.
  7. Cost of migrating from legacy systems; skills gap.
  8. Energy use of proof-of-work chains (a sustainability concern; permissioned and proof-of-stake chains use far less energy).
  9. Data integrity: the ledger records what it is told (garbage in, garbage out).

India's bond market is already heavily electronic (NDS-OM for G-secs, CCIL as central counterparty, NSDL/CDSL depositories, exchange RFQ platforms for corporate bonds), so the marginal gain from DLT is smaller than in paper-heavy markets. RBI's e₹-W pilot (2022) tested G-sec settlement with digital rupee; SEBI has used blockchain-based systems for monitoring security created on assets for listed debt securities (via depositories) — an example of DLT used for transparency rather than trading.

Self-check

1. Every blockchain is a DLT, but is every DLT a blockchain?

No. DLT is the wider category; a blockchain is a DLT that stores data in hash-linked blocks.

2. Why do banks prefer permissioned ledgers for bonds?

Known, regulated participants; privacy of positions; faster consensus; clear governance and legal accountability.

Model exam answer: "Examine the role of blockchain and DLT in bond markets." (10)

Introduction — definitions of blockchain (hash-linked blocks, nodes, consensus) and DLT (shared synchronised ledger without central administrator).

Architecture — public vs permissioned vs consortium; layers from network to applications.

Role in bond markets: (1) issuance on-chain; (2) golden-record ownership; (3) clearing and settlement collapsed into atomic DvP (diagram of traditional vs DLT); (4) transparency and auditability, including green-bond allocation tracking; (5) fewer intermediaries and reconciliations; (6) programmable servicing via smart contracts.

Security and privacy — strengths and weaknesses; permissioned ledgers, zero-knowledge proofs; DPDP Act 2023.

Challenges — legal, interoperability, cash leg, scalability, network effects, cost, data integrity.

Examples — World Bank bond-i, EIB, Hong Kong green bonds, HSBC Orion; India: RBI e₹-W G-sec settlement pilot, SEBI/depository DLT for security-cover monitoring of listed debt.

Conclusion. DLT can make bond markets faster, cheaper and more transparent — especially useful for proving the integrity of green and sustainability-linked bonds — but adoption depends on legal and infrastructure readiness.

Topic 14. Excel-based bond valuation: price, yield and cash-flow analysis

A bond is just a list of promised cash flows (coupons plus principal). Its price today is what those promises are worth when each is discounted at the return investors currently demand (the yield). Higher required yield → each future rupee is worth less today → lower price. That single idea explains every pointer in this topic. It matters for sustainable finance because green bonds and SLBs are priced exactly like ordinary bonds — the "greenium" and the SLB step-up are simply changes to the yield or to the cash flows.

Bond cash-flow structure

A plain-vanilla (fixed-coupon, bullet) bond pays a coupon C = Face value × Coupon rate every period and returns the face value F at maturity. A ₹1,000, 8%, 5-year annual bond pays ₹80 in years 1–4 and ₹1,080 in year 5.

Present value of coupons, of principal, and bond price

P = C × [1 − (1 + y)−n] ÷ y + F ÷ (1 + y)n

P = price; C = coupon per period (₹); y = yield to maturity per period (decimal); n = number of periods; F = face (redemption) value. First term = PV of coupons (an annuity); second = PV of principal. For half-yearly bonds use C/2, y/2 and 2n.

Worked example U5.8 — price by cash-flow table (basic). ₹1,000 face, 8% annual coupon, 5 years, market yield 9%.

Year tCash flow (₹)Discount factor 1/(1.09)tPV (₹)
1800.91743173.394
2800.84168067.334
3800.77218361.775
4800.70842556.674
51,0800.649931701.926
Price961.103
  1. PV of coupons = 73.394 + 67.334 + 61.775 + 56.674 + (80 × 0.649931 = 51.995) = ₹311.17.
  2. PV of principal = 1,000 × 0.649931 = ₹649.93.
  3. Price = 311.17 + 649.93 = ₹961.10 (a discount bond because coupon 8% < yield 9%).

Excel: =PV(9%,5,80,1000) returns −961.10 (negative because it is an outflow to buy); use =-PV(9%,5,80,1000).

Relationship between price and yield

Rules: (1) price and yield move inversely; (2) the curve is convex — a fall in yield raises price by more than an equal rise lowers it; (3) longer maturity and lower coupon mean greater price sensitivity; (4) as maturity approaches, price converges to face value ("pull to par").

Coupon rate, current yield and YTM

MeasureFormulaWhat it tells you
Coupon rateAnnual coupon ÷ Face valueFixed contractual rate (on face value)
Current yieldAnnual coupon ÷ Current priceIncome return only; ignores capital gain/loss
Yield to maturity (YTM)Rate y that makes PV of all cash flows = price (the IRR)Total return if held to maturity and coupons reinvested at y
Approximate YTM[C + (F − P)/n] ÷ [(F + P)/2]Quick hand estimate

Premium, par and discount bonds

CaseRelationPriceExample (8% bond)
PremiumCoupon rate > YTM; current yield between them> FaceYTM 7% → ₹1,041.00; CY = 80/1,041.00 = 7.68%
ParCoupon rate = YTM = CY= FaceYTM 8% → ₹1,000.00
DiscountCoupon rate < YTM; CY between them< FaceYTM 9% → ₹961.10; CY = 80/961.10 = 8.32%

Excel functions: PV, RATE, PRICE and YIELD

FunctionSyntaxExample and result
PV=PV(rate, nper, pmt, [fv], [type])=-PV(9%,5,80,1000) → 961.10
RATE=RATE(nper, pmt, pv, [fv])=RATE(5,80,-960,1000) → 9.03% (YTM when price is ₹960)
PRICE=PRICE(settlement, maturity, rate, yld, redemption, frequency, [basis])=PRICE(DATE(2025,4,1),DATE(2030,4,1),8%,9%,100,1) → 96.11 per 100 face = ₹961.10 per ₹1,000
YIELD=YIELD(settlement, maturity, rate, pr, redemption, frequency, [basis])=YIELD(DATE(2025,4,1),DATE(2030,4,1),8%,96,100,1) → 9.03%
AlsoNPV, IRR, XNPV/XIRR, DURATION, MDURATION, COUPNUMCash-flow based checks and duration (Topic 15)

PRICE and YIELD quote per 100 of face value and need real dates; frequency 1 = annual, 2 = half-yearly; basis 0 = 30/360 (default). With settlement on a coupon date and exactly 5 years to maturity, PRICE gives the same answer as the cash-flow table.

Worked example U5.9 — reverse problem: find YTM from price. The same 8%, 5-year bond trades at ₹960. Find (a) current yield, (b) approximate YTM, (c) exact YTM.

  1. Current yield = 80 ÷ 960 = 8.33%.
  2. Approximate YTM = [80 + (1,000 − 960)/5] ÷ [(1,000 + 960)/2] = (80 + 8) ÷ 980 = 8.98%.
  3. Exact YTM: solve 960 = Σ 80/(1+y)t + 1,000/(1+y)5. At 9% the price is 961.10 (slightly above 960), so y is slightly above 9%. Excel =RATE(5,80,-960,1000) = 9.03%.
  4. Check: coupon 8% < CY 8.33% < YTM 9.03% — the ordering for a discount bond.

Worked example U5.10 — half-yearly coupons (intermediate). Same bond (8%, 5 years) but coupons paid half-yearly; market yield 7% p.a. compounded half-yearly.

  1. Per period: C = 80 ÷ 2 = ₹40; y = 7% ÷ 2 = 3.5%; n = 5 × 2 = 10.
  2. P = 40 × [1 − 1.035−10] ÷ 0.035 + 1,000 ÷ 1.03510.
  3. Excel: =-PV(3.5%,10,40,1000) = ₹1,041.58 (slightly above the annual-pay price of ₹1,041.00 because coupons arrive sooner).

Numerical bond valuation example (longer bond)

Worked example U5.11. A green bond: ₹1,000 face, 7% annual coupon, 10 years, investors demand 8%. Price?

  1. C = 70, y = 8%, n = 10.
  2. Excel =-PV(8%,10,70,1000) = ₹932.90 (discount of ₹67.10).
  3. Compare with the 5-year 8% bond at 9% (discount ₹38.90 for a 1% gap): the longer bond has a larger discount for the same 1% gap — longer maturity means greater price sensitivity.

Yield sensitivity analysis

Set yields in a column and compute price with =-PV(A2,5,80,1000), or use Data → What-If Analysis → Data Table with the yield as the column input cell.

Yield6%7%8%9%10%
Price (₹)1,084.251,041.001,000.00961.10924.18
Change vs par+8.42%+4.10%0−3.89%−7.58%

Note the asymmetry: −2% yield gives +8.42%, but +2% gives only −7.58% (convexity).

Self-check

1. A bond's coupon is 7% and its YTM is 6.5%. Premium or discount?

Premium (coupon > YTM), price above face.

2. Which Excel function gives YTM from price for an annual bond without dates?

=RATE(nper, coupon, -price, face).

3. Why does current yield understate return for a discount bond?

It ignores the capital gain as price pulls up to par at maturity; YTM includes it.

Model exam answer: "Explain bond valuation using Excel with a numerical example." (10)

Introduction — price = PV of coupons + PV of principal at the market yield.

Cash-flow structure and formula (with variables).

Numerical example — ₹1,000, 8%, 5 years, 9% yield: PV coupons ₹311.17 + PV principal ₹649.93 = ₹961.10 (table of discount factors). Excel: =-PV(9%,5,80,1000); =PRICE(…) = 96.11 per 100.

Reverse — at ₹960, CY 8.33%, approximate YTM 8.98%, exact YTM 9.03% via =RATE or =YIELD.

Price–yield relationship (sketch the convex curve) and premium/par/discount table.

Sensitivity table 6%–10% showing asymmetry.

Relevance to sustainable finance — a greenium lowers the yield and raises the price; an SLB step-up changes cash flows; the same Excel tools price both.

Conclusion. Excel makes valuation, YTM and sensitivity analysis quick and auditable.

Topic 15. Excel-based bond portfolio analysis: duration, convexity and interest-rate risk

Concept of bond portfolio analysis

Analysing a group of bonds together to measure total value, return, and exposure to interest-rate changes, so that the manager (e.g. a bank treasury, insurance company or debt mutual fund holding green and conventional bonds) can match liabilities, set limits and stress-test.

Formulas

Portfolio market value V = Σ (Unitsi × Pricei);   weight wi = Valuei ÷ V
Portfolio return = Σ wi × Returni
Macaulay duration D = Σ [t × PV(CFt)] ÷ Price
Modified duration MD = D ÷ (1 + y)
Convexity = Σ [t(t + 1) × PV(CFt)] ÷ [Price × (1 + y)2]
%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy + ½ × Convexity × (Δy)2
Portfolio MD (or convexity) = Σ wi × MDi (or Convexityi)

t = period (years); PV(CFt) = present value of the cash flow at t; y = yield per period (annual bonds here); Δy = change in yield in decimals (1% = 0.01). Duration is in years; MD is the approximate % price change for a 1% (100 bps) change in yield.

Macaulay and modified duration

Worked example U5.12 — duration and convexity of one bond. ₹1,000, 8% annual, 5 years, YTM 9% (price ₹961.103 from U5.8).

tCFPV at 9%t × PVt(t+1) × PV
18073.39473.394146.789
28067.334134.669404.006
38061.775185.324741.296
48056.674226.6961,133.480
51,080701.9263,509.62921,057.777
Total961.1034,129.71323,483.349
  1. Macaulay D = 4,129.713 ÷ 961.103 = 4.2968 years.
  2. MD = 4.2968 ÷ 1.09 = 3.9421.
  3. Convexity = 23,483.349 ÷ (961.103 × 1.092) = 20.5654.
  4. If yield rises by 1% (to 10%): duration estimate = −3.9421 × 0.01 = −3.9421% (−₹37.89); with convexity = −3.9421% + ½ × 20.5654 × 0.0001 = −3.8393% (−₹36.90). Actual price at 10% = ₹924.18, a change of −₹36.92 (−3.8413%). Convexity brings the estimate almost exactly to the actual.

Excel: =DURATION(DATE(2025,4,1),DATE(2030,4,1),8%,9%,1) = 4.2968; =MDURATION(…same…) = 3.9421. Convexity has no built-in function: build the t(t+1)×PV column as above.

Portfolio market value, return and duration

Worked example U5.13 — numerical portfolio case study. A treasury holds three annual-pay bonds (face ₹1,000 each):

BondUnitsCouponMaturityYTMPriceValue (₹)WeightMac. DMDConvexity
A (green bond)2,0008%5 yrs9%961.1019,22,206.970.32224.29683.942120.5654
B (corporate)3,0006%3 yrs7%973.7629,21,270.520.48962.83102.64589.6962
C (zero-coupon)1,5000%4 yrs7.5%748.8011,23,200.790.18824.00003.720917.3067
Portfolio59,66,678.281.00003.52333.265814.6304
  1. Value: 2,000 × 961.1035 + 3,000 × 973.7568 + 1,500 × 748.8005 = ₹59,66,678.28 (prices shown rounded; values computed from unrounded prices).
  2. Weights: 19,22,206.97 ÷ 59,66,678.28 = 0.3222; 0.4896; 0.1882.
  3. Zero-coupon duration = its maturity (4 years), since it has a single cash flow; MD = 4 ÷ 1.075 = 3.7209.
  4. Portfolio MD = 0.3222 × 3.9421 + 0.4896 × 2.6458 + 0.1882 × 3.7209 = 1.2701 + 1.2954 + 0.7003 = 3.2658.
  5. Portfolio return if yields stay unchanged for a year (each bond earns its YTM): 0.3222 × 9% + 0.4896 × 7% + 0.1882 × 7.5% = 7.74%.
  6. +1% parallel shift: duration estimate = −3.2658% × 59,66,678.28 = −₹1,94,859; with convexity: −3.2658% + ½ × 14.6304 × 0.0001 = −3.1926% (−₹1,90,494). Full revaluation at new yields: −3.1940% (−₹1,90,574).

Convexity and estimating price changes; interest-rate risk measurement

Duration is a straight-line (tangent) estimate; convexity corrects for the curve. For small shifts duration alone is enough; for large shifts (100 bps or more) add convexity. Interest-rate risk measures: MD, PV01/DV01 (rupee change for a 1 bp move ≈ MD × Value × 0.0001; for the portfolio ≈ 3.2658 × 59,66,678.28 × 0.0001 = ₹1,949), and scenario losses.

Scenario and stress analysis in Excel

Parallel shiftDuration-only estimateDuration + convexityFull revaluation (actual)
−1% (rates fall)+3.2658% (+₹1,94,859)+3.3389% (+₹1,99,224)+3.3403% (+₹1,99,306)
+1%−3.2658% (−₹1,94,859)−3.1926% (−₹1,90,494)−3.1940% (−₹1,90,574)
+2% (stress)−6.5316% (−₹3,89,718)−6.2390% (−₹3,72,259)−6.2495% (−₹3,72,887)

Excel steps: (1) one row per bond with inputs; (2) price with =-PV(y,n,C,F) or PRICE; (3) value = units × price; weight = value/SUM; (4) DURATION and MDURATION, or build the t × PV table with SUMPRODUCT; (5) portfolio MD = =SUMPRODUCT(weights, MDs); (6) add a "shock" cell (+0.01) and reprice each bond at y + shock; (7) Data Table or Scenario Manager for −1%, +1%, +2%; (8) chart value vs shift. Non-parallel (twist) scenarios: apply different shocks to short and long bonds.

Debt mutual funds in India must disclose portfolio Macaulay duration, and SEBI's categorisation of debt schemes (e.g. short-duration, medium-duration, long-duration funds) is defined by Macaulay duration bands. RBI requires banks to measure interest-rate risk in the banking book using duration-gap and earnings approaches. A green bond held by a bank treasury carries the same interest-rate risk as any bond of that duration.

Self-check

1. What is the Macaulay duration of a 6-year zero-coupon bond?

6 years (single cash flow at maturity).

2. A portfolio has MD 5 and value ₹10 crore. Estimated loss if yields rise 50 bps?

−5 × 0.005 = −2.5%; loss ≈ ₹25 lakh.

3. Why is the convexity-adjusted estimate always higher (less negative) than the duration estimate?

The convexity term ½ × C × (Δy)2 is positive for option-free bonds whatever the sign of Δy.

Model exam answer: "Explain duration, convexity and interest-rate risk of a bond portfolio with an Excel case." (10/15)

Introduction — portfolio analysis; interest-rate risk as the main market risk for bonds.

Concepts and formulas — value, weights, return, Macaulay D, MD, convexity, %ΔP approximation, PV01.

Single-bond illustration — 8%/5y at 9%: D 4.2968, MD 3.9421, convexity 20.5654; +1% gives −3.94% (duration) vs −3.84% (with convexity) vs −3.84% actual.

Portfolio case — three bonds worth ₹59.67 lakh; weights 0.3222/0.4896/0.1882; portfolio MD 3.2658, convexity 14.6304; return 7.74%; stress table for −1%, +1%, +2%.

Excel method — PV/PRICE, DURATION, MDURATION, SUMPRODUCT, Data Table, Scenario Manager.

Risk management uses — immunisation (match duration to liability horizon), limits, hedging with interest-rate derivatives, SEBI duration-based fund categories.

Conclusion. Duration gives the first-order risk, convexity the correction; together with scenario analysis they give a complete picture of interest-rate risk for any bond portfolio, green or conventional.

The five company cases: sustainable investment perspectives

"Sustainable investment perspective" means looking at a company the way an ESG investor or lender would: What are its material ESG issues? What has it committed to (targets)? What has it done in practice (evidence)? What controversies or risks remain? Is it a good sustainable investment, and on which strategy (best-in-class, transition, exclusion, engagement)? Use this same six-step template for every company and in every Section C answer.

StepQuestion to answerTools from the course
1. ProfileBusiness, sector, why ESG matters hereSASB-style materiality
2. Material issuesTop E, S, G issuesESG pillars (1.4)
3. CommitmentsTargets, policies, frameworksBRSR, GRI, TCFD, net zero, SBTi
4. EvidenceInitiatives, results, financing instrumentsGreen/SLB bonds, CSR, reports
5. Risks and controversiesTransition, physical, social, governance risksRisk identification (4.1)
6. Investment viewWhich strategy fits; what to engage onStrategies (3.1), stakeholder theory

Company facts below are limited to well-documented, public milestones. Exact current figures (emissions, ratings, percentages) change every year — quote the latest BRSR or sustainability report if your professor provided one, and otherwise describe trends without inventing numbers.

Case A. HDFC Bank — a financial institution's perspective

1. Profile

India's largest private-sector bank; merged with its parent, the housing finance company HDFC Ltd, with effect from 1 July 2023, creating one of the largest banks in India by balance sheet and adding a large home-loan book. As a bank, its own (operational) emissions are small; its real ESG footprint is through what it finances (financed emissions, Scope 3 category 15) and through financial inclusion and governance.

2. Material ESG issues

  • E: climate risk in the loan book (transition risk in carbon-intensive corporate borrowers; physical risk to agricultural, MSME and mortgage borrowers); green finance opportunities; operational footprint (branches, data centres, paper).
  • S: financial inclusion (rural and semi-urban reach), responsible lending and customer protection, data privacy and cyber security, employee well-being, CSR.
  • G: board independence, risk management, IT resilience, conduct and compliance, integration after the merger.

3. Commitments and frameworks

  • Stated goal of becoming carbon neutral in its operations by FY2031-32, through energy efficiency, renewable power and offsets.
  • ESG and responsible financing policy with an Environmental and Social Management System (ESMS): E&S due diligence for project and corporate loans, and an exclusion list of activities it will not finance.
  • Reports through the BRSR, an integrated annual report, and ESG/sustainability disclosures referencing global frameworks (GRI, TCFD-style climate disclosure).
  • Subject to RBI's climate-risk agenda: Green Deposits framework (2023), draft climate disclosure framework (2024), climate scenario analysis and stress testing guidance.

4. Evidence — key initiatives

  • Parivartan — the bank's CSR umbrella: rural development, education, skill training and livelihoods, health and hygiene, financial literacy.
  • Sustainable Livelihood Initiative (SLI) — credit plus training for women in rural areas, largely through self-help groups: a social-finance (microfinance-type) programme.
  • Green and inclusive lending: loans for renewable energy, electric vehicles and rooftop solar; priority-sector lending to agriculture and MSMEs.
  • Digital banking reducing paper and branch visits; green buildings and solar at facilities.

5. Risks and controversies

  • Operational/technology risk: after repeated digital outages, RBI in December 2020 directed the bank to pause new digital launches and new credit-card sourcing; card sourcing restrictions were lifted in 2021 and the digital restrictions in 2022. A clear example that IT resilience is a governance (G) and social (customer) issue.
  • Financed emissions: exposure to power, metals, cement and other carbon-intensive corporates; limited public disclosure of financed emissions compared with global peers is a common investor engagement point.
  • Physical risk in agricultural and housing books (floods, heat, drought).
  • Merger integration and funding pressures after absorbing HDFC Ltd's long-tenor home loans.

6. Sustainable investment view

HDFC Bank fits an ESG integration / best-in-class approach: strong governance reputation, broad inclusion reach, and a large opportunity to scale green lending. Engagement priorities: publish PCAF-based financed emissions and sector decarbonisation targets, grow the share of green and sustainability-linked lending, strengthen IT resilience, and disclose climate stress-test results.

Model exam answer: "Discuss the sustainable investment perspective of HDFC Bank." (10)

Introduction. For a bank, sustainability is mainly about what it finances (financed emissions) and how it behaves (inclusion, conduct, governance). HDFC Bank, India's largest private bank (merged with HDFC Ltd from 1 July 2023), illustrates both.

Material issues — E (loan-book climate risk, green finance), S (inclusion, customer protection, data security), G (board, risk, IT resilience).

Commitments — operational carbon neutrality by FY2031-32; ESMS and exclusion list; BRSR and integrated reporting; alignment with RBI's green deposit and climate-risk frameworks.

Initiatives — Parivartan CSR; Sustainable Livelihood Initiative for rural women; renewable, EV and rooftop-solar lending; digital banking.

Risks — RBI's 2020 restrictions after outages (operational/G risk); financed emissions in carbon-intensive sectors; physical risk in farm and housing loans; merger integration.

Evaluation (mini-SWOT): strengths — governance, reach, reporting; weaknesses — limited financed-emissions disclosure; opportunities — green deposits, transition finance, SLLs; threats — climate losses, regulatory tightening, cyber risk.

Conclusion. A strong candidate for ESG-integrated portfolios, with engagement on financed-emissions targets and transition finance as the key next step.

Case B. Infosys — the low-impact services leader

1. Profile

One of India's largest IT services companies, with large campuses in India and a global client base. Direct environmental impact is modest (electricity for offices and data centres, employee commuting and business travel), so its ESG case rests on climate leadership, people (a very large workforce), data security and governance.

2. Material ESG issues

  • E: energy and Scope 2 emissions, business travel and commuting (Scope 3), water on campuses, e-waste.
  • S: talent attraction and retention, diversity and inclusion, employee well-being, data privacy and cyber security for clients, community (CSR via Infosys Foundation).
  • G: board independence, founder–management relations, whistle-blower handling, ethics.

3. Commitments and frameworks

  • Committed in 2011 to become carbon neutral; announced carbon neutrality in 2020, decades ahead of the Paris Agreement's mid-century timeline, and has since reported maintaining it.
  • ESG Vision 2030 (launched 2020): goals across climate change, water, waste, people (diversity, employee well-being), technology for good, data privacy and governance.
  • Member of RE100 (commitment to 100% renewable electricity).
  • Reports: BRSR, a detailed ESG report referencing GRI and climate disclosures along TCFD lines, integrated annual report.

4. Evidence — how carbon neutrality was achieved

  1. Energy efficiency: green building design (many campuses rated under LEED/GRIHA), radiant cooling, smart building management — per-capita electricity use fell substantially from 2008 levels.
  2. Renewable energy: on-site and off-site solar and renewable power purchase.
  3. Carbon offsets: community-based projects in rural India (efficient cookstoves, household biogas) that generate carbon credits and social co-benefits — a link to Unit 3 carbon markets.
  4. Water and waste: rainwater harvesting, sewage recycling, waste segregation and recycling on campuses.

5. Risks and controversies

  • Governance episode (2017): public disagreements between founders and the then management over governance, pay and acquisitions (including Panaya) preceded the CEO's resignation in August 2017 and a board reshuffle, with co-founder Nandan Nilekani returning as non-executive chairman. Shows that even ESG leaders face G risk.
  • Offset reliance: critics of any "carbon neutral" claim ask how much depends on offsets vs actual reductions — a greenwashing test (quality and additionality of credits).
  • Scope 3 (business travel, purchased goods, client-related) is harder to reduce.
  • Data breaches and client-data risk; workforce issues (attrition, working hours).

6. Sustainable investment view

Strong best-in-class and positive-screening candidate; usually included in ESG indices and funds. Engagement: shift from offsets to absolute reductions and science-based Scope 3 targets; continued board independence; diversity at senior levels.

Model exam answer: "How did Infosys become carbon neutral? Evaluate its sustainability strategy." (10)

Introduction — IT services have low direct impact, so leadership is about climate ambition, people and governance.

Journey — 2011 commitment → efficiency and green buildings → renewable energy → community-based offsets → carbon neutrality announced in 2020 → ESG Vision 2030.

Three levers (draw a staircase): reduce energy demand; switch to renewables; offset the residual with high-quality credits that also deliver social benefits (cookstoves, biogas).

Social and governance — Infosys Foundation CSR; diversity goals; data privacy; board and ethics; the 2017 governance episode as a lesson.

Evaluation — strengths (early mover, measurable results, transparent reporting); limitations (offset dependence, Scope 3, reliance on a "carbon neutral" label that can attract greenwashing scrutiny).

Investment perspective — best-in-class holding; engagement on absolute reductions and Scope 3.

Conclusion. Infosys shows that a services company can use efficiency, renewables and quality offsets to lead on climate, but credibility now depends on deeper Scope 3 cuts.

Case C. ITC — "triple positive" conglomerate with a tobacco question

1. Profile

Diversified conglomerate: cigarettes (its largest profit contributor), FMCG foods and personal care, paperboards and packaging, agribusiness; its hotels business was demerged into a separate listed company, ITC Hotels, effective January 2025. No promoter group; British American Tobacco is its largest shareholder.

2. Material ESG issues

  • E: carbon, water (agri-sourcing, paper mills), plantations and biodiversity, packaging and plastic waste.
  • S: public-health impact of tobacco; farmer livelihoods; product safety and nutrition; community development.
  • G: board and management professionalism; the influence of a large foreign tobacco shareholder; capital allocation across very different businesses.

3. Commitments and claims

  • ITC reports being carbon positive, water positive and solid-waste-recycling positive for more than a decade each — meaning it sequesters more CO2 than it emits (mainly through social and farm forestry), creates more rainwater-harvesting potential than it consumes, and recycles more waste than it generates.
  • "Sustainability 2.0" agenda with time-bound targets for 2030 (renewable energy share, water, packaging circularity, livelihoods).
  • Green buildings: its luxury hotels were among the first in the world to be LEED Platinum-rated.
  • Reports: BRSR, sustainability report referencing GRI, integrated reporting.

4. Evidence — key initiatives

  • e-Choupal (launched 2000): internet kiosks giving farmers price and weather information and direct procurement — a pioneering inclusive-business model.
  • Social and farm forestry: supporting farmers to plant trees on marginal land, supplying pulpwood to its paper mills and sequestering carbon (the basis of "carbon positive").
  • Watershed development and water stewardship in water-stressed districts.
  • Well-Being Out of Waste (WOW) recycling programme; plastic-neutral goals for packaging.
  • Paperboards: renewable energy and water recycling in mills.

5. Risks and controversies

  • Tobacco: many global ESG funds exclude tobacco producers on health grounds regardless of environmental performance — ITC's biggest ESG obstacle. This is the classic example of exclusionary screening vs best-in-class conflict.
  • Regulatory risk: tobacco taxation and health regulation.
  • Offsetting logic: "carbon positive" relies heavily on forestry sequestration; investors ask about permanence and whether gross emissions are falling.

6. Sustainable investment view

Excluded by tobacco-screened funds; included by some Indian ESG funds on the strength of environmental and social programmes and governance. The case teaches that ESG scores are not the same as "ethical": a strong E score can coexist with a product that harms health. Engagement: diversification away from tobacco, gross-emission reduction targets, transparent forestry accounting.

Model exam answer: "ITC is 'carbon positive' yet excluded by many ESG funds. Discuss." (10)

Introduction — profile and the paradox.

The case for ITC — carbon, water and solid-waste-recycling positive for more than a decade; social and farm forestry; e-Choupal; watershed and WOW programmes; LEED Platinum hotels; strong reporting.

The case against — cigarettes as the largest profit source; public-health harm (S pillar); norms-based and exclusionary screens; regulatory risk; questions on forestry offsets.

Concepts applied — exclusionary vs best-in-class vs integration strategies; single vs double materiality (tobacco is financially material through tax/regulation and impact-material through health); ESG rating divergence.

Conclusion — whether ITC is a "sustainable investment" depends on the investor's strategy and values; an honest answer states both views and suggests engagement on diversification and absolute emission cuts.

Case D. JSW Steel — financing a hard-to-abate transition

1. Profile

One of India's largest steel producers (part of the JSW Group), with its flagship integrated plant at Vijayanagar, Karnataka, among the largest single-location steel plants in India. Steel is a hard-to-abate sector: blast-furnace steel making relies on coal/coke, so the sector is among the largest industrial emitters worldwide.

2. Material ESG issues

  • E: CO2 intensity (tCO2 per tonne of crude steel), air emissions, water, slag and waste, mining impacts.
  • S: worker safety (LTIFR and fatalities in heavy industry), communities near plants and mines, land acquisition.
  • G: promoter-group structure, related-party transactions, capital discipline in a cyclical industry.

3. Commitments

  • Targets to cut CO2 emission intensity substantially by 2030 and to reach net zero by 2050.
  • Climate disclosure along TCFD lines; BRSR reporting.

4. Evidence — sustainable financing

  • US$500 million sustainability-linked bond (2021) with the KPI of CO2 emission intensity at Vijayanagar works and a target date in 2030; if the target is missed, the coupon steps up (see Topic 1, Topic 9). SLBs fit steel because the company's challenge is whole-company decarbonisation rather than a ring-fenced green project.
  • Decarbonisation levers: energy efficiency and waste-heat recovery; higher scrap use; renewable power (group company JSW Energy); natural gas and, in future, green hydrogen in iron-making (linked to the National Green Hydrogen Mission); carbon capture pilots.
  • Circularity: blast-furnace slag used in cement (group company JSW Cement) — a by-product that replaces clinker and cuts cement emissions.

5. Risks

  • Transition risk: India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (steel is a designated sector for intensity targets), PAT obligations, and the EU Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism (CBAM), which will charge importers for embedded emissions in steel exported to the EU.
  • Technology risk: green hydrogen and CCUS are expensive and unproven at scale.
  • Credibility risk: SLB target ambition and the size of the step-up penalty will be judged by investors.
  • Physical risk: water availability and heat for plants.

6. Sustainable investment view

A transition-finance case: not a "green" company today, but a candidate for investors who fund credible decarbonisation. Evaluate: intensity trend vs baseline, capex on low-carbon technology, verified KPI reports, CBAM readiness. Divesting would not reduce real-economy emissions; engagement and performance-linked finance aim to.

Worked example U5.14 — is the SLB target on track? Illustrative numbers (not company data): baseline intensity 2.50 tCO2/tcs; SPT 2.00 tCO2/tcs in 10 years. Reported intensity after 4 years: 2.34.

  1. Required linear reduction = (2.50 − 2.00) ÷ 10 = 0.05 tCO2/tcs per year.
  2. Expected level after 4 years on a linear path = 2.50 − 4 × 0.05 = 2.30.
  3. Actual 2.34 is 0.04 above the path → behind schedule; remaining reduction needed = 2.34 − 2.00 = 0.34 over 6 years = 0.0567 per year (faster than the original 0.05).
  4. Investor action: engage on capex plan; price in a higher probability of the coupon step-up.
Model exam answer: "Evaluate JSW Steel's approach to sustainable finance." (10)

Introduction — steel is hard to abate; JSW Steel is a large Indian producer; its challenge is transition, not greenness.

Material issues — CO2 intensity, safety, communities, water, slag.

Commitments — 2030 intensity reduction, net zero 2050, TCFD/BRSR disclosure.

Financing — US$500 million SLB (2021) linked to Vijayanagar CO2 intensity; why SLB suits steel; mechanics of step-up (illustration: 25 bps on ₹500 crore = ₹1.25 crore a year).

Levers — efficiency, scrap, renewables, green hydrogen, CCUS, slag to cement.

Risks — CCTS, PAT, EU CBAM, technology cost, credibility of targets.

Evaluation — positive: early SLB issuer, measurable KPI, circularity; concerns: ambition vs science-based pathway, size of penalty, dependence on unproven technology.

Conclusion — a leading example of transition finance in India; investor value lies in engagement and KPI monitoring.

Case E. Vedanta Ltd — ESG controversies and the cost of social licence

1. Profile

Diversified natural-resources company: zinc, lead and silver (through Hindustan Zinc), aluminium, oil and gas (Cairn), iron ore, copper, power. Parent: London-based Vedanta Resources (promoter group). Mining and metals have high E and S impacts and depend on a social licence to operate.

2. Material ESG issues

  • E: GHG emissions (coal-based captive power for aluminium smelting), tailings and waste, air and water pollution, biodiversity and forest land.
  • S: indigenous and community rights, health impacts, worker safety, land acquisition.
  • G: complex group structure, high parent-level debt, related-party transactions, minority-shareholder treatment.

3. Key controversies (well documented)

  • Niyamgiri (Odisha): a proposed bauxite mine in the Niyamgiri hills, sacred to the Dongria Kondh community, to supply the Lanjigarh alumina refinery. In 2013 the Supreme Court held that the local gram sabhas must decide on religious and cultural rights; the gram sabhas rejected the project and the Environment Ministry refused forest clearance in 2014.
  • Sterlite Copper, Thoothukudi (Tamil Nadu): long-running pollution complaints; in May 2018 protests against the smelter's expansion ended in police firing that killed 13 people; the Tamil Nadu government ordered permanent closure; the Madras High Court upheld the closure (2020) and the Supreme Court dismissed Vedanta's appeal in 2024.
  • Investor exclusion: Norway's Government Pension Fund Global excluded Vedanta Resources and group companies in 2007 on ethical grounds (environmental damage and human-rights concerns) — a textbook example of norms-based exclusion.
  • Governance: an attempt by the promoter to delist Vedanta Ltd failed in 2020 when the offer did not receive enough shares; concerns over parent-level debt and dividend up-streaming recur in analyst and rating commentary.

4. Commitments and response

  • Target of net-zero carbon by 2050 with a stated transition investment programme (renewable power for smelters, energy efficiency).
  • ESG reporting through BRSR and sustainability reports; ESG rating improvements are cited by the company; Hindustan Zinc, a subsidiary, is often cited as a stronger ESG performer in the group.
  • CSR programmes (e.g. Nand Ghar child-care and nutrition centres).

5. Investment lessons

  1. Social licence is a financial asset: the Thoothukudi closure stranded a major copper smelter; Niyamgiri left a refinery without its planned bauxite source — ESG risk became financial loss.
  2. Controversy screening and exclusion can protect investors from such tail risks.
  3. Engagement vs divestment: engaged investors push for independent board oversight, community consent (FPIC), tailings safety and credible net-zero capex.
  4. Rating divergence: different raters weight controversies differently, so scores for such companies vary widely.
Model exam answer: "Analyse Vedanta Ltd from a sustainable investment perspective." (10/15)

Introduction — profile; mining depends on social licence; stakeholder theory lens.

Material issues — E, S, G lists.

Controversies — Niyamgiri (SC 2013, gram sabhas, clearance refused 2014); Thoothukudi (2018 firing, closure upheld by HC 2020 and SC 2024); Norway GPFG exclusion 2007; governance (failed 2020 delisting, parent debt).

Financial consequences — stranded assets, lost production, legal costs, higher cost of capital, investor exclusion.

Company response — net zero 2050 target, renewable power, reporting, CSR.

Investment view — exclusion (norms-based) vs engagement (conditions: independent oversight, FPIC, tailings, capex); ESG rating divergence.

Conclusion — Vedanta shows that ESG risks are financial risks; for most ESG funds it remains a high-risk holding unless governance and community relations visibly improve.

If a Section C case asks you to "rank" or "choose" among companies for an ESG fund, state the fund's strategy first (e.g. "for a best-in-class fund…"), then rank. Without the strategy, there is no single right answer — and saying so earns marks.

Unit 5 case kit (Section C practice)

Case U5-1. A pension fund's ESG committee: include JSW Steel's SLB? (15)

Case. An Indian pension fund's ESG policy allows "transition" holdings. It is considering ₹50 crore of a steel company's 5-year SLB: coupon 7.50%, KPI = CO2 intensity, baseline 2.50, SPT 2.20 by end of year 3, step-up 25 bps for years 4–5 if missed. Analysts put the probability of missing at 40%. (a) Explain why an SLB rather than a green bond (4). (b) Compute the fund's annual coupon income if met and if missed, and expected coupon in years 4–5 (5). (c) Recommend, with conditions (6).

(a) Steel's problem is the whole production process; there are few ring-fenced "green projects". An SLB links the cost of money to company-wide performance (use of proceeds is general), fitting ICMA SLBP's five components (KPI, SPT, bond characteristics, reporting, verification). A green bond would finance only specific assets and might leave most emissions untouched.

(b) If met: 50 crore × 7.50% = ₹3.75 crore a year for 5 years. If missed: years 1–3 ₹3.75 crore; years 4–5 at 7.75% = ₹3.875 crore a year (extra ₹12.5 lakh a year, ₹25 lakh total). Expected coupon in years 4–5 = 0.6 × 3.75 + 0.4 × 3.875 = ₹3.80 crore.

(c) Recommend investing under the transition sleeve if: the SPT is ambitious (required reduction (2.50 − 2.20)/3 = 0.10 a year, 12% in total — compare with sector pathway), the KPI covers the main plants, verification is by an independent assurer, there is no call option before the observation date, and the issuer has a funded capex plan. Monitor annual KPI reports; engage; set an exit trigger if targets are weakened. Conclusion: the SLB aligns the fund's return with real decarbonisation and gives a small compensation if the issuer fails.

Case U5-2. Should the "Bharat ESG Leaders Fund" hold Vedanta, ITC or Infosys? (15)

Case. A new best-in-class ESG fund with a norms-based exclusion list (controversial weapons, tobacco, severe human-rights controversies) must decide on three stocks.

Framework. Step 1 apply exclusions; Step 2 rank remaining companies by ESG performance relative to sector peers; Step 3 check controversies; Step 4 engagement plan.

Infosys — passes exclusions; sector leader (carbon neutral since 2020, ESG Vision 2030, strong disclosure); governance episode of 2017 resolved. Include; engage on Scope 3 and offset quality.

ITC — strong environmental credentials (carbon, water, waste positive; e-Choupal; forestry), but tobacco exclusion applies. Exclude under this fund's rules, while noting that a fund without a tobacco screen might include it.

Vedanta — severe controversies (Niyamgiri, Thoothukudi 2018), GPFG exclusion since 2007, governance concerns. Exclude or place on watch list; readmission conditions: independent verification of community consent, pollution control, board independence, credible net-zero capex.

Disclosure. The fund's name and SEBI ESG scheme category (e.g. best-in-class with exclusions) must match what it does; publish exclusion list and stewardship report.

Conclusion. Only Infosys qualifies; the answer shows that strategy and screens, not just ESG scores, decide inclusion.

Case U5-3. HDFC Bank designs a green deposit and green lending programme (15)

Case. A large private bank plans to raise green deposits and lend to rooftop solar, EVs and green buildings. The board wants (a) the regulatory requirements, (b) a proceeds-allocation check, (c) risks and safeguards.

(a) Under RBI's framework for acceptance of Green Deposits (2023): board-approved policy and financing framework; allocation only to eligible green activities (renewable energy, energy efficiency, clean transport, climate adaptation, sustainable water and waste management, pollution control, green buildings, sustainable land use and biodiversity); exclusion of fossil-fuel and certain other activities; annual third-party verification of allocation; impact assessment; disclosure in annual financial statements.

(b) Allocation illustration. Green deposits raised ₹2,000 crore; allocated: rooftop solar ₹700 crore, EV loans ₹500 crore, green buildings ₹400 crore. Allocated = ₹1,600 crore = 1,600 ÷ 2,000 = 80%; unallocated ₹400 crore to be held in permitted temporary investments and disclosed.

(c) Risks and safeguards. Greenwashing (ineligible assets tagged green) → clear taxonomy and external verification; credit risk of new green borrowers → standard appraisal plus ESMS; data → impact metrics (MW installed, tCO2 avoided); operational → tagging systems; reputational → transparent reporting. Link with SLI and priority-sector lending to reach rural customers (solar pumps).

Conclusion. A well-governed programme lets the bank grow green assets credibly and meets RBI's expectations.

Case U5-4. A municipal corporation plans a tokenised green bond (15)

Case. A city wants to raise ₹100 crore for rooftop solar on public buildings and electric buses, and a fintech proposes a tokenised issue with ₹1,000 tokens and a 7.2% annual coupon. (a) Explain the structure (5). (b) Compute tokens, coupon per token and the income of a citizen holding 50 tokens (4). (c) Benefits, risks and recommendation (6).

(a) Green bond framework aligned to ICMA GBP (use of proceeds, project selection, management of proceeds, reporting) with an SPO; tokens minted on a permissioned ledger by a smart contract; KYC-whitelisted wallets; coupons paid automatically; allocation and impact (MWh generated, tCO2 avoided) published on-chain; trustee and regulator nodes.

(b) Tokens = 100,00,00,000 ÷ 1,000 = 10,00,000; coupon per token = 1,000 × 7.2% = ₹72 a year; 50 tokens cost ₹50,000 and earn ₹3,600 a year.

(c) Benefits: citizen participation, transparency of proceeds (reduces greenwashing), lower servicing costs, faster settlement. Risks: legal recognition of tokens in India (issue would need to comply with SEBI's municipal debt regulations and use recognised depositories), smart-contract and oracle risk, liquidity, investor protection for retail. Recommendation: issue a SEBI-compliant listed green municipal bond (as Indian cities such as Indore and Ghaziabad did with green municipal bonds) and pilot DLT for reporting allocation and impact, moving to full tokenisation when regulation permits.

Analytics angle: doing sustainable finance in Excel, Power BI, R, Jamovi and Orange3

Your syllabus lists "analytical thinking" as the 21st-century skill for this course. These are the practical ways each tool handles the quantitative topics above. Numbers in comments match the worked examples.

Excel

TaskExact functions / steps
Carbon footprintColumns: Source, Scope, Activity, EF. Emissions =C2*D2/1000 (kg to t). Totals by scope: =SUMIF(B:B,"Scope 1",E:E). Intensity: total ÷ output. PivotTable by scope and site.
ESG composite score=SUMPRODUCT(B2:D2,$B$1:$D$1) with weights in row 1; =RANK.EQ() to rank companies; Goal Seek (Data → What-If → Goal Seek) for "what E score is needed to reach 70?".
Green bond allocationAllocation % =SUM(alloc)/net_proceeds; refinancing share with SUMIF on a "New/Refinanced" column; avoided emissions =MW*CUF*8760*EF.
SLB couponsCoupon row =IF(AND(year>obs_year,KPI>SPT),base+stepup,base)*face; price =NPV(y,coupons) + PV of face; expected price with probability weights.
PAT / CCTS=(SEC_target-SEC_actual)*production; conditional formatting red if negative (must buy).
PCAF / WACIAF =invested/EVIC; financed emissions =SUMPRODUCT(invested/EVIC,emissions); WACI =SUMPRODUCT(invested/SUM(invested),emissions/revenue) (→ 121 in Case 5).
Stress testEL =PD*LGD*EAD for base and stressed columns; Scenario Manager for orderly/disorderly/hot-house cases.
Heat mapLikelihood × impact grid with Home → Conditional Formatting → Color Scales.
BondsPV, RATE, PRICE, YIELD, DURATION, MDURATION, NPV, IRR, XIRR; Data Table for yield sensitivity (Topics 14–15).
Microfinance EMI=PMT(rate/12,n,-loan); effective rate of a flat loan with =RATE(n,-EMI,loan)*12.

Power BI

  1. Get Data from Excel/CSV (BRSR extracts, holdings file, emission data); clean in Power Query (unpivot years, fix types).
  2. Model: Holdings table linked to Company table (sector, EVIC, emissions, revenue, ESG score).
  3. DAX measures: 
    Financed Emissions =
SUMX ( Holdings, DIVIDE ( Holdings[Invested], Holdings[EVIC] ) * Holdings[Emissions] )

WACI =
VAR TotalInv = SUM ( Holdings[Invested] )
RETURN
SUMX ( Holdings,
       DIVIDE ( Holdings[Invested], TotalInv ) * DIVIDE ( Holdings[Emissions], Holdings[Revenue] ) )

Allocation % = DIVIDE ( SUM ( Allocation[Amount] ), [Net Proceeds] )
  4. Visuals: stacked bar of Scope 1/2/3 by year; card visuals for financed emissions and WACI; matrix heat map for risk scores; slicers by sector; map visual for physical-risk exposure of branches or plants; decomposition tree for "which holding drives WACI".
  5. Publish a dashboard for the ESG committee and set scheduled refresh.

R

# Bond price, YTM and Macaulay duration (Topic 14-15)
bond_price <- function(F, c, y, n) {
  t  <- 1:n
  cf <- rep(F * c, n); cf[n] <- cf[n] + F
  sum(cf / (1 + y)^t)
}
bond_price(1000, 0.08, 0.09, 5)                        # 961.10
uniroot(function(y) bond_price(1000, 0.08, y, 5) - 960,
        c(0.0001, 0.5))$root                           # 0.0903 (9.03%)
mac_dur <- function(F, c, y, n) {
  t <- 1:n; cf <- rep(F * c, n); cf[n] <- cf[n] + F
  pv <- cf / (1 + y)^t
  sum(t * pv) / sum(pv)
}
mac_dur(1000, 0.08, 0.09, 5)                           # 4.2968

# PCAF financed emissions and WACI (Unit 4 Case 5)
pf <- data.frame(co = c("A", "B", "C"), inv = c(50, 30, 20),
                 evic = c(1000, 600, 800), em = c(400000, 60000, 8000),
                 rev = c(2000, 900, 1600))
pf$fe <- pf$inv / pf$evic * pf$em
sum(pf$fe)                                             # 23200
sum(pf$inv / sum(pf$inv) * pf$em / pf$rev)             # 121

# Composite ESG score
sum(c(E = 55, S = 72, G = 80) * c(0.5, 0.3, 0.2))      # 65.1

For analysis of ESG data sets: lm(return ~ esg_score + size + beta, data = d) to test whether ESG scores explain returns; cor() between ratings from two providers to show divergence; ggplot2 for emission trends.

Jamovi

  • Exploration → Descriptives: mean, SD, box plots of ESG scores by sector.
  • Regression → Correlation Matrix: correlation between two agencies' ESG ratings (rating divergence) or between ESG score and cost of debt.
  • Regression → Linear Regression: stock return or bond spread on ESG score with controls (size, leverage).
  • T-Tests → Independent Samples: do green bonds have lower yields than matched conventional bonds (greenium)?
  • ANOVA: compare emission intensity across sectors.

Orange3 (visual, no-code machine learning)

  • ESG score prediction: File → Data Table → Feature Statistics → Linear Regression / Random Forest → Test & Score → Predictions (predict a missing ESG score or emissions from company features — the ML-based estimation discussed in 3.13).
  • Clustering: k-Means → Scatter Plot to group companies by E, S, G profiles.
  • Text mining (Text add-on): Corpus (sustainability reports or news) → Preprocess Text → Bag of Words / Word Cloud → Sentiment Analysis to flag controversy or vague "green" language (greenwashing screening).

Course-material questions solved

Your Unit 1, Unit 2 and Unit 3 slide decks contain teaching content but no end-of-chapter exercises or numerical problems, and the Unit 5 file is a list of presentation topics (all 15 are answered in full in Unit 5). So that nothing from the slides is missed, every slide that makes an examinable claim or gives an example is turned into a question below and answered. Slide numbers refer to your decks.

From Unit 1 slides

S1 (slides 10–11). Trace the history of sustainable finance using the course timeline.

1713 Hans Carl von Carlowitz (forestry: harvest no more than regrows — origin of "sustainability") → 1962 Silent Spring (Rachel Carson; pesticides and pollution) → 1972 UN Conference on the Human Environment, Stockholm (first major UN environment conference; led to UNEP) → 1987 Brundtland Report (definition of sustainable development) → 1992 Earth Summit, Rio (UNFCCC, CBD, Agenda 21) → 1997 Kyoto Protocol (binding targets for developed countries; carbon markets) → 2004 Who Cares Wins (coined "ESG") → 2006 PRI → 2015 SDGs and Paris Agreement → 2020 onwards rapid growth of ESG and green finance (green, social, sustainability-linked bonds; mandatory disclosure such as BRSR). Structure it as three eras: ethical exclusion → ESG integration → impact and climate alignment (see 1.1).

S2 (slides 12, 14). Define sustainable finance and ESG as per the course.

Sustainable finance (course definition): financial activities, investments and banking decisions that consider not only profit but also environmental, social and governance factors, aiming to support long-term economic growth while protecting the environment and society. ESG: standards used to measure how responsibly an organisation operates and how it affects society and the environment, helping investors, companies, governments and stakeholders evaluate sustainability and ethical practice. Examples (slide 16): investing in solar projects; companies cutting emissions; green bonds for renewable energy.

S3 (slide 21). Map the slide's SDG examples to specific goals.
