Free education programmes for childrenSDG 4 Quality educationSDG 10 Reduced inequalities
Clean drinking water facilitiesSDG 6 Clean water and sanitationSDG 3 Good health
Women entrepreneurship loansSDG 5 Gender equalitySDG 8 Decent work; SDG 1
Tree plantation drivesSDG 15 Life on landSDG 13 Climate action

Financing route: green bonds (7, 13, 15), social bonds (4, 5, 6), microfinance (1, 5, 8), CSR under Section 135.

S4 (slides 23–30). Compare UNFCCC, the Kyoto Protocol and the Paris Agreement.
BasisUNFCCC (1992)Kyoto Protocol (1997)Paris Agreement (2015)
NatureFramework convention adopted at Rio Earth SummitProtocol under UNFCCCAgreement under UNFCCC, adopted at COP21
GoalStabilise GHG concentrations at a safe levelBinding emission cuts for developed (Annex I) countriesWell below 2°C, pursuing 1.5°C
Who actsAll; CBDR — developed countries leadOnly developed countries had binding targetsAll countries, through NDCs
ApproachPrinciples and reportingTop-down targets; flexible mechanisms: CDM, emissions trading, joint implementationBottom-up NDCs, updated every five years; global stocktake; climate finance support
SignificanceFoundation of all later agreementsCreated carbon markets (CDM credits)Near-universal participation; basis of net-zero targets

Also on slide 23: CBD (Convention on Biological Diversity, 1992) and CITES (trade in endangered species, 1973). See 1.8.

S5 (slides 34–35). Explain the ten points on ESG risk management and reporting.
  1. Beyond financials — non-financial risks matter to value.
  2. E pillar — carbon, water scarcity, climate disruption.
  3. S pillar — labour rights, safety, community, data privacy.
  4. G pillar — anti-corruption, executive pay, independent boards.
  5. Risk cycle — identify → assess → mitigate → monitor (continuous).
  6. Reporting shift — from retrospective financial to forward-looking sustainability reporting.
  7. ISSB and GRI — ISSB: how sustainability/climate affects company value (financial materiality); GRI: company's impact on the world (impact materiality).
  8. BRSR — mandatory for top 1,000 listed companies by market capitalisation.
  9. BRSR Core and value chain — reasonable assurance of core KPIs; value-chain ESG disclosure for large companies.
  10. Integrated reporting vs greenwashing — one honest story combining financial and ESG data.

Detail in 1.10, 1.11, 4.3.

S6 (slide 37). Distinguish BRSR, GRI, SASB and TCFD.
FeatureBRSRGRISASBTCFD
ScopeIndiaGlobalGlobalGlobal
UsersListed Indian companiesAll organisationsInvestors and companiesInvestors and companies
FocusESG and responsible business (9 NGRBC principles)Impacts on economy, environment, peopleFinancially material ESG issues by industry (77 standards)Climate-related financial risks (4 pillars)
Mandatory?Yes, for specified listed companiesMostly voluntaryVoluntary; now part of ISSB architectureOften voluntary; embedded in regulation in several jurisdictions and in IFRS S2
S7 (slide 41). What are ISS and CDP? Explain the CDP rating scale.

ISS (Institutional Shareholder Services): global provider of ESG ratings, governance research, proxy advisory and responsible-investment solutions. CDP (formerly Carbon Disclosure Project): global disclosure system that collects and scores company data on climate change, water security and forests. Scale: A and A− (Leadership), B and B− (Management), C and C− (Awareness), D and D− (Disclosure); F = failure to disclose when requested. Indian companies such as Infosys and several large manufacturers respond to CDP. See 1.14.

From Unit 2 slides

S8 (slides 2–3). Define climate change and analyse the Tiruppur textile example.

Definition (course): long-term changes in Earth's temperature, rainfall, wind patterns and other climatic conditions, mainly caused by increased GHG emissions from human activities. Tiruppur analysis: the knitwear cluster adopts (1) solar energy → lower Scope 2 emissions and power bills; (2) water recycling (zero liquid discharge after courts acted on river pollution) → less freshwater dependence and compliance; (3) energy-efficient machinery → lower cost per piece. Financial effects: lower operating cost, protection from water-scarcity disruption, access to global buyers who demand ESG compliance, and eligibility for green loans. Lesson (slide): sustainability improves both environmental and business performance.

S9 (slide 4). How does climate change affect corporate financial performance?

Higher operating costs (energy, water, cooling); supply-chain disruption; lower productivity (heat stress); higher compliance and insurance costs; asset impairment and stranded assets; revenue shifts as customers prefer low-carbon products; higher cost of capital for laggards; opportunities in efficiency and new markets. Map to income statement, balance sheet and cash flow (see 2.3).

S10 (slides 7, 11, 13, 15). State the Paris objectives and outcomes of COP 26, 27 and 28.

Paris (2015): well below 2°C, pursue 1.5°C; NDCs; renewable energy and efficiency; resilience; climate finance, technology transfer, capacity building. COP26 Glasgow (2021): keep 1.5°C within reach; stronger targets; "phase down" of unabated coal; methane cuts; more finance; India's net zero by 2070 (Panchamrit). COP27 Sharm el-Sheikh (2022): climate justice and adaptation; Loss and Damage Fund agreed; food and water security. COP28 Dubai (2023): first Global Stocktake; transition away from fossil fuels in energy systems; triple renewable capacity and double the rate of energy-efficiency improvement by 2030; private-sector role. See 2.4–2.5.

S11 (slides 17–29). List the Government of India's regulations and schemes to mitigate climate risk.

NAPCC 2008 with eight missions (Solar; Enhanced Energy Efficiency; Sustainable Habitat; Water; Sustaining the Himalayan Ecosystem; Green India; Sustainable Agriculture; Strategic Knowledge for Climate Change); Environment (Protection) Act 1986; National Green Hydrogen Mission; Carbon Credit Trading Scheme; PAT scheme with ESCerts; National Solar Mission; FAME India; SEBI BRSR; National Green Tribunal Act 2010. Each with its example as in 2.6. PAT numerical: Extra numerical N6.

S12 (slides 30–33). Explain scenario analysis, stress testing and credit rating with the slide examples.

Scenario analysis — plausible futures (high carbon tax, severe drought) to test strategy resilience; long horizon. Stress testing — severe shocks to measure losses and capital adequacy (banks testing loan books against climate disasters), e.g. EL rising from ₹45 crore to ₹96.25 crore in N8. Credit rating — creditworthiness including climate/ESG risk; strong ESG may earn a better outlook. Together they identify risk, build resilience, meet regulation and support sustainable decisions (see 2.8).

S13 (slide 35). Explain the impact of climate change and ESG on managerial decisions and corporations.

Climate change impact: guides strategic and financial decisions (e.g. investing in renewable technology), encourages sustainable investment and resilience, helps manage environmental risk. ESG impact: responsible decisions and risk management, investor trust, transparency and accountability (ESG reporting). Impact on corporations: reputation and efficiency, lower risk, sustainable growth, competitive advantage. Chain on slide: climate awareness + ESG integration → better decisions → sustainable growth. Add decision areas: capital budgeting with internal carbon price, sourcing, product design, financing mix, executive pay linked to ESG (see 2.9).

From Unit 3 slides

S14 (slides 2–4). Explain the eight sustainable finance strategies listed in the course.
  1. ESG integration
  2. Responsible investing (ethical, avoids harmful industries)
  3. Impact investing (measurable benefit + return)
  4. Green finance (renewables, efficiency, pollution control, green infrastructure)
  5. Climate finance (mitigation, adaptation, low-carbon transition)
  6. Sustainable lending (loans linked to ESG performance)
  7. Net-zero investment strategy (portfolio aligned to net zero)
  8. Sustainable portfolio management (diversified, ESG-monitored)

Explained with Indian examples in 3.1, alongside the GSIA's seven industry strategies.

S15 (slides 6–7). Explain the product table: green, social and sustainability-linked bonds with examples.

Green bonds finance green projects (solar, wind, green buildings) — World Bank green bonds (from 2008), SBI green bonds. Social bonds finance social projects (hospitals, schools, affordable housing) — African Development Bank social bonds. SLBs link the interest rate to ESG targets — Enel (2019, first), Suzano (2020).

Slide 7 lists "wind farm, solar park, electric grid" as SLB examples. Under ICMA's SLB Principles, SLB proceeds are for general purposes — they are not tied to projects. Enel's SLB was linked to a company-level target (share of renewable capacity), which is why renewable assets appear on the slide. In the exam, write: "SLB: coupon linked to issuer-level KPI (e.g. Enel's renewable-capacity share); proceeds not ring-fenced." See 3.5.

Past papers & CIA questions — solved

Your files contain one past question paper with an official scheme of evaluation: the Mid-Semester Examination, August 2026 (BBA304F-5, 50 marks, 2 hours). Every question in it is solved below, including both options of every either/or pair, and each answer is aligned with the official scheme — its points, its marks split and its evaluator notes. The scheme prints the full wording of Questions 1–3; for Questions 4–6 it prints only headings and marking points, so those questions are stated below in a paraphrase reconstructed from the scheme. CIA 1 and CIA 3 are group assignments, not question papers; what they reveal about emphasis is noted at the end.

The August 2026 mid-semester paper at a glance

SectionQuestionsChoiceMarksTopics tested
AQ1–Q3, each 5 marksEach question is an (a) OR (b) pair3 × 5 = 15SF definition + ESG; TBL; climate change drivers; UNFCCC + Kyoto; BRSR Core; Paris + COP26/27/28
BQ4–Q5, each 10 marksEach question is an (a) OR (b) pair2 × 10 = 20Paradigm shift vs rebranding; India vs a developed economy; green vs social bond; pillars in a lending strategy
CQ6 case study, three 5-mark partsCompulsory1 × 15 = 15Physical and transition risk; scenario analysis and stress testing for a credit rating; evaluating strategic options

Units 1 and 2 supplied almost the whole mid-sem, with one Unit 3 question (Q5a). For the end-semester paper expect Units 3, 4 and 5 to carry more weight (the course plan maps the ESE to all five course outcomes, with CO4 – evaluating products and instruments – carrying the most marks).

What the examiner rewards (distilled from the official scheme)

  • Definitions carry real marks. 1–2 marks out of 5 went to a precise definition (sustainable finance, TBL, climate change, BRSR Core). Open every answer with one.
  • Tie every example to a finance decision. ESG examples earned their mark only when framed as an investing or lending decision ("a bank assessing a thermal plant's carbon footprint before approving a loan"), not as a generic company fact.
  • Specific beats generic. TBL actions had to be "clear, actionable and specific" (fair-wage audits, rooftop solar with water recycling), not "the company should care for people".
  • One mark per point. 3-mark blocks were marked "one point per mark": three distinct, headed points.
  • Take a stance. In the evaluative 10-mark question, a purely descriptive answer was capped at 7–8 out of 10; full marks needed a clear position.
  • Name the difference. Comparisons needed "specific, named points, not generic 'they are stricter'" — scope, disclosure vs classification, assurance, enforcement.
  • Distinct points only. Two challenges "must be genuinely distinct"; two lending mechanisms must be two different mechanisms.
  • Mechanism, not definition. "The institution should manage risk" scored nothing; "stress-test the corporate loan book against a 2°C transition scenario" scored.
  • Stay on finance. A COP development counted only if "clearly tied to climate finance rather than general climate policy".
  • Case: evaluate, then recommend with a trade-off. Listing options earned little; weighing each option and recommending one (or a combination) with an explicit trade-off earned the marks. Use the case's details (e.g. "significant employer").
  • Alternatives are accepted. The scheme accepts any valid driver, any credible developed economy and any COP development — so pick the one you know best and explain it well.

Mid-Semester Examination, August 2026 — full solutions

Section A (3 × 5 = 15 marks; answer (a) or (b) of each)

Q1 (a). Define Sustainable Finance and explain the meaning of ESG components (Environmental, Social, Governance), with one example each. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: definition 2 marks; E, S and G each 1 mark, explained with reference to investing or financing decisions and illustrated with one example.

Definition (2 marks). Sustainable finance is the integration of environmental, social and governance (ESG) considerations into financial decision-making — investing, lending and financing — so as to generate long-term value while addressing environmental and social challenges. It marks a shift from purely profit-driven finance towards finance that also accounts for its impact on people, the planet and long-term economic resilience.

Environmental (1 mark). Factors covering a company's impact on natural resources and the climate (emissions, energy, water, waste, biodiversity). Investors and lenders use them to judge exposure to environmental risk, such as carbon costs or stranded assets. Example: a bank assesses a thermal power project's carbon footprint and future carbon-cost exposure before approving a loan.

Social (1 mark). Factors covering how a company manages relationships with employees, customers, suppliers and communities (labour practices, health and safety, data privacy, community impact). In financing decisions they signal reputational and labour-related risk. Example: an investor screens out a garment manufacturer with poor labour practices in its supply chain.

Governance (1 mark). Factors covering board structure, executive accountability, shareholder rights and transparency. Investors use them to judge management quality and the risk of fraud or expropriation. Example: a mutual fund avoids a company with weak board independence or a history of related-party transactions.

Conclusion. ESG turns sustainability into inputs a credit officer or fund manager can use; in India, SEBI's BRSR supplies the underlying data for the top 1,000 listed companies.

Q1 (b) [OR]. A mid-sized Indian manufacturing company wants to embed the Triple Bottom Line (TBL) approach into its operations. Suggest one concrete action it could take under each of the three TBL dimensions: People, Planet, Profit. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: what TBL is, in relation to sustainable finance, 1 mark; one clear, actionable and specific action per dimension, 4 marks (about 1.3 each).

What TBL is (1 mark). The Triple Bottom Line (John Elkington, 1994) evaluates business performance on three dimensions — People, Planet and Profit — rather than on financial profit alone, so that social and environmental outcomes are treated as being as important as economic returns. For sustainable finance it matters because lenders and investors increasingly price all three (BRSR disclosures, green loans, ESG funds).

People — action. Carry out fair-wage audits and obtain worker-safety certification (e.g. ISO 45001 occupational health and safety) across the factory floor, together with a skill-upgrade training programme for workers. Measurable through BRSR Principle 3 indicators (wages, safety incidents, training hours).

Planet — action. Install rooftop solar panels and a water-recycling system at the plant, cutting Scope 2 emissions, energy bills and freshwater withdrawal. The capex can be funded with a green loan from a bank that offers concessional pricing for eligible renewable-energy projects.

Profit — action. Redesign the product line to use recycled or lower-carbon raw materials, opening access to green-conscious buyers and export markets that pay a sustainability premium (and reducing future exposure to carbon border charges such as the EU's CBAM).

Conclusion. Each action is specific, measurable and pays back — which is what turns TBL from a slogan into strategy.

Q2 (a). Define Climate Change and explain the meaning of any two of its major drivers with examples. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: definition 1 mark; two drivers, 2 marks each (1 for explaining the driver, 1 for a relevant example). Valid alternatives: industrial processes, agriculture/livestock methane, land-use change.

Definition (1 mark). Climate change refers to long-term shifts in global temperatures and weather patterns, primarily driven since the industrial era by human activities that increase the concentration of greenhouse gases (GHGs) in the atmosphere.

Driver 1 — Burning of fossil fuels (2 marks). Combustion of coal, oil and natural gas for electricity, industry and transport releases large volumes of carbon dioxide, the single largest contributor to global warming. Example: coal-based thermal power plants remain a major source of India's carbon emissions.

Driver 2 — Deforestation (2 marks). Large-scale clearing of forests reduces the earth's capacity to absorb CO2, since trees act as natural carbon sinks; burning or decay of the cleared biomass also releases stored carbon. Example: converting forest land for agriculture, mining or urban expansion directly increases net atmospheric carbon.

Alternative driver (if preferred) — Agriculture and livestock. Enteric fermentation in cattle and flooded paddy cultivation release methane, a GHG far more potent than CO2 over short horizons; India's large livestock population and rice area make this significant.

Conclusion. For finance, these drivers define which sectors carry transition risk (power, steel, cement, agriculture) — see 2.2.

Q2 (b) [OR]. Discuss the roles played by the UNFCCC and the Kyoto Protocol in building a global framework for sustainable resource use, and assess their relevance to safeguarding resources for future generations. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: UNFCCC's role 2 marks; Kyoto's role 2 marks; assessment of relevance 1 mark.

UNFCCC (2 marks). Adopted in 1992 (at the Rio Earth Summit), the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change created the foundational global framework and the institutional process — the annual Conference of the Parties (COP) — through which countries negotiate climate action. Its objective is to stabilise GHG concentrations at a level that prevents dangerous interference with the climate system, and it introduced the principle of Common But Differentiated Responsibilities (CBDR): developed nations bear greater responsibility for historical emissions and should lead.

Kyoto Protocol (2 marks). Adopted in 1997 under the UNFCCC, it was the first legally binding agreement committing developed (Annex I) countries to specific emission-reduction targets. It introduced flexible, market-based mechanisms — emissions trading, Joint Implementation and the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM), which let developed countries fund emission-reduction projects in developing countries such as India in exchange for tradeable carbon credits (CERs).

Relevance for future generations (1 mark). Together they laid the institutional and market-based foundation of climate policy: binding targets, carbon-pricing logic and international cooperation. Their limits (no binding targets for developing countries; key emitters outside) were addressed by the Paris Agreement, which built on them. They remain foundational, if imperfect, building blocks for intergenerational resource sustainability — the Brundtland idea of meeting present needs without compromising those of future generations.

Q3 (a). Explain the significance of BRSR Core disclosure indicators for Indian listed companies. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: what BRSR Core is, 2 marks; significance, 3 marks, one point per mark.

What BRSR Core is (2 marks). BRSR Core is a subset of the Business Responsibility and Sustainability Report mandated by SEBI (2023) for the top 1,000 listed companies by market capitalisation. It is a defined set of key performance indicators across nine ESG attributes — including greenhouse gas emissions (Scope 1 and 2), water, energy, waste management, employee wellbeing and safety, gender diversity, inclusive development, fairness with customers and suppliers, and openness of business — designed to be quantifiable, comparable and subject to independent assurance (phased in from the top 150 companies).

Significance 1 — Comparability across companies. Because BRSR Core standardises specific numeric indicators rather than open-ended narrative, investors and analysts can compare ESG performance across companies in the same sector — something the earlier, more qualitative BRSR made difficult.

Significance 2 — Credibility through assurance. The indicators are subject to reasonable or limited assurance by an independent assurance provider (in 2025 SEBI also allowed an independent "assessment" as an alternative), giving the data more credibility than self-reported claims and reducing the risk of greenwashing.

Significance 3 — Input into capital allocation. Standardised, assured ESG data feeds directly into ESG ratings, ESG fund screening (SEBI requires ESG schemes to invest mainly in companies with assured BRSR Core disclosures) and lending decisions such as green-loan pricing — so BRSR Core is a practical input into capital allocation, not just compliance.

See 1.13 for BRSR structure.

Q3 (b) [OR]. Explain the key objectives of the Paris Agreement (2015), and briefly outline one major climate finance development from COP26, COP27 or COP28 that advanced those objectives. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: three objectives, one mark each; one COP development tied to climate finance, 2 marks.

Objective 1 — Temperature goal. Hold the increase in global average temperature to well below 2°C above pre-industrial levels, while pursuing efforts to limit it to 1.5°C.

Objective 2 — Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). Unlike Kyoto's binding targets for developed countries only, Paris requires all countries, developed and developing, to submit their own emission-reduction plans (NDCs), updated and strengthened every five years and reviewed through a global stocktake.

Objective 3 — Finance flows. Make finance flows consistent with low-emission, climate-resilient development; developed countries committed to mobilising climate finance for developing countries' mitigation and adaptation, building on the earlier USD 100 billion a year goal.

COP development (2 marks) — COP27 Loss and Damage Fund. At COP27 (Sharm el-Sheikh, 2022) countries agreed to establish a Loss and Damage Fund, the first dedicated financing mechanism to compensate climate-vulnerable developing countries for damage already occurring. It formally recognised that some impacts can no longer be prevented, only compensated for — a structural change in climate-finance architecture. At COP28 (Dubai, 2023) the fund was operationalised with the first funding pledges.

Other accepted answers: COP26's Glasgow Climate Pact push to double adaptation finance, or COP28's operationalisation of the fund with pledges. See 2.5.

Section B (2 × 10 = 20 marks; answer (a) or (b) of each)

Q4 (a). "Sustainable finance is a genuine paradigm shift in the financial system, not merely a rebranding of CSR and ethical investing." Critically evaluate, with reference to the financial system's role in financing the SDGs. (10) [wording reconstructed from the scheme]

Aligned with the official scheme: distinguishing CSR/ethical investing from sustainable finance 2 marks; case for a paradigm shift 3 marks; case for rebranding 3 marks; SDG-financing role plus a clear final position 2 marks. A descriptive answer with no stance is capped at 7–8.

1. The distinction (2 marks). CSR and traditional ethical investing are voluntary and peripheral: philanthropy-adjacent spending (e.g. CSR under Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013) or exclusion of "sin" stocks, sitting outside core financial decisions. Sustainable finance is systemic and risk-integrated: ESG factors enter credit appraisal, asset pricing, portfolio construction and supervision, so they change where capital is allocated and at what price.

2. The case for a genuine paradigm shift (3 marks).

  • Mandatory disclosure: ESG reporting has moved from voluntary to mandatory — SEBI's BRSR for the top 1,000 listed companies and assured BRSR Core; the EU's SFDR and CSRD.
  • Regulators and central banks are involved: RBI's draft climate-risk disclosure and scenario-analysis guidance, green deposit framework, NGFS membership; SEBI's green debt and ESG fund rules. Prudential supervisors do not regulate a marketing fad.
  • Integration into risk models: banks and rating agencies build climate and ESG risk into credit assessment, stress tests and ratings.
  • Scale of capital: labelled green, social, sustainability and sustainability-linked bonds run into trillions of US dollars globally; India has issued sovereign green bonds since 2023.

3. The case for "rebranding" (3 marks).

  • Greenwashing: labels without substance (funds with fossil-heavy holdings, vague net-zero pledges).
  • Continued short-termism: quarterly-earnings pressure still dominates; ESG is dropped when it costs returns.
  • ESG scores vs real impact: ratings measure risk to the company more than impact on the world, and diverge across providers.
  • Easing of rules: SEBI's 2025 relaxation (assessment as an alternative to assurance; deferral of value-chain disclosure) and the EU's 2025 Omnibus simplification suggest commitments are shallow when compliance costs bite.
  • Motives: firms adopt ESG for litigation defence, social image and reputation building rather than conviction.

4. SDG financing and my position (2 marks). The SDGs need investment on a scale that public budgets alone cannot supply; the financial system's role is to channel private capital towards them by pricing risk correctly and creating instruments (green bonds, SLBs, blended finance). Position: sustainable finance is a genuine paradigm shift in architecture — disclosure, supervision and risk models have permanently changed — but it remains incomplete in outcomes, because greenwashing and regulatory easing show that behaviour lags the architecture. The shift is real and irreversible in direction; its depth now depends on assurance, taxonomies and enforcement.

Q4 (b) [OR]. Compare India's approach to ESG regulation and disclosure with that of a developed economy of your choice. What does the comparison suggest about India's path? (10) [wording reconstructed from the scheme]

Aligned with the official scheme: India's approach 2 marks; the developed economy's approach 2 marks; two to three specific, named differences 4 marks; what it suggests about India's path 2 marks. Any credible developed economy is accepted; the EU is used here. Full comparison: 1.17.

1. India (2 marks). SEBI-led and disclosure-based: BRSR mandatory for the top 1,000 listed companies by market capitalisation from FY 2022-23; BRSR Core KPIs with third-party assurance phased in from the top 150; value-chain disclosure for the largest companies. The approach is phased and proportionate, and in 2025 SEBI eased it (independent assessment allowed as an alternative to assurance; value-chain requirements deferred). A national climate finance taxonomy was released in draft in 2025.

2. European Union (2 marks). A classification plus disclosure system: the EU Taxonomy (2020) defines which activities are environmentally sustainable (substantial contribution to one of six objectives, do no significant harm, minimum safeguards); the SFDR (from 2021) makes fund managers classify products as Article 6, 8 or 9; the CSRD requires large companies, listed or not, to report under the ESRS on a double-materiality basis with mandatory limited assurance. In 2025 the Omnibus package delayed and proposed narrowing parts of this.

3. Differences (4 marks).

BasisIndiaEU
Scope of applicabilityListed companies only (top 1,000 by market cap)Large companies whether listed or not, plus non-EU groups with large EU turnover
System designDisclosure-based; taxonomy still in draftClassification-based: Taxonomy defines "green", SFDR classifies funds
AssuranceReasonable assurance of BRSR Core KPIs, phased by size (with an assessment alternative from 2025)Limited assurance of the whole sustainability statement under CSRD
Enforcement maturityYoung regime, building assurance and ratings capacity (SEBI ESG Rating Provider rules, 2023)Detailed product-level rules and supervisory enforcement across member states

4. What it suggests about India's path (2 marks). India's lighter, phased approach reflects regulatory-capacity limits and a developing economy's need to balance growth with compliance cost. It is best read as a deliberate sequencing strategy — build data (BRSR), then credibility (Core + assurance), then classification (taxonomy) — rather than a weakness. The EU's own 2025 simplification shows that over-ambitious scope carries costs, which supports India's proportionate path.

Q5 (a). An Indian company plans to raise funds for a large solar power project. Explain why a Green Bond is more appropriate than a Social Bond, and discuss two challenges it may face in raising a green bond in India. (10) [wording reconstructed from the scheme]

Aligned with the official scheme: why green over social 4 marks; challenge 1, 3 marks; challenge 2, 3 marks; the two challenges must be genuinely distinct.

1. Why a green bond (4 marks).

  • Green bonds ring-fence proceeds for environmental projects. Under ICMA's Green Bond Principles (use of proceeds, project evaluation and selection, management of proceeds, reporting), renewable energy is the first listed eligible category; SEBI's green debt securities framework also lists renewable and sustainable energy.
  • Social bonds fund social outcomes for target populations: affordable housing, education, healthcare, employment, food security — under ICMA's Social Bond Principles.
  • A solar project is categorically environmental, not social: its primary benefit is avoided GHG emissions (measured in tCO2e avoided per year), which is exactly the impact green-bond investors want reported.
  • Therefore a green bond matches the project to the right investor pool (dedicated green mandates) and the right reporting metrics. A sustainability bond would be needed only if the project also had a defined social component.

2. Challenge 1 — market depth and pricing (3 marks). The domestic corporate green bond market is thin: few dedicated green investors, limited liquidity and a small investor base for rupee-denominated green paper. The "greenium" (lower yield) is small or absent in India, so the issuer bears the extra costs without a clear pricing benefit; issuers seeking longer tenors and larger size often go overseas, taking on currency risk and tenor mismatch against rupee revenues.

3. Challenge 2 — verification, reporting and taxonomy (3 marks). SEBI requires an independent external reviewer, tracking of proceeds, and allocation and impact reporting for the life of the bond; these add cost and management effort, heavier for a mid-sized issuer. Until India's climate finance taxonomy is final, investors face uncertainty about what counts as green, raising due-diligence costs and the risk of greenwashing allegations.

Conclusion. A green bond is the correct label; the company should reduce the challenges by obtaining an external review early, using a standard framework and considering a rupee issue sized to attract domestic institutions. See 3.3.

Q5 (b) [OR]. Explain the key pillars of sustainable finance, and show how a bank could operationalise any two pillars in its lending strategy. (10) [wording reconstructed from the scheme]

Aligned with the official scheme: key pillars 4 marks; operationalising pillar 1 in lending 3 marks; pillar 2, 3 marks. Each must be an actual lending-process mechanism, not a restated definition.

1. Key pillars (4 marks). The scheme's framework (any consistent framework taught in class is accepted; see 1.16):

  1. Transparency and disclosure — reliable, comparable ESG data (BRSR, BRSR Core, TCFD-style reporting).
  2. Risk management — identifying and pricing climate and ESG risk in credit and investment.
  3. Capital mobilisation — directing funds to sustainable activities (green bonds, green loans, SLBs).
  4. Stakeholder value creation — value for society and environment as well as shareholders.
  5. Policy alignment — consistency with national goals and regulation (Paris/NDCs, RBI and SEBI rules).

2. Operationalising transparency and disclosure (3 marks). Embed ESG disclosure checks in loan underwriting: for listed corporate borrowers, the credit memo must include key BRSR Core indicators (Scope 1 and 2 emissions, energy and water intensity) with a sector benchmark; borrowers without assured data receive a lower internal ESG score, which feeds into the internal rating and the loan covenants (annual ESG reporting as a condition precedent to disbursement).

3. Operationalising risk management (3 marks). Stress-test the corporate loan book against climate scenarios: map exposures to high-emitting sectors (power, steel, cement), apply an orderly 2°C transition scenario and a disorderly scenario with a sharp carbon price, recompute borrowers' debt-service coverage and probability of default, and set sector limits and risk-based pricing accordingly. Add green-linked pricing: a margin discount on sustainability-linked loans when the borrower meets pre-agreed KPIs.

Conclusion. Pillars matter only when written into credit policy, pricing and limits; RBI's draft disclosure and scenario-analysis guidance pushes Indian banks in exactly this direction.

Section C (compulsory case, 15 marks)

Case (reconstructed from the scheme). A large Indian integrated steel producer relies on coal-based blast furnaces, sends about one-third of its output to the EU and East Asia, and is a significant employer in its region. Lenders and a rating agency are reassessing its credit rating in the light of climate risk. Management is weighing four options: transition to green steel, invest in carbon capture, phase out old coal-based capacity, and raise green bond financing for the transition.

Q6 (a). Identify the physical and transition climate risks the company faces. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: physical risks 2 marks; transition risks 3 marks.

Physical risks (2 marks).

  • Acute: extreme weather (floods, cyclones, heatwaves) disrupting operations at plant locations, damaging equipment and interrupting production and logistics (ports, rail).
  • Chronic: long-term water stress — steelmaking is water-intensive (cooling, quenching), so declining water availability threatens production continuity and raises costs.

Transition risks (3 marks).

  • Policy/market — CBAM: the EU's Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism charges importers for embedded emissions (definitive phase from 2026), acting as a tariff on the share of output sold to the EU.
  • Stranded assets: coal-based blast furnaces may lose value or need early retirement as emission norms tighten.
  • Compliance costs: India's Carbon Credit Trading Scheme (CCTS) sets emission-intensity targets for steel; missing them means buying credits.
  • Reputation and financing: lenders and investors increasingly price climate exposure, raising the cost of capital or restricting access.
Q6 (b). Explain how scenario analysis and stress testing could be applied to reassess the company's credit rating, and identify factors that would influence a downgrade or upgrade. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: scenario analysis 2 marks; stress testing 2 marks; rating factors 1 mark.

Scenario analysis (2 marks). Run the company's projected financials against several plausible future pathways — e.g. NGFS-style orderly net-zero transition, delayed/disorderly transition and current policies (hot house) — and trace how each changes revenue (export access, demand for low-carbon steel), costs (carbon prices, CBAM, energy) and asset values (impairment of blast furnaces). The rating agency compares leverage and coverage ratios across scenarios.

Stress testing (2 marks). Apply one specific, severe but plausible shock — a sharp carbon-price increase or sudden loss of EU access under CBAM — and test whether cash flows still cover debt service.

Illustration with assumed numbers (not from the case; for practice). EBITDA ₹12,000 crore; interest ₹2,500 crore; principal due ₹3,000 crore; debt service = ₹5,500 crore.

  1. Base: DSCR = EBITDA ÷ debt service = 12,000 ÷ 5,500 = 2.18×; interest cover = 12,000 ÷ 2,500 = 4.80×.
  2. Stress 1 (CBAM): 2 million tonnes sold to the EU × 2.3 tCO2/t × ₹4,000/tCO2 = ₹1,840 crore. EBITDA = 12,000 − 1,840 = 10,160; DSCR = 10,160 ÷ 5,500 = 1.85×; interest cover 4.06×.
  3. Stress 2 (combined): CBAM ₹1,840 crore + domestic carbon cost (50 Mt × 20% above target × ₹1,000/t = ₹1,000 crore) + lost margin on 1 Mt of EU sales at ₹8,000/t (₹800 crore) = ₹3,640 crore, a 30.33% fall in EBITDA. EBITDA = 8,360; DSCR = 8,360 ÷ 5,500 = 1.52×; interest cover 3.34×.
  4. Against an assumed lender covenant of 1.50× (EBITDA of at least 1.5 × 5,500 = ₹8,250 crore), headroom under Stress 2 is only ₹110 crore — a signal of downgrade pressure unless the transition plan is credible.

Rating factors (1 mark). Credibility and funding of the decarbonisation plan; pace of the coal-to-green-steel transition; diversification of export markets away from CBAM exposure; liquidity and financial buffers to absorb transition costs.

Q6 (c). Evaluate the strategic options and recommend a course of action. (5)

Aligned with the official scheme: genuine evaluation of the options 3 marks; a recommendation with justified trade-offs 2 marks.

OptionStrengthWeakness
Green steel transition (hydrogen-based DRI, electric arc furnaces, scrap)Tackles the core transition risk directly; preserves EU access under CBAMHigh capex and long timeline; green hydrogen still costly
Carbon capture (CCUS) on existing furnacesKeeps existing assets productive; avoids early write-offsTechnology and cost maturity uncertain; storage and transport infrastructure lacking in India
Phased decommissioning of coal-based capacityCuts emissions and stranded-asset exposureJob losses and regional economic damage — the company is a significant employer, so a just-transition plan is essential
Green bond financingFunds transition capex from dedicated green investorsProceeds must be ring-fenced for eligible projects, with credible allocation and impact reporting; risk of greenwashing claims if the company remains coal-heavy

Recommendation (2 marks). A phased green steel transition: convert capacity in stages (starting with the lines that serve EU exports), retire the oldest blast furnaces in step with reskilling and redeployment of workers, and finance it with a sustainability-linked bond or loan tied to an emission-intensity KPI (tCO2 per tonne of steel), supplemented by green bonds for specific eligible projects. Trade-off accepted: higher near-term financing and capex costs in exchange for lower long-term transition risk, continued EU market access and a more stable credit rating. CCUS is kept as a pilot, not the core strategy, because of its technology risk.

CIA 1 and CIA 3: what they tell you about emphasis

Neither is a question paper, so there is nothing to "solve", but both show what your examiner values:

  • CIA 1 (ESG Intelligence Profile of a BRSR-compliant top-1,000 company): ESG risk mapping scored on BRSR data for two cycles, peer benchmarking, SDG alignment, disclosure-gap and greenwashing analysis ("one ESG promise versus actual outcome") — so expect exam questions that ask you to judge a company's ESG claims, not describe them (see 4.5 and the Unit 5 company cases).
  • CIA 3 (Sustainable Finance War Room): advising a government or institution using country initiatives (India's sovereign green bonds and SEBI ESG disclosure, the EU Green Deal and Taxonomy, the US Inflation Reduction Act, China's green credit policy, Japan's GX strategy, and others) with at least two finance tools and a trade-off analysis — the same skills tested in mid-sem Q4(b) and Q6. The initiatives are summarised in 1.17.

Question bank

Try each question before opening the answer. MCQs explain why every wrong option is wrong — that is where the learning is.

Multiple-choice questions (30)

1. The term "ESG" was popularised by which 2004 report? (a) Brundtland Report (b) Who Cares Wins (c) Stern Review (d) Silent Spring

Answer: (b)

(a) Brundtland (1987) defined sustainable development, not ESG. (c) The Stern Review (2006) was on the economics of climate change. (d) Silent Spring (1962) was about pesticides.

2. Emissions from a company's own diesel generators are: (a) Scope 1 (b) Scope 2 (c) Scope 3 (d) Avoided emissions

Answer: (a)

(b) Scope 2 is purchased electricity/steam/heat/cooling. (c) Scope 3 is other value-chain emissions. (d) Avoided emissions are reductions compared with a baseline, not a scope.

3. A bank's financed emissions fall under: (a) Scope 1 (b) Scope 2 (c) Scope 3 category 15 (investments) (d) Outside the GHG Protocol

Answer: (c)

(a) and (b) cover the bank's own operations and purchased energy. (d) Wrong: the GHG Protocol's Scope 3 Standard includes investments as category 15; PCAF gives the method.

4. Which is NOT one of the four core components of ICMA's Green Bond Principles? (a) Use of proceeds (b) Process for project evaluation and selection (c) Coupon step-up (d) Reporting

Answer: (c)

(a), (b), (d) are core components along with management of proceeds. A coupon step-up is a feature of sustainability-linked bonds, not green bonds.

5. In a sustainability-linked bond, the proceeds are: (a) Ring-fenced for green projects (b) Used for general corporate purposes (c) Only for social projects (d) Held in escrow until SPTs are met

Answer: (b)

(a) describes green bonds. (c) describes social bonds. (d) There is no escrow; the financial consequence is usually a coupon step-up.

6. The Paris Agreement's long-term temperature goal is: (a) Below 3°C (b) Well below 2°C, pursuing 1.5°C (c) Exactly 1°C (d) Zero warming by 2030

Answer: (b)

(a) is far weaker than agreed. (c) and (d) are not in the Agreement; about 1.1°C of warming has already occurred.

7. The Loss and Damage Fund was agreed at: (a) COP26 Glasgow (b) COP27 Sharm el-Sheikh (c) COP21 Paris (d) COP28 Dubai

Answer: (b)

(a) COP26 focused on 1.5°C, coal phase-down and India's 2070 net zero. (c) COP21 adopted the Paris Agreement. (d) COP28 operationalised the fund and held the first Global Stocktake, but the fund was agreed at COP27.

8. India's net-zero target year, announced at COP26, is: (a) 2050 (b) 2060 (c) 2070 (d) 2047

Answer: (c)

(a) is the target of many developed economies. (b) is China's (carbon neutrality before 2060). (d) is the "Viksit Bharat" development horizon, not the net-zero year.

9. Under the PAT scheme, one ESCert equals: (a) 1 tonne of CO2 (b) 1 tonne of oil equivalent (c) 1 MWh (d) ₹1,000

Answer: (b)

(a) is the unit of a carbon credit. (c) is the unit of a renewable energy certificate. (d) ESCert prices are market-determined, not fixed.

10. SEBI's BRSR is mandatory for: (a) All companies in India (b) Top 1,000 listed companies by market capitalisation (c) Only public sector banks (d) Only companies with foreign listing

Answer: (b)

(a) Unlisted companies are not covered. (c) and (d) are invented restrictions.

11. Which framework uses "double materiality"? (a) ISSB IFRS S1 (b) SASB (c) EU CSRD/ESRS (d) TCFD

Answer: (c)

(a), (b), (d) take an investor (financial materiality) perspective.

12. The TCFD's four pillars are: (a) Environment, Social, Governance, Economy (b) Governance, Strategy, Risk management, Metrics and targets (c) Use of proceeds, Selection, Management, Reporting (d) Plan, Do, Check, Act

Answer: (b)

(a) mixes ESG and TBL. (c) are the Green Bond Principles. (d) is a quality-management cycle.

13. WACI measures: (a) Total emissions of a portfolio (b) Portfolio-weighted carbon intensity per unit of revenue (c) Weighted average cost of capital (d) Water consumption

Answer: (b)

(a) is financed emissions. (c) WACC is a finance concept, not a carbon metric. (d) Unrelated.

14. A bond's coupon is 8% and its YTM is 9%. It trades: (a) At par (b) At a premium (c) At a discount (d) Cannot be determined

Answer: (c)

(a) Par needs coupon = YTM. (b) Premium needs coupon > YTM. (d) The relation is sufficient to decide.

15. The Macaulay duration of a 4-year zero-coupon bond is: (a) 2 years (b) Less than 4 years (c) 4 years (d) More than 4 years

Answer: (c)

A zero has one cash flow at maturity, so its weighted-average time equals maturity. (a), (b), (d) are wrong for that reason; coupon bonds have duration below maturity.

16. "Greenium" refers to: (a) Extra yield investors demand on green bonds (b) Lower yield on a green bond than an otherwise similar conventional bond (c) Fee paid to a second-party opinion provider (d) Coupon step-up on an SLB

Answer: (b)

(a) is the opposite. (c) is an issuance cost. (d) is an SLB feature.

17. Which of these is an example of greenwashing? (a) Publishing a verified allocation report (b) Labelling a fund "ESG" while holding companies that fail its own stated screens (c) Issuing a transition bond with a published transition plan (d) Obtaining a second-party opinion

Answer: (b)

(a), (c), (d) are credibility-enhancing practices.

18. India's first sovereign green bonds were issued in: (a) 2015 (b) 2019 (c) January–February 2023 (d) 2025

Answer: (c)

₹16,000 crore in two tranches in FY2022-23. (a) and (b) predate the framework (November 2022). (d) Later issues exist, but the first was 2023.

19. The CDM was created under: (a) Paris Agreement (b) Kyoto Protocol (c) Montreal Protocol (d) Energy Conservation Act

Answer: (b)

(a) Paris created Article 6 mechanisms (successor). (c) Montreal deals with ozone-depleting substances. (d) is Indian law (PAT, CCTS).

20. In PCAF, the attribution factor for a listed company is: (a) Outstanding amount ÷ revenue (b) Outstanding amount ÷ EVIC (c) Emissions ÷ revenue (d) Market cap ÷ debt

Answer: (b)

(a) mixes a stock with a flow. (c) is emission intensity. (d) is a leverage-type ratio.

21. Which is a performance-based (not use-of-proceeds) instrument? (a) Green bond (b) Social bond (c) Sustainability-linked loan (d) Blue bond

Answer: (c)

(a), (b), (d) are all use-of-proceeds instruments (green, social, ocean/water projects).

22. Under Section 135 of the Companies Act, 2013, CSR spending is: (a) 5% of turnover (b) 2% of average net profit of the three preceding years (c) 2% of current-year revenue (d) Voluntary for all companies

Answer: (b)

(a) and (c) use the wrong base and rate. (d) It is mandatory for companies meeting the net worth, turnover or net profit thresholds.

23. A smart contract's "oracle" is: (a) The issuer's CEO (b) A data feed bringing off-chain information onto the blockchain (c) The consensus algorithm (d) A type of token

Answer: (b)

(a) is a person, not a data source. (c) validates blocks. (d) Tokens represent assets.

24. Which strategy removes tobacco and weapons companies from a portfolio? (a) Best-in-class (b) Exclusionary (negative) screening (c) Thematic investing (d) Active ownership

Answer: (b)

(a) picks leaders within each sector, including controversial sectors. (c) targets themes such as clean energy. (d) engages with companies rather than removing them.

25. RBI's framework for Green Deposits (2023) requires: (a) Green deposits to be invested only in government bonds (b) Allocation to eligible green activities with annual third-party verification (c) A minimum 10% interest rate (d) No disclosure

Answer: (b)

(a) Funds go to green activities/projects. (c) No interest-rate floor is prescribed. (d) Disclosure in financial statements is required.

26. ITC is excluded by many global ESG funds mainly because of: (a) Poor water management (b) Tobacco products (c) Lack of BRSR reporting (d) Coal mining

Answer: (b)

(a) ITC reports being water positive. (c) It reports under BRSR. (d) It is not a coal miner.

27. Which UN SDG is "Affordable and Clean Energy"? (a) SDG 6 (b) SDG 7 (c) SDG 9 (d) SDG 13

Answer: (b)

(a) Clean water and sanitation. (c) Industry, innovation and infrastructure (the SDG named in your syllabus). (d) Climate action.

28. If yields rise by 1% on a bond with modified duration 4, the price falls by approximately: (a) 1% (b) 4% (c) 0.4% (d) 40%

Answer: (b)

%ΔP ≈ −MD × Δy = −4 × 1%. (a) ignores duration. (c) and (d) misplace the decimal.

29. The first sustainability-linked (SDG-linked) bond was issued by: (a) Enel, 2019 (b) World Bank, 2008 (c) EIB, 2007 (d) UltraTech, 2021

Answer: (a)

(b) World Bank's 2008 issue was a green bond. (c) EIB's 2007 Climate Awareness Bond was the first green (labelled) bond. (d) UltraTech's was India's first SLB, not the world's.

30. BRSR Core requires: (a) Limited assurance of all BRSR items (b) Reasonable assurance of a defined set of core KPIs for the largest listed companies (c) No assurance (d) Assurance only by SEBI

Answer: (b)

(a) The requirement is reasonable assurance of core KPIs, not all items. (c) Assurance is the point of BRSR Core. (d) Assurance is by independent assurance providers, not the regulator.

Short-answer questions (2–3 marks) (18)

1. Define sustainable finance.

Financial activities, investments and banking decisions that consider ESG factors alongside profit, aiming to support long-term economic growth while protecting the environment and society (course definition); e.g. green bonds for solar power.

2. What is the Triple Bottom Line?

John Elkington's (1994) idea that firms should report on People, Planet and Profit, not profit alone — social, environmental and economic performance.

3. Differentiate Scope 2 location-based and market-based methods.

Location-based uses the average grid emission factor; market-based reflects contracts and certificates (e.g. renewable PPAs, RECs) and uses residual factors for uncovered electricity.

4. What is an SPT?

A Sustainability Performance Target: a measurable, time-bound target on a KPI (e.g. 2.20 tCO2/tcs by 2027) whose achievement determines an SLB's coupon.

5. What is a second-party opinion?

An independent pre-issuance assessment (by firms such as specialist ESG reviewers) of whether a green/social/SLB framework aligns with ICMA principles and is credible.

6. What is CBDR?

Common but Differentiated Responsibilities — UNFCCC principle that all countries share responsibility for climate action but developed countries bear greater responsibility given historical emissions and capacity.

7. What are NDCs?

Nationally Determined Contributions: each country's self-set climate plan under the Paris Agreement, updated every five years with increasing ambition.

8. What is carbon trading?

Buying and selling of emission allowances or credits (1 credit = 1 tCO2e) so reductions happen where cheapest; e.g. India's CCTS, EU ETS.

9. Define impact investing.

Investments made with the intention to generate positive, measurable social and environmental impact alongside a financial return (GIIN).

10. What is green microfinance?

Microfinance that integrates environmental goals: small loans for clean energy (solar lamps, clean cookstoves), climate-resilient farming and green enterprises, plus environmental risk screening of micro-borrowers.

11. What is PCAF?

Partnership for Carbon Accounting Financials: global standard for measuring and disclosing financed emissions of loans and investments, using attribution factors and data-quality scores 1–5.

12. What is a transition bond?

A bond financing a carbon-intensive issuer's move to lower emissions (e.g. efficiency upgrades at a steel plant), usually with a published transition strategy (ICMA Climate Transition Finance Handbook).

13. What is stress testing?

Assessing losses under severe but plausible shocks (e.g. carbon price jump, flood) to test resilience and capital adequacy; e.g. EL = PD × LGD × EAD under stressed PD and LGD.

14. What is a tokenized bond?

A bond whose ownership units are represented as digital tokens on a distributed ledger, enabling fractional ownership, automated servicing and near-instant settlement.

15. What is modified duration?

Macaulay duration ÷ (1 + y); the approximate % price change for a 1% change in yield. MD 3.94 → about 3.94% price fall if yields rise 1%.

16. What is blended finance?

Strategic use of concessional (public/philanthropic) capital to mobilise private investment in sustainable development projects, e.g. first-loss guarantees.

17. What is greenhushing?

Deliberately under-reporting sustainability goals or progress to avoid scrutiny or accusations of greenwashing.

18. What are the PRI's six principles in one line?

Incorporate ESG in investment analysis; be active owners; seek ESG disclosure; promote the principles; collaborate; report on activities and progress.

Long-answer and case questions with full model answers (10)

Write these as shown: introduction → headed points → diagram/table → Indian example → conclusion.

L1. Trace the evolution of sustainable finance globally and in India. (10)

Introduction. Sustainable finance has moved from avoiding "sin" stocks to integrating ESG risks and now to financing the net-zero transition and measuring impact.

Global evolution.

  1. Roots: 1713 Carlowitz's sustainable forestry; faith-based exclusions; 1962 Silent Spring.
  2. Environmental awakening: 1972 Stockholm Conference; 1987 Brundtland definition.
  3. Global governance: 1992 Rio Earth Summit (UNFCCC, CBD); 1997 Kyoto Protocol (CDM, emissions trading).
  4. ESG era: 2003 Equator Principles for project finance; 2004 Who Cares Wins (ESG); 2006 PRI; 2007 EIB Climate Awareness Bond; 2008 World Bank green bond.
  5. Standardisation: 2014 Green Bond Principles; 2015 SDGs and Paris; 2017 TCFD; 2019–2020 SLBs and SLB Principles.
  6. Mainstreaming and regulation: 2020 onwards ESG boom; EU Taxonomy and SFDR; 2023 ISSB standards; anti-greenwashing enforcement.

India. 2011 National Voluntary Guidelines → 2012 SEBI BRR for top 100 → 2013 Companies Act Section 135 (mandatory CSR) → 2015 first green bond (Yes Bank) and India's NDC → 2017 SEBI green bond disclosure norms → 2021 BRSR (mandatory from FY2022-23 for top 1,000) → 2021 RBI joins NGFS → 2022 sovereign green bond framework; Energy Conservation (Amendment) Act enabling carbon market → 2023 sovereign green bonds, CCTS, RBI green deposits, BRSR Core, SEBI ESG fund and green debt rules → 2024 RBI draft climate disclosure framework.

Diagram: timeline with three eras (exclusion → integration → impact/alignment).

Drivers: climate science, investor demand, regulation, disclosure standards, SDG financing gap.

Conclusion. India has moved from voluntary guidelines to one of the most prescriptive ESG disclosure regimes, and sustainable finance is now part of mainstream capital allocation.

L2. Explain ESG factors and the fundamentals of ESG risk management. (10)

Introduction. ESG factors are environmental, social and governance issues that affect, and are affected by, a company's operations; managing them is now part of enterprise risk management.

E: climate (emissions, transition and physical risk), resource use (water, energy), pollution and waste, biodiversity. S: labour practices, health and safety, human rights, communities, product responsibility, data privacy. G: board independence and diversity, executive pay, shareholder rights, ethics and anti-corruption, transparency.

ESG risk management cycle (draw a loop): (1) Identify material issues (materiality assessment, SASB map, stakeholder input); (2) Assess likelihood and impact, quantify (heat map, carbon cost, stress tests); (3) Mitigate (policies, capex, supplier codes, insurance, targets); (4) Monitor and report (KPIs, BRSR, board oversight).

Integration: board-level ESG committee, risk appetite statements, ESG in credit and investment decisions, links to executive pay.

Indian example: a Tiruppur textile unit identifies water scarcity, assesses cost of tanker water, invests in zero liquid discharge and solar, and reports water intensity; banks use ESMS to screen loans.

Benefits: lower cost of capital, resilience, reputation, regulatory readiness. Challenges: data gaps, rating divergence, short-termism.

Conclusion. ESG risk management turns "non-financial" issues into managed financial risks.

L3. Discuss the role of the financial system in promoting sustainable development. (10)

Introduction. The financial system allocates capital, prices risk and monitors borrowers; each function can steer the economy towards or away from sustainability.

  1. Capital mobilisation and allocation — green bonds, green deposits, ESG funds channel savings to renewables, clean transport, affordable housing (SDG financing).
  2. Risk pricing — climate and ESG risk in credit ratings, loan pricing, insurance premiums; higher cost for polluters.
  3. Monitoring and governance — covenants, stewardship, voting, engagement.
  4. Financial inclusion — microfinance, PM Jan Dhan accounts, SHG-bank linkage.
  5. Innovation — SLBs, blended finance, carbon markets, digital bonds.
  6. Risk transfer — crop insurance (PMFBY), catastrophe and parametric insurance.
  7. Market signals and disclosure — ESG indices, BRSR-based analysis.

Actors: banks (RBI green deposits, climate risk), capital markets (SEBI green debt, ESG funds), insurers (IRDAI), development finance (NABARD, SIDBI, IREDA), multilateral (World Bank, ADB), central banks (NGFS).

Indian example: sovereign green bonds (₹16,000 crore, 2023) financing solar and metro projects; IREDA's renewable lending.

Challenges: short-termism, greenwashing, data gaps, financing gap for adaptation.

Conclusion. A financial system that prices sustainability risks and rewards sustainable activity is essential to meet SDGs and India's 2070 net zero.

L4. Compare green bonds, social bonds, sustainability bonds and sustainability-linked bonds. (10)

Introduction. ICMA's principles define two families: use-of-proceeds bonds (green, social, sustainability) and performance-based bonds (SLBs).

BasisGreenSocialSustainabilitySLB
PrinciplesGBP (2014)SBP (2017)Sustainability Bond GuidelinesSLBP (2020)
ProceedsGreen projectsSocial projects for target populationsMix of green and socialGeneral purposes
Key testEligible project categoriesEligible categories + target populationBothKPI materiality, SPT ambition
CouponFixedFixedFixedStep-up/down
ReportingAllocation + impactAllocation + impactAllocation + impactKPI performance; verification mandatory
ExampleIndia SGrB 2023; SBIAfDB social bondsMany sovereigns/MDBsEnel 2019; UltraTech, JSW Steel 2021

Numerical illustration: ₹500 crore at 7.50%: green bond pays ₹37.50 crore a year always; SLB with 25 bps step-up after year 3 if missed pays ₹38.75 crore in years 4–5.

Suitability: green for issuers with a green-asset pipeline; social for health/housing/inclusion lenders; sustainability for mixed pipelines; SLB for transition issuers without ring-fenceable assets.

Conclusion. The instruments complement each other; credibility rests on frameworks, external review and reporting.

L5. Explain the Government of India's regulations to mitigate climate risk. (10)

Introduction. India combines legal, market-based and mission-mode measures, alongside financial-sector regulation, to meet its NDC (45% emission-intensity reduction from 2005 levels and about 50% non-fossil power capacity by 2030) and net zero by 2070.

  1. NAPCC (2008) — eight missions (Solar, Enhanced Energy Efficiency, Sustainable Habitat, Water, Himalayan Ecosystem, Green India, Sustainable Agriculture, Strategic Knowledge).
  2. Environment (Protection) Act, 1986 — umbrella law; emission standards; environmental clearances.
  3. NGT Act, 2010 — fast environmental justice; compensation orders.
  4. Energy Conservation Act, 2001 (amended 2022) — basis for PAT and the carbon market.
  5. PAT — ESCerts for energy-intensive industries (1 ESCert = 1 toe).
  6. CCTS (2023) — compliance carbon market with intensity targets; offset mechanism.
  7. National Green Hydrogen Mission — decarbonising steel, fertilisers, refining.
  8. National Solar Mission, FAME India for EVs.
  9. SEBI BRSR/BRSR Core, green debt and ESG fund rules; RBI green deposits, climate risk disclosures, NGFS membership.

Numerical example (PAT): target SEC 0.160 toe/t, achieved 0.152, production 5,00,000 t → (0.160 − 0.152) × 5,00,000 = 4,000 ESCerts earned.

Evaluation: strengths — mix of mandates and markets; weaknesses — enforcement, coal dependence, adaptation finance gap.

Conclusion. The framework is shifting from command-and-control to market and disclosure-based tools.

L6. Explain carbon accounting and compute a footprint. (10)

Introduction. Carbon accounting measures GHG emissions under the GHG Protocol so that they can be reported (BRSR, CDP, ISSB S2), targeted and financed (SLB KPIs).

Scopes: Scope 1 direct; Scope 2 purchased energy; Scope 3 value chain (15 categories, including purchased goods, business travel, use of sold products, investments). Gases converted to CO2e using GWP.

Formula: Emissions = activity × emission factor.

Computation (revenue ₹250 crore): diesel 40,000 L × 2.68 kg/L = 107.2 t (Scope 1); grid electricity 1,200 MWh × 0.72 t/MWh = 864 t (Scope 2); air travel 1,50,000 passenger-km × 0.15 kg = 22.5 t (Scope 3). Total 993.7 tCO2e; intensity 3.97 tCO2e per ₹ crore.

Interpretation: 87% from electricity → rooftop solar or renewable PPA is the main lever; market-based Scope 2 would fall accordingly.

Challenges: Scope 3 data, emission-factor choice, boundary setting, double counting.

Conclusion. Accurate carbon accounting is the foundation of climate targets and green finance.

L7. Discuss the role of AI and ML in sustainable finance. (10)

Introduction. ESG data are large, unstructured and patchy; AI and ML turn them into decision-useful information.

  1. Data extraction (NLP) from reports, BRSRs, news.
  2. ESG scoring and prediction — estimate missing emissions or scores (regression, random forests).
  3. Controversy and sentiment monitoring in real time.
  4. Greenwashing detection — compare claims with data.
  5. Satellite/geospatial analytics — deforestation, methane, flood exposure.
  6. Climate risk modelling — scenario engines, credit PD with climate variables.
  7. Portfolio construction — optimisation with carbon constraints.
  8. Green lending — alternative-data credit scoring for small green borrowers.

Tools: Orange3 text mining, Python/R models, Power BI dashboards.

Limitations: bias, black-box models, data quality, privacy, energy use of models.

Conclusion. AI makes ESG analysis faster and wider but must be governed and explainable.

L8. Case: Surya Textiles seeks a sustainability-linked loan. (15)

Case. A Tiruppur exporter wants ₹100 crore for modernisation. The bank offers an SLL at base margin 250 bps over its benchmark, reduced by 10 bps if water intensity falls 20% in 3 years, increased by 10 bps if not. Its European buyers demand ESG compliance. (a) Explain SLL and suitable KPIs (5). (b) Compute annual interest saving or penalty (4). (c) Advise management (6).

(a) SLL: a loan whose margin varies with the borrower's performance on pre-agreed sustainability KPIs (LMA/APLMA/LSTA SLL Principles: KPI selection, SPT calibration, loan characteristics, reporting, verification). Proceeds need not be green. KPIs for a textile unit: water intensity (litres per kg of fabric), share of renewable electricity, ZLD compliance, chemical management, worker safety (LTIFR).

(b) 10 bps on ₹100 crore = 100 × 0.0010 = ₹10 lakh a year saved if the SPT is met, or ₹10 lakh a year extra if missed — a swing of ₹20 lakh a year between outcomes.

(c) Accept the SLL: the direct saving is modest, but the KPIs match buyer requirements; water recycling cuts tanker-water cost and regulatory risk; renewable power cuts electricity cost. Set a realistic but ambitious baseline; install metering for verification; report annually; combine with a green loan or rooftop solar for capex; disclose in BRSR if listed (or to buyers). Risks: failure to meet SPT (penalty, reputational damage), measurement cost.

Conclusion. The SLL aligns finance with the firm's commercial need to satisfy global buyers and manage water risk.

L9. Case: Sagar Cement's green bond and allocation report. (15)

Case. Sagar Cement issued a ₹1,000 crore green bond (net proceeds ₹990 crore). Allocated: 100 MW solar plant ₹450 crore (part of a ₹600 crore project; ₹300 crore of this refinances a plant commissioned last year), waste-heat recovery ₹250 crore, green building ₹150 crore. Solar CUF 20%, grid EF 0.71 tCO2/MWh. (a) Allocation %, unallocated, refinancing share (5). (b) Avoided emissions and attributed impact (5). (c) Is there a greenwashing risk? (5)

(a) Allocated = 450 + 250 + 150 = ₹850 crore; allocation % = 850 ÷ 990 = 85.86%; unallocated = ₹140 crore; refinancing share = 300 ÷ 850 = 35.29%.

(b) Solar output = 100 × 0.20 × 8,760 = 1,75,200 MWh; avoided = 1,75,200 × 0.71 = 1,24,392 tCO2 a year; attributed to bond = 1,24,392 × 450/600 = 93,294 tCO2 a year.

(c) Risks: (1) 35% refinancing reduces additionality — acceptable under GBP if disclosed with a look-back period; (2) a cement company's main emissions are process and kiln emissions; green bond projects do not address them — investors may call it "selective" unless there is a company-wide transition plan; (3) unallocated ₹140 crore must be held per stated policy (not in fossil assets). Safeguards: publish framework and SPO; annual allocation and impact reports with third-party verification; disclose refinancing share; consider an SLB tied to clinker factor or CO2/t cement for company-wide credibility. Regulatory: SEBI's green debt framework (2023) requires disclosures and prohibits misleading green claims.

Conclusion. The bond is legitimate but credibility requires transparency on refinancing and a transition plan.

L10. Case: Vedanta and the ESG committee of an insurer. (15)

Case. An insurer's ESG committee must decide whether to continue holding Vedanta Ltd bonds. (a) Identify ESG risks (5). (b) Link them to financial risk (5). (c) Recommend (5).

(a) E — pollution allegations, tailings, coal-based captive power; S — Niyamgiri and Dongria Kondh rights, Thoothukudi 2018 deaths and closure; G — group complexity, parent debt, failed 2020 delisting; exclusion by Norway's GPFG since 2007.

(b) Operational risk (plant closure stranded the copper smelter); credit risk (group leverage, refinancing); market risk (exclusion by global investors narrows the investor base, raising yields); legal and reputational risk; transition risk (carbon cost on aluminium smelting under CCTS and EU CBAM).

(c) Options: divest; hold and engage; hold with limits. Recommendation: place on a watch list with exposure limits; engage on FPIC, independent environmental audits, tailings standards, board independence and a funded net-zero plan; set a time-bound exit trigger. Report the decision in the stewardship report.

Conclusion. ESG controversies have already turned into financial losses for Vedanta; a disciplined engagement-with-exit approach protects policyholders.

Extra numericals with full solutions (14)

All emission factors and prices are given in the question and are illustrative; in the exam, always use the values provided.

N1. Carbon footprint. A company uses 40,000 litres of diesel (EF 2.68 kgCO2/L), 1,200 MWh of grid electricity (0.72 tCO2/MWh) and its staff fly 1,50,000 passenger-km (0.15 kgCO2/pkm). Revenue ₹250 crore. Find emissions by scope, total and intensity.

  1. Scope 1 = 40,000 × 2.68 = 1,07,200 kg = 107.2 t.
  2. Scope 2 = 1,200 × 0.72 = 864 t.
  3. Scope 3 = 1,50,000 × 0.15 = 22,500 kg = 22.5 t.
  4. Total = 107.2 + 864 + 22.5 = 993.7 tCO2e; intensity = 993.7 ÷ 250 = 3.97 tCO2e per ₹ crore.

N2. Scope classification. Classify: (i) LPG in the canteen; (ii) purchased steam; (iii) emissions of a logistics firm transporting finished goods (paid by the company); (iv) company-owned delivery vans; (v) employees' commuting; (vi) customers using the company's air-conditioners; (vii) refrigerant leakage from office ACs; (viii) a bank's loan to a coal power plant.

  1. (i) Scope 1 (combustion in owned equipment). (ii) Scope 2. (iii) Scope 3 — downstream transportation (or upstream if the company pays for it as a purchased service; either way Scope 3). (iv) Scope 1. (v) Scope 3 (employee commuting). (vi) Scope 3 (use of sold products). (vii) Scope 1 (fugitive emissions). (viii) Scope 3 category 15 (investments / financed emissions).

N3. ESG weighting (with reverse). Pillar scores E 55, S 72, G 80. (a) Composite with weights 50/30/20. (b) With equal weights. (c) With 50/30/20, what E score gives a composite of 70?

  1. (a) 0.5 × 55 + 0.3 × 72 + 0.2 × 80 = 27.5 + 21.6 + 16.0 = 65.1.
  2. (b) (55 + 72 + 80) ÷ 3 = 207 ÷ 3 = 69.0.
  3. (c) 70 = 0.5E + 21.6 + 16.0 → 0.5E = 32.4 → E = 64.8.
  4. Lesson: weights alone change the score by 3.9 points — one cause of rating divergence.

N4. Green bond proceeds allocation. See L9: net proceeds ₹990 crore; allocated ₹850 crore (₹300 crore refinancing).

  1. Allocation % = 850 ÷ 990 × 100 = 85.86%.
  2. Unallocated = 990 − 850 = ₹140 crore.
  3. Refinancing share = 300 ÷ 850 × 100 = 35.29%; new financing = 64.71%.

N5. Avoided emissions and attribution. 100 MW solar, CUF 20%, grid EF 0.71 tCO2/MWh; project cost ₹600 crore, green bond finances ₹450 crore.

  1. Generation = 100 × 0.20 × 8,760 = 1,75,200 MWh.
  2. Avoided = 1,75,200 × 0.71 = 1,24,392 tCO2/yr.
  3. Attributed = 1,24,392 × 450 ÷ 600 = 93,294 tCO2/yr.

N6. PAT ESCerts. Plant production (baseline) 5,00,000 t; target SEC 0.160 toe/t. (a) Achieved 0.152: ESCerts? (b) Achieved 0.165: position and cost at ₹1,500 per ESCert?

  1. (a) (0.160 − 0.152) × 5,00,000 = 0.008 × 5,00,000 = 4,000 ESCerts earned (sellable).
  2. (b) (0.160 − 0.165) × 5,00,000 = −0.005 × 5,00,000 = −2,500 → must buy 2,500 ESCerts; cost = 2,500 × 1,500 = ₹37,50,000 = ₹37.5 lakh (or face penalty).

N7. CCTS. Target intensity 0.90 tCO2e/t, achieved 0.86, output 20,00,000 t. Credits?

  1. (0.90 − 0.86) × 20,00,000 = 0.04 × 20,00,000 = 80,000 carbon credit certificates (1 CCC = 1 tCO2e).

N8. Climate stress test. Loan book ₹5,000 crore. Base PD 2%, LGD 45%. Stressed (disorderly transition) PD 3.5%, LGD 55%.

  1. Base EL = 0.02 × 0.45 × 5,000 = ₹45 crore.
  2. Stressed EL = 0.035 × 0.55 × 5,000 = ₹96.25 crore.
  3. Increase = ₹51.25 crore (2.14 times the base).

N9. SLB step-up (issuer cost, with PV). ₹750 crore, 6-year SLB, 8.00% annual coupon; SPT observed at end of year 3; step-up 30 bps for years 4–6 if missed. Missed. Find (a) coupon before and after, (b) total extra cost, (c) PV of the extra cost at 8%.

  1. (a) Before: 750 × 8.00% = ₹60 crore; after: 750 × 8.30% = ₹62.25 crore.
  2. (b) Extra = 750 × 0.0030 × 3 = ₹6.75 crore (₹2.25 crore a year).
  3. (c) PV = 2.25/1.084 + 2.25/1.085 + 2.25/1.086 = 1.654 + 1.531 + 1.418 = ₹4.60 crore.

N10. PCAF financed emissions and WACI. Data as in Unit 4 Case 5 (₹50/30/20 crore in A/B/C).

  1. AF: 0.05, 0.05, 0.025; financed emissions: 20,000 + 3,000 + 200 = 23,200 tCO2e.
  2. Footprint = 23,200 ÷ 100 = 232 tCO2e per ₹ crore.
  3. WACI = 0.5 × 200 + 0.3 × 66.67 + 0.2 × 5 = 100 + 20 + 1 = 121.

N11. Carbon cost on EBITDA. Emissions 20 lakh t; free allowance 15 lakh t; carbon price ₹2,000/t.

  1. Chargeable = 20,00,000 − 15,00,000 = 5,00,000 t.
  2. Cost = 5,00,000 × 2,000 = ₹1,00,00,00,000 = ₹100 crore a year (reduces EBITDA).

N12. Greenium. ₹2,000 crore green bond yields 7.32%; comparable conventional bond 7.40%.

  1. Greenium = 7.40% − 7.32% = 8 bps.
  2. Annual saving = 2,000 × 0.0008 = ₹1.6 crore.

N13. Bond price and YTM (reverse). ₹1,000, 8%, 5 years. (a) Price at 7%. (b) YTM if price is ₹960.

  1. (a) =-PV(7%,5,80,1000) = ₹1,041.00 (premium).
  2. (b) Approximate: (80 + 8) ÷ 980 = 8.98%; exact =RATE(5,80,-960,1000) = 9.03%.

N14. Duration-based loss. Portfolio ₹59,66,678.28, modified duration 3.2658, convexity 14.6304. Estimate the change for a 2% rise in yields.

  1. Duration term = −3.2658 × 0.02 = −6.5316%.
  2. Convexity term = ½ × 14.6304 × 0.022 = +0.2926%.
  3. Estimate = −6.2390% × 59,66,678.28 = −₹3,72,259 (full revaluation: −6.2495%, −₹3,72,887).

Differences-between master table

Every "distinguish between" pair the paper can ask. In the exam, add a one-line definition of each term above the table and an example below it.

1CSR vs ESGCompany's voluntary/mandated social responsibility activities (Sec. 135 spending; inside-out; philanthropic)Framework investors use to assess risks and performance on E, S, G (outside-in and inside-out; integrated in core business)Audience (society vs investors); spending vs measurement
2Sustainable finance vs green finance vs climate financeAll E, S, G considerations in financeGreen: environmental projects / Climate: mitigation and adaptation onlyBreadth: SF ⊃ green ⊃ climate (approximately)
3Shareholder vs stakeholder theoryFriedman: maximise shareholder profit within lawFreeman: create value for all stakeholdersWhose interest the firm serves
4Kyoto Protocol vs Paris AgreementTop-down binding targets for developed countriesBottom-up NDCs for all countriesWho commits and how

6Physical vs transition riskAcute (floods, cyclones) and chronic (heat, sea level)Policy, technology, market, reputation, legal changes towards low carbonSource: climate itself vs response to it
7Scope 1 vs 2 vs 3Direct owned/controlled sourcesPurchased energy / other value-chainOwnership and control of source
8Scenario analysis vs stress testingRange of plausible futures; strategic, long horizonSevere but plausible shock; loss and capital adequacyPurpose and severity

11Green bond vs green loanTradable security; many investors; listedBilateral/syndicated bank loan; GLP (LMA)Instrument form and market

13Social bond vs sustainability bondOnly social projectsMix of green and social projectsProject mix
14Impact investing vs ESG investingIntentional, measurable positive impactESG risks and opportunities integrated to improve risk-adjusted returnIntentionality and measurement of impact
15Microfinance vs green microfinanceSmall loans for the poor (inclusion)Microfinance with environmental objectives and screeningEnvironmental dimension
16Carbon tax vs emissions tradingPrice fixed, quantity uncertainQuantity capped, price discoveredWhich is fixed: price or quantity
17Compliance vs voluntary carbon marketMandated by law (EU ETS, India CCTS compliance)Voluntary buyers offsetting (Verra, Gold Standard)Legal obligation

23Financed emissions vs WACIAbsolute tCO2e attributed to investorPortfolio-weighted intensity per revenueAbsolute vs intensity; ownership-based vs weight-based
24Greenwashing vs greenhushingOverstating green credentialsUnderstating to avoid scrutinyDirection of distortion
25Exclusionary screening vs best-in-classRemove whole sectors/activitiesPick leaders within every sectorSector-level vs within-sector choice
